# Matrix Sediment Standards Options Paper
## Working Draft for Collaborative Research and Framework Development
### Sediment Standards Project, Phase 2 (2026-2027)

**Purpose:** This document is intended to serve as the working draft of the final Matrix Options Paper deliverable identified in the Phase 2 project plan. It will be iteratively refined over the course of Phase 2 as research tasks are completed, reviewed, synthesized, and appended.

---

> **Successor identity.** This is the source-authoritative internal remediation candidate `1.0.11-remediated-20260913`, assembled from the live `_sources` directory on 2026-09-13. The prior V14 candidate is preserved as a frozen predecessor. This candidate is review-ready only; it is not an independent GREEN, publication, distribution, scientific adoption, or frontend-release decision.

> **DOCX navigation note.** The DOCX artifact has no generated field-code table of contents. Its manual contents list is retained; the PDF contains a generated TOC.

## Acknowledgements

The Science Advisory Board for Contaminated Sites in British Columbia (SABCS) and the BC Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV) jointly acknowledge the professionals, academics, and volunteer contributors whose time and expertise will support the Sediment Standards Project.

> **The contributor lists below are not yet populated, and this is deliberate.** No Technical Working Group or SSTAC review cycle has taken place in Phase 2. Under the Phase 2 project plan, focused TWG review of this early draft is scheduled at Tasks 2.2 to 2.4 (weeks 3 to 5), and the full TWG and SSTAC review of the final Matrix Options Paper is scheduled at Task 9.2 (month 11). Nobody has yet reviewed or contributed to this draft. The lists will be populated once Phase 2 participation has actually occurred, following the grouping used in the Phase 1 report, and only with the confirmation of each person named. Names from the Phase 1 report have deliberately **not** been carried forward, because Phase 1 participation is not Phase 2 participation.

**Funding and Leadership.** Phase 1 of the Sediment Standards Project was solely funded by the SABCS. Phase 2 (2026-2027) is a project of the Site Remediation Program within ENV, carried out in partnership with the SABCS. The modernization effort is led by the Science and Standards Technical Advisory Committee (SSTAC), established by the SABCS in partnership with ENV, and relies on a volunteer-based Technical Working Group (TWG). Funding and resourcing arrangements for Phase 2 are set out in the Phase 2 project plan and are not restated here.

<div id="sec-scope-note" class="section-anchor"></div>

## A Note on the Scope and Status of this Paper

*The paragraph immediately below adapts, for Phase 2, the scope caveat carried in the Phase 1 White Paper (Preliminary Framework for Modernizing British Columbia's Sediment Standards, v3.0, 2025).*

This document is a summary of a scientific research endeavour supporting a proposed approach for updating sediment standards. All frameworks, pathways, categories and standards described herein are proposals for consideration. This paper and its findings are intended to constitute recommendations to the BC Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV). ENV, as the sole legal decision-maker, will follow its own distinct policy decision framework, including further consultation and executive approvals, before amending the CSR.

For this early draft in particular:

* **Status.** This is an early working draft prepared under Task 2 ("Develop Early DRAFT Options Paper") of the Phase 2 project plan. The final Matrix Options Paper is scheduled to be drafted between April and June 2027 and finalized at month 12 of Phase 2. The Phase 2 project plan is an internal ENV and SABCS planning and coordination document, used to align internal resources and timelines. It is not a regulatory instrument, it confers no authority, and nothing in this paper draws legal or regulatory authority from it. Three things follow, and they govern how the rest of this paper should be read. **First**, where the plan directs something - as it does the three-part structure of Schedule 3.4 described in Section 4.0 - that direction is a given for this paper and not a choice this paper makes. **Second**, the plan being a planning document means its own status, draft or otherwise, changes nothing about the authority of anything in here: the paper does not become more authoritative if the plan is approved, or less if it is not. **Third**, ENV decides. Nothing the plan sets and nothing this paper recommends is a decision.
* **No review, no endorsement.** This draft has not been reviewed or endorsed by the Technical Working Group, by the SSTAC, by the SABCS Board, or by ENV. No recommendation in it has been adopted by anyone.
* **No legal effect.** This paper creates, amends, interprets and announces nothing under the *Environmental Management Act* or the *Contaminated Sites Regulation*. It does not represent the position of the Province of British Columbia.
* **Numerical standards are not produced in Phase 2.** Candidate equations and parameters are structural options only. Unsupported or unreconciled values are withdrawn or quarantined as Phase 3 working material; no value in this paper is a standard or may be applied to a site.
* **Out of scope for Phase 2.** Final numeric standards; regulatory amendments; supporting technical guidance or protocols; and a complete sediment database.
* **Indigenous engagement is not within the scope of Phase 2.** This project develops the scientific framework only. Consultation with Indigenous Nations forms part of ENV's own policy, consultation and approval processes for any future regulatory change. No Nation, Indigenous organization, or Knowledge Holder has been consulted on, has reviewed, or has endorsed any content in this paper. Where Indigenous consumption rates and high-use dietary scenarios appear in this paper, they appear only as published exposure parameters taken from documents prepared for or with specific Indigenous Nations - principally the Tsleil-Waututh Nation Burrard Inlet water quality objectives work and the Lower Athabasca criteria report prepared for the Athabasca Chipewyan First Nation - and from the general technical literature, and not as the product of engagement.
* **Drafting method.** This draft was prepared with AI-assisted drafting and research tools. All content requires expert verification before it is relied on or cited.

<div id="sec-audience" class="section-anchor"></div>

## Audience and Submission Path

This paper is an early working draft, prepared for the Task 2.2 two-week review by the focused
Technical Working Group, with Task 2.3 bringing in the full TWG and the SSTAC behind it. **That
review has not yet begun, and no part of this draft has been sent to anyone.** It has not been
reviewed or endorsed by the TWG, by the SSTAC, by the SABCS Board, or by ENV, and no comment on it
has been received.

The plan describes the finished document and where it goes:

> "The final deliverable for Phase 2 is a Matrix Options Paper, a scientifically defensible
> recommendation package presented to ENV for consideration." (*plan section 1.1*)

and, as the first of the five Phase 2 Success Criteria:

> "Delivery of the Matrix Options Paper: Finalization and submission of the Matrix Options Paper to
> ENV executive leadership. This deliverable must provide scientifically defensible
> recommendations, articulate the multi-part Schedule 3.4 expansion concept, and provide a clear,
> policy-ready outline for future regulatory integration." (*plan section 8*; emphasis added)

The path from this draft to that submission has five steps, each with its plan task and month:

| Step | What happens | Task | When |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1 | This draft is circulated for a two-week review focused on Sections 4 and 7; comments compiled and the paper revised | 2.2 - 2.5 | Weeks 3-5, then month 2 |
| 2 | Research workstreams run and report; outputs are aligned and gaps resolved at the integration workshop | 4 - 8 | Months 4-9 |
| 3 | The final Matrix Options Paper is drafted | 9.1 | Months 10-11 (April - May 2027) |
| 4 | Full TWG and SSTAC review; internal revisions | 9.2 | Month 11 (May 2027) |
| 5 | The paper is finalized and the deliverables package submitted to ENV executive leadership | 9.3 | Month 12 (June 2027) |

What happens after step 5 is not part of Phase 2, and this paper does not anticipate it. ENV is
responsible for both regulating the implementation of the contaminated sites legal framework and for
developing and maintaining these standards, and "will subsequently follow its internal policy,
consultation, and approval processes before recommending any regulatory changes to the CSR"
(*plan section 1.1*). Consultation, including consultation with Indigenous Nations, forms part of that ENV
process.

Accordingly: this paper recommends, it does not decide. It does not create, amend, interpret or
announce any standard, protocol or requirement under the *Environmental Management Act* or the
*Contaminated Sites Regulation*, and it does not represent the position of the Province of British
Columbia. Final numerical sediment standards, regulatory amendments, supporting technical guidance or
protocols, and a complete sediment database are all outside the scope of Phase 2 (*plan section 2.2*).

<div id="sec-reviewers-guide" class="section-anchor"></div>

## Reviewer's Guide - Two-Week Review, Sections 4 and 7

### Why you have this

The Phase 2 project plan's first immediate next step is to "Circulate draft Options Paper for 2-week
review (with focus on Sections 4 and 7)" (*plan section 7*). This draft is prepared for that circulation,
which has not yet taken place. It is Task 2.2, with the focused TWG core sub-group, and Task 2.3
brings the full TWG in behind it.

This is an **early working draft**. Nothing in it has been reviewed, endorsed, adopted or decided by
anyone. You are not being asked to approve it. You are being asked to tell us where it is wrong,
where it is thin, and what we have missed.

### If you have one hour

Read Section 4.0 (about nine pages; its later subsections are a freshwater coverage audit that can be skimmed on a first pass) and then the one or two subsections of Section 7.0 closest to
your own expertise. Skip the appendices unless a Section 7 subsection sends you to one. Then answer
whichever of the questions below you have an opinion on. Partial answers are useful; a single
well-aimed comment on one parameter is worth more to us than a general impression of the whole
document.

### What Section 4 and Section 7 contain

* **Section 4.0 - Vision for Modernized Sediment Standards.** The three-part Schedule 3.4 structure
  (Part 1 matrix standards; Part 2 generic standards for human health; Part 3 generic standards for
  ecological health), and the four proposed **Sediment Use Categories**: Aquatic Wildlands (AW),
  Aquatic Recreational / Traditional Harvest (AR/TH), Commercial Aquatic (CA), and Industrial Aquatic
  (IA).
* **Section 7.0 - Phase 2 Research Topics.** Eight subsections, one per research topic:
  bioavailability adjustment (7.1), bioaccumulation (7.2), substance classification (7.3), substance
  prioritization (7.4), matrix standards derivation options (7.5), the generic standards adoption
  procedure (7.6), the BC Aquatic Database (7.7), and policy-ready input parameters (7.8).

### Status of the framework's elements - what is fixed, scheduled, and open

**Read this before commenting.** The paper's elements do not all have the same decision status,
and a comment aimed at a fixed element cannot be actioned in Phase 2.

| Element | Status | Basis |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Three-part Schedule 3.4** - Part 1 matrix, Part 2 generic human health, Part 3 generic ecological | **Plan-given. Not selectable.** | Project plan: *"Modernize Schedule 3.4 to include three parts"*; Task 7.1 |
| **The four receptor-pathways** - ecological direct, ecological food, human direct, human food | **Plan-given. Not selectable.** The four exist and are named. | Project plan, Task 6.1: *"four receptor-pathways (eco direct, eco food, HH direct, HH food)"* |
| **The structure of each pathway** | **Open - scheduled work.** | Task 6.2, *"Define structure for the four pathways"*, months 6-7 |
| **Which receptors sit within each pathway** | **Open - your judgement sought.** | Reviewer's Guide layer 2 |
| **The four Sediment Use Categories** (AW, AR/TH, CA, IA) - how many, what they are, where boundaries fall | **Open - a proposal of this draft, against a requirement of the plan.** The plan requires **"water lot use classes"** to be defined at Task 3.7 but does not specify them; these four are this draft's proposal for that requirement, and **Section 9.9 sets them out as one option among four**. | Reviewer's Guide layer 1; Sections 4.1 and 9.9; plan Task 3.7 |
| **How a site is assigned to a use category**, and what happens when it meets more than one | **Remains within the existing CSR framework; not re-opened as an option in this paper.** | Section 9 |
| **Waterbody classes** - two (freshwater, marine-and-estuarine) or three | **Open - a scoping decision with arithmetic consequences.** | Section 4.4.2 |
| **The applicability rule** - direct-contact pathways are mandatory; food pathways are conditional; all standards that apply apply | **Settled in principle for this paper.** | Sections 6.5 and 18.1 |
| **Exposure assumptions and receptor characteristics** | **Open - your judgement sought.** | Reviewer's Guide layer 3 |
| **Input parameter selection** | **Open - your judgement sought.** | Reviewer's Guide layer 4 |
| **Equation form** | **Open, but with few live alternatives.** | Reviewer's Guide, fifth layer |
| **Indigenous Uses as a distinct pathway** | **Open, and a divergence from Phase 1.** The Phase 1 framework treated Indigenous Uses as a distinct pathway; this draft places Indigenous receptors within the two human-health pathways. | Sections 5 and 10 |
| **Numerical standards themselves** | **Out of scope for Phase 2.** | Project plan section 2.2 |

### What is actually being decided - the option layers

The Phase 2 deliverable is a **framework and its options, not standards**. It is easy to read a paper
full of equations and assume the equations are the question. For the more straightforward pathways
they largely are not - there are few genuine alternative forms. The real choices sit in the layers below - four principal ones and a fifth -
and these are what we need your judgement on:

1. **Sediment uses** - how many use categories, what they are, and where their boundaries fall.
2. **Receptors within each pathway** - which receptors each pathway should protect, and how they are characterised. **Note that *which* pathways is not open**: the project plan names the four (ecological direct, ecological food, human direct, human food), and Section 9.1 treats them as the fixed structure of Part 1. What Task 6.2 leaves open, and what we need your judgement on, is the structure of each pathway and the receptors inside it.
3. **Exposure assumptions and receptor characteristics** - the values and behaviours assumed for each
   receptor in each scenario.
4. **Input parameter selection** - which source governs each parameter, and what value or range it
   takes.

Equation form is a fifth layer, and it matters, but it is the layer with the fewest live alternatives.

### The questions we want answered

**On sediment uses (Section 4.1)**

1. Do the four Sediment Use Categories describe the aquatic receiving environments you actually work
   in? What is missing, and what is over-split?
2. Is Industrial Aquatic the right fourth category, and is its scope right?
3. Could you assign a real site to one of these four categories without ambiguity? If not, what makes
   it ambiguous?

**On receptors and pathways (Sections 4.1 and 6.0)**

4. Four receptor-pathways across four use categories gives $4 \times 4 = 16$ exposure scenarios as the
   starting point. Is that the right starting grid?
5. Which of the sixteen do you expect to be implausible, or wholly dominated by another scenario, and
   could be dropped with a stated reason rather than carried?

**On exposure assumptions and receptor characteristics (Sections 7.5 and 7.8)**

6. In the scenarios closest to your expertise, which assumptions are defensible, which are not, and
   which are missing entirely?
7. Where does an assumption need a BC-specific value rather than one imported from another
   jurisdiction?

**On input parameter selection (Section 7.8)**

8. For any parameter in your area: which source should govern, and what is the defensible value or
   range? A citation is more useful to us than a number.
9. Where a parameter in this draft is unsourced or thinly sourced, say so - we would rather withdraw
   a number than defend one we cannot trace.

**On equation form (Sections 6.0 and 7.5)**

10. Where do you see a genuine alternative equation form, as opposed to an apparent choice that is
    really a parameter choice in disguise?

**On water type**

11. The framework is intended to apply to freshwater, estuarine and marine sediments. What changes
    across the three in your area, and where do the parameter sets not yet exist?

**On data (Sections 7.7 and 7.8)**

12. What sediment concentration data, reports, datasets or published parameter sets should we know
    about? This directly feeds Task 3.1 and Task 3.8, and it is the single most useful thing most
    reviewers can contribute.

### What we already know is incomplete

Please do not spend your hour reporting these - they are known.

* **No review cycle has happened yet.** This is the first. Nothing is endorsed.
* **Most of Sections 7 and 9 and Appendices A-H are drafted ahead of schedule.** The research tasks
  that are supposed to produce them run months 4 to 9 (October 2026 - March 2027). That content is
  unreviewed and unvalidated, not merely provisional.
* **The BC Aquatic Database does not exist.** It is Task 3, built at month 7. Appendix G describes a
  proposed design, not a system.
* **No derived numerical outputs are part of this Phase 2 paper.** Candidate equations and input parameters are documented as structural options. Future calculation work is separate and requires source verification, parameter provenance, and independent review before any value could be considered for a later phase.
* **The policy-ready outline for future regulatory integration is now drafted at Section 18.0**, ahead of schedule and un-reviewed. It is Task 7.5, month
  7 (January 2027).
* **The Director's Interim Standards material is now drafted at Section 18.4**, ahead of schedule and un-reviewed. It is Task 7.2, months 7-8, and
  the source material has to come from ENV internally.
* **There is no engagement summary yet.** Appendix I is an outline of the planned approach.
* **Future calculation work is outside this package.** It is not needed to answer any Phase 2 review question. Any future numerical testing is contingent on source verification, equation reconciliation, parameter provenance, and independent review.

* Sections and appendices are being actively revised, so page and section references may move.

### What is out of scope for Phase 2

Comments on these will be recorded but cannot be actioned in this phase. Four are the plan's own
out-of-scope list (*plan section 2.2*):

* **final numerical sediment standards** - derivation is Phase 3;
* regulatory amendments;
* supporting technical guidance or protocols;
* a complete sediment database;
* **Indigenous engagement** - not within the scope of Phase 2. This project develops the scientific
  framework only; consultation with Indigenous Nations forms part of ENV's own policy, consultation
  and approval processes for any future regulatory change. Indigenous consumption rates and high-use
  dietary scenarios appear in this draft only as published exposure parameters taken from documents prepared for or with specific Indigenous Nations (principally the Tsleil-Waututh Nation Burrard Inlet work and the Lower Athabasca report prepared for the Athabasca Chipewyan First Nation) and from the general technical literature; and
* **the decision itself.** This paper recommends to ENV. ENV decides.

### How to send comments

The comment window, closing date and return address will be confirmed when this draft is
circulated; the project plan provides for a two-week window.

Two practical requests. First, tell us which section and subsection each comment refers to - the
document is long and section numbers are the only reliable anchor. Second, where you are giving us a
value, give us the citation with it; a value we cannot trace has to be withdrawn regardless of how
right it is.

Thank you. The review you are being asked for is a technical review of the framework and its evidence boundaries.

<div id="sec-1-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 1.0 Executive Summary

> **Status of this draft.** This is a working draft of the Matrix Options Paper,
> prepared for Phase 2 of the Sediment Standards Project. It has had no technical
> review: no section has been checked by a reviewer other than its drafter, and
> neither the Science Advisory Board for Contaminated Sites nor the Ministry has
> reviewed or endorsed it. Phase 2 delivers the framework and the options within
> it, not standards - final numerical sediment standards are expressly outside its
> scope. **No number anywhere in this paper is a proposed standard.**

British Columbia's current CSR Schedule 3.4 Generic Numerical Sediment Standards are more than two decades behind the current state of sediment science. The existing standards primarily protect benthic invertebrates from direct exposure and do not systematically address food-web transfer, bioaccumulative substances, human health direct contact, human health dietary exposure, Indigenous food-based exposure pathways, modern bioavailability adjustment, or emerging contaminants.

The Phase 1 White Paper established the scientific rationale for modernizing BC's sediment standards and proposed a matrix-based framework that distinguishes between ecological and human health protection goals, and between direct sediment exposure and exposure through food-web transfer. That work identified the need to move beyond a single-pathway, benthic-focused standards structure toward a framework that is more scientifically defensible, adaptable, and protective of aquatic ecosystems and the communities that depend on them.

The Phase 2 project plan defines the work now underway. Phase 2 is focused on research, framework development, options analysis, and preparation of this Matrix Options Paper. It is not intended to calculate final numerical standards, propose regulatory amendments, complete the full sediment database, or develop final supporting technical guidance or protocols.

This Options Paper is therefore structured as a living technical synthesis document. It will be iteratively refined over the course of Phase 2 as research tasks are completed. The body of the paper summarizes the purpose, rationale, proposed framework architecture, technical options considered, emerging findings, and recommendations. Detailed technical products from the Phase 2 workstreams will be appended as supporting research reports, including reports on bioavailability adjustment, bioaccumulation, substance classification, substance prioritization, matrix standards derivation options, generic standards adoption, the BC Aquatic / Sediment Database, and policy-ready input parameters.

The proposed modernization direction is no longer framed as a question of whether to develop a matrix framework. That work is already underway and is the core of Phase 2. Instead, this paper evaluates the technical and policy-design options for how the matrix standards framework should be structured, supported, and documented.

The Phase 2 vision set out in the Phase 2 project plan is to develop a modernized Schedule 3.4 structure consisting of three parts:
1. Part 1: Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards to protect human and ecological health across four receptor-pathway combinations;
2. Part 2: Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to protect human health; and
3. Part 3: Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to protect ecological health.

Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards are intended for priority substances that warrant a high duty of care based on factors such as biomagnification potential, toxicity, persistence, and local occurrence. Generic Numerical Sediment Standards are intended for substances where it is not feasible or necessary to develop full matrix standards, and where scientifically defensible values can be adopted or adapted from other jurisdictions.

The direction this working draft carries is the three-part Schedule 3.4 structure the project plan sets, supported by a structured substance classification and prioritization process that decides which substances are routed to Part 1 and which to Parts 2 and 3. This approach preserves the matrix framework as the core modernization pathway while recognizing that not every substance can be addressed through full matrix derivation.

This draft carries no worked derived outputs. The framework findings retained here concern source conventions, parameter provenance, evidence gaps, and the need for independent review before any numerical derivation. Future calculation work is separate Phase 3 material and is not a dependency of this review package.

---

<div id="sec-2-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 2.0 Context and Problem Statement

Sediment is a dynamic environmental matrix that can act as both a sink and a source of contaminants. Contaminants may accumulate in sediments, become biologically available to benthic organisms, transfer into aquatic food webs, and contribute to long-term ecological and human health risks.

BC's current CSR Schedule 3.4 sediment standards were developed in the early 2000s using approaches and data that primarily focused on direct toxicity to benthic invertebrates. Although this provided an important foundation for contaminated sediment assessment, it does not reflect the full range of pathways, receptors, and scientific tools now understood to be relevant to sediment risk.

Several limitations of the current framework are central to the need for modernization:
* The standards primarily address ecological direct contact for benthic invertebrates and do not systematically protect higher-trophic ecological receptors.
* Food-web transfer and bioaccumulation are not directly integrated into Schedule 3.4.
* Human health pathways, including direct contact with sediment and dietary exposure through aquatic foods, are not systematically addressed through sediment-specific standards.
* Current standards do not adequately reflect Indigenous uses, including traditional food consumption and culturally important aquatic resources.
* The framework does not systematically incorporate bioavailability adjustment, despite established scientific understanding that sediment chemistry can strongly influence contaminant exposure and toxicity.
* The contaminant list does not adequately address emerging contaminants or substances that are now recognized as important in aquatic systems.
* The lack of a modernized framework increases reliance on site-specific risk assessments, which can be costly, inconsistent, and difficult to manage for high-risk or high-profile sites.

The central problem is therefore not simply that individual numerical values are outdated. The broader issue is that the current Schedule 3.4 structure is pathway-limited and does not provide a comprehensive, scalable, or adaptable framework for deriving and applying modern sediment standards.

<div id="sec-2-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 2.1 The Waste Management Act Criteria and the Schedule 9 Baseline
The earliest formal effort to establish sediment criteria in British Columbia was *Criteria for Managing Contaminated Sediment in British Columbia*, prepared pursuant to section 26(a) of the *Waste Management Act* for the BC Ministry of Environment, Lands and Parks (MacDonald and Macfarlane 1999), and updated for the BC Ministry of Water, Land and Air Protection (Macfarlane and MacDonald 2002). That work evaluated seven distinct approaches for deriving sediment quality criteria - screening level concentration, effects range and effects level, apparent effects threshold, equilibrium partitioning, logistic regression modelling, consensus-based, and tissue residue approaches - after which the Federal/Provincial Technical Committee developed a four-step strategy: establishment of sediment management objectives; identification of preliminary benchmarks for sediment chemistry; development of concentration-response relationships for contaminant mixtures in freshwater and marine sediments; and derivation of the numerical criteria (BC MWLAP 2003, *Technical Appendix*, section 3.3). The criteria were set at a 20% probability of observing roughly a 20% reduction in survival for sensitive sites (the P20 value) and a 50% probability of the same for typical sites (the P50 value).

That criteria work established the numerical values that were added to the *Contaminated Sites Regulation* as Schedule 9 (*Generic Numerical Sediment Criteria*) by B.C. Reg. 324/2004, s. 70, under the *Environmental Management Act*. Schedule 9 was repealed effective November 1, 2017 and replaced by Schedule 3.4, which was created by B.C. Reg. 253/2016, s. 17 (as amended by B.C. Reg. 196/2017, s. 3); the Schedule 3.4 now in force was re-enacted by B.C. Reg. 133/2022, App., s. 12, in force March 1, 2023. These criteria were derived by multiplying a Canadian Council of Ministers of the Environment (CCME) 1999 probable effect level for each substance by a probability factor (P20 = 0.62 for freshwater; P50 = 1.2 for freshwater and marine), as recorded in Chapter 7 of Protocol 28. The derivation addressed exposure scenarios for sediment-dwelling ecological organisms; it *did not evaluate bioaccumulation, food-chain biomagnification, or human seafood consumption risks*, and Protocol 28 states that the resulting sediment standards are not considered protective of human health.

<div id="sec-2-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 2.2 The Amphipod Screening Deficit
The toxicity tests underlying the Schedule 3.4 standards are, per *Protocol 28*, Chapter 7.2, a 28 to 42-day test for the freshwater amphipod *Hyalella azteca* and 10-day tests for the marine amphipods *Ampelisca abdita* and *Rhepoxynius abronius*. Whatever their duration, these single-species chamber bioassays are structurally incapable of evaluating bioaccumulation:
1. **Failure to Reach Steady-State:** For non-polar organic chemicals with log Kow > 5.0, uptake kinetics into invertebrate lipid pools are slow, and ASTM E1688-10 identifies such compounds - along with metabolically refractory chemicals such as highly chlorinated PCBs and dioxins - as unlikely to reach steady-state tissue residues even within a 28-day exposure (E1688-10 section 12.5). ASTM E1688-10 recommends a 28-day exposure, not the 10-day duration used in both marine tests here, as "the standard single sampling procedure" (E1688-10 section 1.2), because a 28-day exposure "provides inherently better estimates of steady-state than a 10-day bioaccumulation test" (E1688-10 section 12.2). In the studies ASTM compiles, 10-day tissue residues averaged only 56% of the steady-state value across the organic contaminants studied, with only 33% of organisms reaching 80% of steady-state within 10 days; PCB residues specifically averaged approximately 34% of steady-state after 10 days, ranging as low as 12% (Annex A3.2-A3.2.1, p. 37). **[Source to be confirmed.** The previously cited 15% to 30% figure for 10-day recovery of steady-state, and the Landrum et al. (1992) and Boese et al. (1995) citations offered in support of it, appear on no other line of this corpus and in no bibliography; neither source is held in the project reference library and neither claim could be verified. Both have been removed rather than repaired. The percentages retained above are quoted directly from ASTM E1688-10, the only one of the three original citations held in the reference library.**]**
2. **Absence of Dietary Trophic Transfer:** Acute laboratory bioassays measure direct interstitial water and particle contact in single-species chambers without multi-trophic predator-prey feeding, eliminating the biomagnification process entirely.

<div id="sec-2-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 2.3 The 2017 Restructuring: Schedule 9 to Schedule 3.4
Effective November 1, 2017, B.C. Reg. 253/2016 restructured the *Contaminated Sites Regulation*: section 18 repealed the former Schedule 9 (*Generic Numerical Sediment Criteria*) and section 17 (as amended by B.C. Reg. 196/2017, s. 3) created Schedule 3.4, in which the values are expressed as sediment *standards* rather than sediment *criteria*. The Schedule 3.4 now in force was re-enacted by B.C. Reg. 133/2022, App., s. 12 (as amended by B.C. Reg. 2/2023), in force March 1, 2023. Through both steps the underlying numbers, derivation algorithms, and single-species toxicity assumptions were carried forward without scientific modification. Phase 2 modernization would be the first comprehensive scientific overhaul of B.C.'s sediment regulatory architecture in over two decades.

---

<div id="sec-3-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 3.0 Purpose, Scope, and Role of this Options Paper

This paper is intended to serve as the working draft of the final Matrix Options Paper identified as the primary Phase 2 deliverable in the Phase 2 project plan. It will be refined over the course of Phase 2 as technical research is completed and appended to the report.

The purpose of this paper is to:
1. Summarize the scientific and regulatory rationale for modernizing BC's sediment standards.
2. Synthesize the Phase 1 White Paper foundation into the Phase 2 options-analysis framework.
3. Describe the proposed matrix standards framework that Phase 2 is developing.
4. Identify and evaluate technical options associated with the Phase 2 research tasks.
5. Provide a structure for integrating technical workstream reports into the final Matrix Options Paper.
6. Document the rationale for emerging recommendations and prioritize high-concern emerging contaminants (e.g., per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances [PFAS], 6PPD-quinone, alkylphenols, microplastics, and pharmaceuticals).
7. Support ENV's consideration of a scientifically defensible Schedule 3.4 modernization package by producing the final Matrix Options Paper as a recommendation package for submission to ENV executive leadership at month 12 (June 2027), after which ENV follows its own internal policy, consultation and approval processes.

This paper does not determine whether the sediment standards modernization project should proceed. That direction is set out in the Phase 1 White Paper and in the Phase 2 project plan. The purpose of this paper is to evaluate how the matrix framework should be designed, supported, documented, and carried forward.

The following items are outside the scope of Phase 2:
* final numerical sediment standards;
* regulatory amendments;
* supporting technical guidance or protocols; and
* a complete sediment database.

The final Matrix Options Paper is expected to summarize research findings, progress made, options considered, and the rationale supporting recommendations. It will also document the collaborative partnership between ENV, SABCS, SSTAC, and the TWG.

---

<div id="sec-4-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 4.0 Vision for Modernized Sediment Standards

The vision for modernized sediment standards is to establish a fully integrated framework that protects ecological and human health across all major exposure routes. The future framework should be scientifically defensible, scalable, adaptable, transparent, and practical for contaminated sites decision-making.

**The three-part structure below is given by the Phase 2 project plan. It is not an option this paper selects, and this paper does not evaluate whether to adopt it.** The plan directs it in five places - its Vision Statement ("Modernize Schedule 3.4 to include three parts"), its Background ("a modern, multi-part Schedule 3.4"), its Objectives, its Task 7.1 ("three part structure"), and its deliverables table - and nothing in the plan frames it as selectable. What the plan leaves genuinely open, and where the options work in this paper therefore sits, is the derivation equations for the four receptor-pathways (Task 6.1), the dimensioning of Part 1 (Tasks 6.2 and 7.1), and the adoption procedure for Parts 2 and 3 (Task 7.2). Section 9.7 explains why the alternative architectures a reader may have in mind were not pursued; it is context for a plan-given structure, not a selection between candidates. As set out in the scope note above, the plan confers no authority, and ENV decides.

<div id="sec-4-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 4.1 Part 1: Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards & Four Proposed Sediment Uses
Part 1 would include Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards for priority substances that warrant a refined, pathway-specific approach. These standards would address four receptor-pathway combinations:
* protection of human health from direct exposure to sediment contaminants;
* protection of human health from exposure to sediment contaminants in food;
* protection of ecological health from direct exposure to sediment contaminants; and
* protection of ecological health from exposure to sediment contaminants in food.

**Applicability: freshwater, estuarine and marine.** Sediment standards apply to freshwater, estuarine and marine sediments, and the modernized framework is intended to apply across all three. Readers of this early draft should be aware that the supporting material assembled so far is predominantly coastal and marine. The sentinel receptors, dietary assumptions and exposure scenarios illustrated in this draft and its appendices are drawn largely from coastal and estuarine settings, and no freshwater-specific receptor set, diet or exposure-parameter set has yet been compiled. Freshwater receptor and parameter coverage is therefore an identified gap in this draft, to be filled through the Phase 2 input-parameter compilation and the pathway-structure work. No freshwater parameter has been assumed, extrapolated or generated for this draft, and none should be inferred from the coastal and marine values shown.

To replace the "sensitive vs. typical" binary categorization, this paper puts forward **four draft Sediment Use Categories for discussion and refinement**, intended to reflect the operational realities of provincial aquatic receiving environments. They are a rough-draft proposal prepared for this early draft. They are not defined in the *Contaminated Sites Regulation*, they have no legal status, they are not a position of the Province, and they are expected to change through SSTAC and TWG review.

*Table: ROUGH-DRAFT PROPOSAL FOR DISCUSSION - NOT A STATUTORY CLASSIFICATION. The four categories below, their definitions, and every assumption in them are draft proposals only. Nothing here is in force and nothing here may be applied to any site.*

| Sediment Use Category | Spatial & Ecological Definition | Human Activity & Exposure Assumptions | Applicable Receptors & Protection Goals |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **1. Aquatic Wildlands (AW)** | Pristine, high-conservation-value aquatic habitats, provincial marine parks, ecological reserves, critical salmonid spawning streams, and sensitive estuaries. | Incidental wilderness recreation; baseline ecological background protection. | Apex piscivorous wildlife (Heron, Otter, Seal, Mink); sensitive cold-water salmonid spawning; high-sensitivity benthic taxa (Ephemeroptera, Plecoptera, Trichoptera). |
| **2. Aquatic Recreational / Traditional Harvest (AR/TH)** | Coastal shorelines, tidal mudflats, estuarine harvesting grounds, shellfish beds, public beaches, and First Nations Food, Social, and Ceremonial (FSC) areas. | High-frequency wading, swimming, clam digging; harvesting and consumption of locally caught aquatic foods, including by high-intake consumers. No consumption rate defines this category: a future seafood ingestion rate would be assigned per exposure scenario only after primary verification of a candidate record (Section 7.8.2). | Human adult and toddler subsistence harvesters; country food consumers (eating whole crabs/hepatopancreas); recreational beach users; nearshore intertidal benthos. |
| **3. Commercial Aquatic (CA)** | Marinas, commercial fishing harbours, aquaculture tenures, commercial transport waterways, and urbanized waterfronts. | Moderate occupational exposure; commercial navigation; limited direct contact. | Commercial fish and bivalve aquaculture stocks; intermediate benthic macroinvertebrate communities; non-harvested forage fish. |
| **4. Industrial Aquatic (IA)** | Active industrial berths, shipping terminals, deep-water log handling tenures, shipyard repair basins, and chemical manufacturing outfalls. Industrial Aquatic settings may be freshwater, estuarine or marine; the category is named "Aquatic" rather than "Marine" for that reason. | Occupational shoreline workers; industrial divers (restricted public access; commercial fishing prohibited). | Protection against catastrophic benthic lethality, acute chemical toxicity, and non-aqueous phase liquid (NAPL) mobilization into adjacent water bodies. |

*Note on how the four categories are defined: each is defined by its setting and by the receptors present in it, and none is defined by a consumption rate. Where an exposure scenario within a category involves eating locally caught aquatic food, a future seafood ingestion rate would be assigned for that scenario only after primary verification of a candidate record in Section 7.8.2; the rate is a property of the scenario, not of the category. No rate has been adopted or assigned to any category in this draft.*

**How the two axes combine.** The four receptor-pathways are the fundamental structure of Part 1. Different sediment uses carry different receptors, different exposure pathways and different protection levels, so each receptor-pathway is expected to carry more than one scenario. Four receptor-pathways across four Sediment Use Categories give 16 exposure scenarios initially, and that is the working basis for this draft. The permutation count is not fixed at sixteen. Some combinations may not apply, others may need to be subdivided, and the final count is to be settled as the options in Section 9.0 are evaluated.

<div id="sec-4-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 4.2 Part 2: Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to Protect Human Health
Part 2 would include generic sediment standards to protect human health. These values may be adopted or adapted from other jurisdictions where matrix derivation is not feasible, not warranted, or not supported by adequate data.

<div id="sec-4-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 4.3 Part 3: Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to Protect Ecological Health
Part 3 would include generic sediment standards to protect ecological health. These values would also be adopted or adapted through a transparent and scientifically defensible procedure.

This three-part structure recognizes that Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards require substantial data, technical review, equations, input parameters, and supporting rationale. It also recognizes that broader contaminant coverage may be achieved more efficiently by adopting generic standards for substances where full matrix derivation is not appropriate or feasible.

The matrix framework therefore represents the refined standards pathway for priority substances, while the generic standards pathway provides a practical mechanism for expanding Schedule 3.4 coverage. Together, these components are intended to create a defensible, scalable, and adaptable foundation for modernized sediment standards.

<div id="sec-4-3-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

#### 4.3.1 The Schedule 3.1 Precedent for Soil

> **[STATUS: SOURCE-GAP QUARANTINE - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]** Burrard Inlet source identifiers and consumption-objective claims in this subsection are retained as research context only. No rate, objective, or source-specific parameter is accepted as a current derivation input pending exact primary-source verification.

It is worth being explicit, since the three-part structure above is given by the project plan rather than chosen here, that it is also not a novel invention for sediment. The *Contaminated Sites Regulation* already operates an identical three-part architecture for soil, in Schedule 3.1, and has done so since 1 November 2017.

Schedule 3.1 is drafted in exactly three parts: Part 1 is a set of per-substance matrix tables headed "MATRIX *n* - NUMERICAL SOIL STANDARDS"; Part 2 is "GENERIC NUMERICAL SOIL STANDARDS TO PROTECT HUMAN HEALTH"; and Part 3 is "GENERIC NUMERICAL SOIL STANDARDS TO PROTECT ECOLOGICAL HEALTH". CSR s. 1 defines "matrix numerical soil standard" by reference to Part 1 of Schedule 3.1 and "generic numerical soil standard" by reference to Part 2 or 3, and ss. 11(1)(a) and 17(1)(a) each engage "(i) the applicable generic numerical soil standard, or (ii) the lowest value of the applicable matrix numerical soil standards". Each Part 1 matrix is a single substance-specific table whose rows are site-specific factors grouped under "HUMAN HEALTH PROTECTION" and "ENVIRONMENTAL PROTECTION" and whose columns are the eight land uses -- the same shape (receptor-pathway rows against use-category columns) proposed for sediment above.

**A second B.C. precedent, in a marine setting.** Schedule 3.1 is a soil precedent: it establishes
that the drafting form works, but it says nothing about whether the Province sets protective
objectives in a marine sediment context. The Burrard Inlet work does. B.C. ENV and the B.C. Ministry
of Health, with Tsleil-Waututh Nation, published *Tissue Quality Objectives Recommendations for
Burrard Inlet* (Thompson and Stein 2021, ISBN 978-0-7726-8005-1), which describes consumption-based
objectives for a specific marine inlet and records a toddler subsistence consumption rate that is
not accepted as a current derivation input in this successor.

**What it is and is not.** It is a **tissue** quality objective for **one inlet**, developed with a
specific Nation, not a sediment standard and not a province-wide instrument. It does not establish
the matrix structure. What it establishes is narrower, and still useful. The report's own cover reads *Burrard Inlet Water Quality
**Proposed** Objectives*, its chapter summary says the tissue screening values were calculated
*"for recommendation as water quality objectives"* and are *"provided in this report as examples"*,
and no objective in it has been set. **The Province has not set protective objectives for a marine
setting; it has proposed them.** What the precedent does establish is that the Province has already
had to make, and publish, the receptor and consumption assumptions that consumption-based marine
work requires, with a First Nation as partner. That is the useful part, and it does not depend on
the objectives having been adopted.

**What this establishes, and what it does not.** The precedent shows that a three-part matrix/generic structure of this kind is already workable regulatory drafting within this same regulation, and that the operative cross-references -- the s. 1 definitions and the ss. 11(1)(a) and 17(1)(a) mechanics -- are already drafted around it, so extending the pattern to Schedule 3.4 is not a novel drafting exercise. It does **not** establish the number or shape of the sediment receptor-pathway rows, the sediment use category columns, how a sediment matrix standard would combine with a generic standard, or any other design choice for Part 1 of a sediment schedule -- those remain open questions. The full statutory citations above, together with the drafting questions the soil precedent leaves unresolved for sediment (dimensioning, waterbody types, and how the four pathway values combine), are set out in Section 18.1.

---

<div id="sec-4-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 4.4 Waterbody Applicability: Freshwater, Estuarine and Marine

#### 4.4.1 What the framework is intended to cover

The modernized Schedule 3.4 is intended to apply to freshwater, estuarine and marine sediments, for
all three Parts. That is the scope of the schedule it would replace, and it is the scope of the
provincial framework the project was set up to modernize.

This is not a new scope. The *Contaminated Sites Regulation* already frames sediment in exactly these
terms. Section 1 defines "sensitive sediment use" as "the use as habitat for sensitive components of
freshwater, marine or estuarine aquatic ecosystems of a site containing sediment", and defines "generic
numerical sediment standard" as "the concentration of a substance specified in Schedule 3.4 for a
particular sediment use". Protocol 28, which sets out how the in-force standards were derived, states
that they "include standards for both freshwater and marine/estuarine aquatic life environments".

#### 4.4.2 The structure the in-force schedule already uses

The in-force Schedule 3.4 resolves the waterbody question in its column headings. Its four value columns
are:

| Column | Waterbody class | Sediment use |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Freshwater Sediment | Standard for Sensitive Use |
| 2 | Freshwater Sediment | Standard for Typical Use |
| 3 | Marine and Estuarine Sediment | Standard for Sensitive Use |
| 4 | Marine and Estuarine Sediment | Standard for Typical Use |

Two features of that structure matter for this framework:

1. **Waterbody class is a column axis, not a separate schedule.** The same substance carries a
   freshwater value and a marine-and-estuarine value side by side, derived by the same method with
   different inputs. This is the natural structure for the modernized schedule to follow, because it
   keeps one substance list, one method and one document, and it puts the difference exactly where the
   difference actually is -- in the parameters.
2. **The in-force schedule groups estuarine with marine, giving two classes rather than three.**
   Where a site sits in transitional water and two standards both apply, ministry guidance directs
   that the more stringent be used. **Technical Guidance 15, *Concentration Limits for the
   Protection of Aquatic Receiving Environments*** (Version 2.0, effective 1 November 2017), whose
   *Salinity* section expressly covers the Regulation's Schedule 3.4 sediment standards, states
   that "[w]here the water is considered to be transitional between freshwater and
   marine/estuarine water, or if a proponent elects not to characterize the salinity per se, the
   more stringent of the freshwater or marine/estuarine standards or guidelines should be used."
   The same guidance defines freshwater as **salinity below 1.5 practical salinity units**, citing
   Schedule 3.2. **This is policy set out in guidance rather than a rule codified in the
   regulation, and the instruction is that the more stringent value *should* be used - so some
   flexibility exists.**
   That status matters for the modernized schedule. Guidance policy carries forward more readily
   than a bare convention, but it is not self-executing: if the matrix schedule wants the
   more-stringent outcome to hold consistently in transitional waters, it still has to decide
   whether to state it in the schedule itself, codify something different, or continue to rely on
   the guidance and accept the flexibility that comes with it. **That is a live decision for the
   modernization, not a settled position it can assume.** If the framework separates
   estuarine as a third class, the question has to be answered directly and the parameter set grows by a
   third. This draft has not decided the question, but it does not treat the three settings identically
   either: its own bivalve PAH ecosystem multiplier already takes three distinct values -- 1.0 for
   freshwater, 5.0 for estuarine and 15.0 for coastal marine intertidal systems -- which is a three-way
   split sitting inside a framework whose output structure is two-way. That inconsistency should be
   resolved deliberately.

The choice between two waterbody classes and three belongs to Task 6.2, "define structure for the four
pathways", in months 6 to 7. What this draft can usefully record now is its arithmetic consequence. Part 1 of
the modernized schedule is currently framed as four receptor-pathways across four sediment use
categories, giving sixteen exposure scenarios as a starting point. Adding waterbody class as an
explicit axis makes that thirty-two scenarios if estuarine stays grouped with marine, or
forty-eight if estuarine is separated. That is a scoping decision with real consequences for Task
3.8, and it should be taken deliberately rather than inherited by default.

#### 4.4.3 What the current draft material actually supports

> **[STATUS: SOURCE-GAP QUARANTINE - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]** Quantitative freshwater parameter records listed below are candidate research records only. Their values, scope, units, and applicability are not accepted as current derivation inputs or decision-driving claims until exact primary sources are independently checked.

The supporting material assembled for this draft is weighted toward coastal and estuarine
settings. Term frequency alone does not establish this - marine and freshwater terms occur at
comparable rates across the draft - because a statement of scope and a derivation input count
alike. What separates them is the character of the occurrences.

Most freshwater occurrences are not derivation content. The majority are one of: a statement of
Part 2 and Part 3 scope, which correctly covers
freshwater; a description of the BC Aquatic Database's intended geographic coverage; a recital of the
1999 CCME and Protocol 28 derivation history, which used a freshwater amphipod; or general partitioning
literature. > **[STATUS: QUARANTINED - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]** The quantitative records in the list below are retained as candidate research material only. They are not accepted as operative Part 1 inputs, defaults, thresholds, or decision-driving values until exact primary sources, scope, units, and applicability are independently verified.

What remains as **candidate freshwater records pending primary verification** is four items:

1. A mink dietary composition of 70% small freshwater fish and 30% benthic invertebrates (Pathway 4).
2. A candidate methylmercury freshwater/estuarine protein-normalized $BSAF_{protein}$ record with a reported value of 15.0, retained for verification only; no library default is carried in this successor (Pathway 2).
3. An ecosystem multiplier for freshwater of $M_{eco} = 1.0$ -- that is, the null case, the value that
   applies when no adjustment is made (Pathway 2, PAH class).
4. One parenthetical alternative calculation in a worked example, which pairs the freshwater
   $BSAF_{protein}$ with a coastal marine diet, and so is not a freshwater case at all.

Against that, the coastal and estuarine parameterisation is complete enough to run end to end. The
imbalance by derivation element:

| Element | What the draft carries | Freshwater coverage |
|---|---|---|
| Wildlife sentinel receptors (Pathway 4) | Four: Great Blue Heron, River Otter, Harbour Seal, Mink | One of four, the mink, is parameterised on a freshwater diet. One, the harbour seal, is marine-only and is the governing receptor for several substances. The heron and otter are described as spanning both settings but are given coastal and estuarine prey |
| Empirical BSAF compendium | Two tables, each 10 substances by 4 taxon columns: benthic infauna, crab muscle, crab hepatopancreas, demersal sole | Zero freshwater taxa columns and zero freshwater BSAF values. One footnote gives a single freshwater methylmercury default. The heading of one of the tables says "Marine and Freshwater Ecosystems"; the contents are marine |
| Human food-pathway intake (Pathway 2) | An intake rate apportioned across salmonids and pelagic finfish, bivalves and intertidal shellfish, and Dungeness crab | The apportionment is labelled "Pacific Marine" and contains no freshwater-harvested species. How Pathway 2 is parameterised for province-wide standards is itself an open question for Task 6.2, so this cell should be revisited when that question is settled rather than filled in with a freshwater equivalent of the present approach |
| Human direct contact (Pathway 1) | Incidental ingestion and dermal adherence, with a wetted surface area and adherence factor | The scenario is explicitly intertidal: the adherence factor is justified by "cohesive, fine-grained intertidal mudflats" and the wetted surface area by clam digging. There is no freshwater contact scenario |
| Ecological direct contact (Pathway 3) | Equilibrium partitioning with $K_{oc}$ and a chronic water benchmark; AVS-SEM for divalent metals | The equations are salinity-neutral in form. The chronic water benchmark table carries one value per substance with **no freshwater or marine designation at all**, so it cannot be told which water type each value protects. Whether AVS-SEM behaves the same way in freshwater is not examined |
| Sediment use categories | Four: Aquatic Wildlands, Aquatic Recreational / Traditional Harvest, Commercial Aquatic, Industrial Aquatic | Only Aquatic Wildlands names freshwater settings, as "critical salmonid spawning streams" and "pristine freshwater lakes". The other three are defined in tidal, coastal and harbour terms |
| Supporting evidence exercise | Coastal and estuarine source material is used to test whether the framework inputs are available; no worked derived outputs are carried in this paper | Freshwater parameter coverage remains an evidence gap |
| Named BC waterbodies | Coastal harbours appear repeatedly | The Columbia, Thompson and Skeena rivers and Okanagan, Kootenay and Stuart lakes appear only in the BC Aquatic Database appendix, describing intended data coverage. They carry no framework content |

There is one further consistency point. Part 3 of the modernized schedule, the generic ecological
standards adopted from other jurisdictions, is already structured with separate freshwater and marine
columns, and the adoption procedure already carries a step requiring that adopted standards "maintain
strict separation between Freshwater and Marine/Estuarine ecosystems". Part 1 is currently the only Part
of the three without that separation.

#### 4.4.4 The applicability statement

Stated plainly, so that a reader at Kootenay Lake or on the Thompson River knows where they stand:

* **The framework is intended to apply to freshwater, estuarine and marine sediments.** That intent is
  not qualified, and it is the scope this project was set up to deliver.
* **The supporting material in this draft exercises the framework on coastal and estuarine cases only.**
  The supporting case material, empirical BSAF values and human exposure scenarios
  in Part 1 is coastal or estuarine. The equations are not marine-specific; the parameters are.
* **Nothing in this draft should be applied to a freshwater sediment site.** Not as a screening value,
  not as an indication of direction, not as a starting point for a site-specific assessment. The
  parameters that would make it a freshwater result do not exist yet.
* **No freshwater parameter or receptor has been invented to fill the gap, and none will be.** A
  plausible-looking freshwater column would be worse than an empty one, because it would be used.

#### 4.4.5 What is needed to close the gap

Closing the gap is parameterisation, not redesign. The four receptor-pathways and their equation forms
are waterbody-neutral: a tolerable daily intake divided by an intake rate, an equilibrium partitioning
relationship, a wildlife no-effect level back-calculated through a food chain. What changes between
freshwater and marine is which receptor, which prey, which partitioning constant and which benchmark.

Three parameter families are required, in this order:

**(a) Freshwater receptors.** For each pathway, at least one freshwater-relevant receptor must be
selected and defended:

* *Ecological direct contact.* The starting candidates are already in this draft and already carry
  standing. *Hyalella azteca* is the freshwater amphipod used in the 28 to 42-day tests behind the
  current freshwater standards; *Chironomus dilutus* appears in this draft's own list of standard
  freshwater bioassay organisms; and *Lumbriculus variegatus* is the freshwater bioaccumulation test
  organism named by ASTM E1688-10, which this draft also names. Which of them the modernized framework
  uses is a choice to be made and defended, and none of the three yet carries a BC parameter set.
* *Ecological food-web.* At least one freshwater-feeding wildlife receptor is required. The mink is
  already parameterised on a freshwater diet in this draft and is a candidate. Whether a freshwater
  piscivorous bird and a freshwater piscivorous fish are also required is a question for the
  bioaccumulation review.
  **[NEEDED -- TWG bioaccumulation stream, Task 5.1, months 5 to 6: selection of freshwater sentinel
  receptors with documented BC or Pacific Northwest body weights, ingestion rates and dietary
  compositions. No receptor is to be entered without a documented source.]**
* *Human health.* Both human pathways need a freshwater exposure scenario -- who is in contact with
  freshwater sediment, and in what circumstances. **[NEEDED -- ENV, Task 3.7: confirmation of whether
  human direct-contact and food scenarios for freshwater sediment are to be defined generically for
  province-wide standards, or left to site-specific assessment under Protocol 1.]**

**(b) Freshwater partitioning and bioaccumulation parameters.**

* Freshwater chronic water benchmarks, identified as freshwater, to sit beside the marine values in the
  equilibrium partitioning table. The present table gives one unlabelled value per substance and
  therefore cannot be used for either waterbody class with confidence.
* Freshwater biota-sediment accumulation factors, for freshwater benthic invertebrates and freshwater
  fish, with the same tissue basis and normalisation as the marine values.
* Confirmation of whether the acid volatile sulfide and simultaneously extracted metals approach carries
  across to freshwater sediments unchanged.
* A freshwater sediment organic carbon baseline, or confirmation that the single provincial 1.0% working
  baseline is defensible across freshwater river basins and coastal marine flats alike. This draft
  already identifies that question as unresolved.
* **[NEEDED -- Task 3.8, month 3 onward: freshwater partitioning and bioaccumulation parameters
  compiled to the same template and the same evidentiary standard as the marine set, with a source for
  every value. Task 3.9 reviews both sets together in month 8.]**

**(c) Freshwater evidence package.** A freshwater evidence package is required before the framework can be said to have been exercised province-wide. It must document freshwater receptors, parameters, source provenance, and site-condition criteria. It must not be created by substituting marine parameters into a freshwater label, and no worked derived output is part of this Phase 2 paper.

#### 4.4.6 How this is carried forward

* Task 6.2, "define structure for the four pathways", months 6 to 7, takes the structural question: two
  waterbody classes or three, and how that interacts with the four sediment use categories and the
  sixteen exposure scenarios.
* Task 3.8, month 3 onward, compiles the freshwater parameters alongside the marine ones. Task 3.9
  reviews them in month 8.
* Task 5.1, months 5 to 6, selects the freshwater receptors as part of the bioaccumulation review.
* Task 8.3, month 9, "resolve gaps and inconsistencies", is where any residual freshwater gap is either
  closed or declared in the final Matrix Options Paper.

Until then, this draft is a coastal and estuarine exercise of a framework intended to be
province-wide, and it says so.

---

<div id="sec-5-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 5.0 Scientific Foundation from Phase 1

> **Which Phase 1 document this section rests on, and what the reconciliation found.**
> The findings below were originally drawn from the Phase 1 white paper
> *Preliminary Framework for Modernizing British Columbia's Sediment Standards*
> (v3.0, 2025). They have since been reconciled against the later Phase 1
> document confirmed as authoritative: *Proposed Framework for Modernizing
> British Columbia's Sediment Standards*, prepared by the Science Advisory Board
> for Contaminated Sites and the Science and Standards Technical Advisory
> Committee, July 2026 (circulated as v3.1.3). A note on how that document is
> cited: its title page carries a title, an authorship line and the date
> July 2026 but no version number, so the v3.1.3 designation is taken from the
> circulated file name rather than from the document text; the only version
> string inside the document refers to "a draft version (2.5) of this report"
> reviewed by the Technical Working Group.
>
> **Result of the reconciliation.** Each of the eight findings listed below is
> supported by the July 2026 framework, and each now carries a locator to the
> section of that document where the supporting text appears. No finding was
> contradicted. The framework has, however, moved on from this summary in six
> respects, recorded after the list; the draft is not silently rewritten to
> match. The reconciliation covered the main report, Sections I through VII of
> the July 2026 framework. Its appendices, which include the survey, workshop
> and Technical Working Group transcripts, were not reviewed line by line, so a
> finding could in principle be qualified there without that being reflected
> here. The alignment discussed in Section 10.0 was likewise written against
> v3.0 and has been checked against the July 2026 framework; a corresponding
> note appears there.

The Phase 1 White Paper provides the scientific and engagement foundation for this Options Paper. It reviewed the derivation and limitations of current Schedule 3.4 standards, summarized scientific concepts relevant to modern sediment standards, completed a jurisdictional scan, documented engagement feedback, and proposed a preliminary matrix standards framework.

Key Phase 1 findings include, each with the locator in the July 2026 framework where the supporting text is found:

* Modern sediment standards should distinguish between direct exposure and food-pathway exposure.
  *Supported. July 2026 framework, Section V.A, "Derivation of Matrix Sediment Standards," which organizes standards on "two distinct axes: the protection goal (Ecological Health vs. Human Health) and the primary exposure pathway (Direct Exposure vs. Food Pathway Exposure)."*
* Modern sediment standards should distinguish between ecological and human health protection goals.
  *Supported. Same two-axis passage, Section V.A; and Section III.D, "Synthesis and Key Learnings for BC," which lists among the key elements for BC "Adopting a matrix sediment standard framework protective of human health and ecological receptors (including separate standards for direct and food pathway protection)."*
* Bioavailability adjustment is needed to improve the scientific relevance of sediment standards and site assessments.
  *Supported. Section V.A, "Baseline Bioavailability Normalization"; Section V.B, which lists "Inadequate Bioavailability Considerations" among the deficiencies the framework is designed to address; and Section VII.A, which sets "Formally Incorporate Bioavailability Adjustments" as a named priority.*
* Bioaccumulation and food-web transfer must be addressed for substances that can accumulate in aquatic organisms and expose wildlife or people through diet.
  *Supported. Section V.A, "Sediment Standard for Bioaccumulation Protection (SedS-food)," and Section VI.A, "Food Web Protection (SedS-food)." Section V.B describes food web transfer as "a critical pathway not covered by the current benthic-focused standards."*
* Substance prioritization is needed because not every contaminant can be addressed through full matrix derivation.
  *Supported. Section V.A, "Prioritization Grid Classification System," which would weigh "occurrence (detection frequency in BC) against hazard (toxicity, persistence, bioaccumulation)" so that resources focus on the highest-risk substances "rather than simply adopting every available standard from other jurisdictions."*
* The framework should be supported by a BC-specific data foundation, including occurrence data, background data, bioavailability information, and bioaccumulation information.
  *Supported. Section V.A, "Understanding of Background and Current Conditions," and Section VII.A, which names the Data Compilation Project as the first priority and identifies the gaps as "background concentrations, bioavailability parameters, and bioaccumulation factors." Occurrence data is named in Section VI.B, in the description of the data-driven foundational research option: "occurrence of detectable concentrations (to identify new priority contaminants)."*
* Indigenous uses and food-based exposure pathways need to be incorporated through respectful and appropriate processes.
  *Supported. Section V, core principle "Protection of Indigenous Uses and Integration of Indigenous Knowledge"; and Section VII.B, under which the SSTAC would prepare "a proposed strategy for supporting respectful and appropriate Indigenous engagement throughout the scientific development process."*
* Clear guidance, decision tools, and transparent documentation will be essential for future implementation.
  *Supported. Section VII.C, "The Critical Path to Success: Investing in Guidance, Data, and Training," which records that respondents specifically requested "decision trees, flowcharts, and worked examples" together with "transparent documentation on how the new standards were derived," and which treats guidance and training as "a core project component with a dedicated budget."*

**Where the July 2026 framework has moved on from this summary.** The eight findings above are not contradicted, but the authoritative Phase 1 document is not simply a restatement of them. Six divergences are recorded here rather than resolved by editing the draft to match.

1. **Two named standards, not four co-equal pathways.** This paper presents the matrix as four receptor-pathway combinations of equal standing (Sections 4.1 and 6.0). The July 2026 framework, Section V.A, states that "The two primary outputs of this matrix are" a "Sediment Standards for Direct Protection (SedS-contact)" and a "Sediment Standard for Bioaccumulation Protection (SedS-food)," each then split by receptor into an ecological and a human health variant (SedS-contactECO, SedS-contactHH, SedS-foodECO, SedS-foodHH). The same four cells are therefore present, but as two named standards with receptor variants rather than as four independently named pathways, and this paper does not use the framework's SedS-contact and SedS-food nomenclature anywhere.
2. **Indigenous Uses: a difference in how the same protection is structured.** The July 2026 framework, Section V, states as a core principle that "The framework categorizes the protection of Indigenous Uses (e.g., safely fishing, hunting, and gathering traditional foods and medicines) as a distinct pathway." This paper does not carry a separate Indigenous Uses pathway. It treats Indigenous receptors as a group of human receptors within the same receptor-pathway matrix standards - the human health direct-contact and country-foods pathways - rather than as a fifth pathway alongside them. That is a structural choice about where the protection sits, not a decision to exclude it. Phase 2 is not formally engaging with First Nations (see Section 11.0), so no Indigenous-specific pathway is developed in this framework at this stage. Receptor-pathway combinations specific to Indigenous uses may well exist and warrant their own treatment; identifying them is outside this phase and is noted here so that the difference from the July 2026 framework is on the record rather than silently carried. **A second half of the framework's principle, which this reconciliation does not reach.** The sentence quoted above is followed in the same passage by a broader statement: Indigenous Knowledge and Science *"serves as a foundational lens through which all sediment standard development, site assessment, and remediation goals may be viewed"* (Section V). **That is not a claim about pathways, and folding Indigenous receptors into the human-health pathways does not answer it.** Nothing in this paper addresses it, and describing the divergence as purely structural - the same protection, differently located - is therefore not the whole of the difference. Whether and how that broader principle is carried forward is a question for the framework's authors and for ENV's own processes, not one this paper settles; it is recorded here so the gap is visible rather than closed by a reconciliation that does not cover it. **On whose authority this structure rests.** This is a project direction, not a conclusion drawn from a source. The Phase 2 project plan does not establish it; the July 2026 framework states the contrary; and no primary source bears on it. It is recorded here on the same terms as every other unsourced position in this paper, so that it can be challenged on the right grounds. Two things should be weighed by anyone reconsidering it: the plan's Task 6.4 is titled to integrate Indigenous consumption modifiers **and high-use scenarios**, which may be broader than the parameterisation reading taken here; and a decision to treat Indigenous uses as a distinct pathway would be a framework-level change, not a parameter change, and is therefore better made before the framework is settled than after.
3. **A tiered assessment approach is a named component of the framework.** The July 2026 framework, Section V.A, "Implementation of a Tiered Assessment Approach," and its Executive Summary describe screening against the matrix standards, site-specific standards accounting for site factors influencing bioavailability and toxicity, and risk assessment integrating lines of evidence. This structure does not appear among the eight findings summarized above.
4. **The "sensitive vs. typical" categories are explicitly proposed for removal.** The July 2026 framework, Section V.A, proposes removing the current Sensitive and Typical use categories, "replacing them so that they are linked to specific ecological receptors (e.g., critical wildlife habitats, salmon spawning areas), human uses (e.g., shellfish harvesting areas, recreational zones), or background conditions." That supports the direction taken in Section 4.1 of this paper, but the framework does not propose the named Sediment Use Categories put forward there, and this proposal is absent from the eight findings.
5. **Ingestion rates for the human food pathway.** The July 2026 framework, Section V.A, states that "Derivation of SedS-foodHH prioritizes the use of community-specific ingestion rates for traditional foods where available," and its Executive Summary likewise describes the bioaccumulation standard as "prioritizing community-specific ingestion rates for traditional foods where available." Section 6.4 of this paper instead carries the pathway on a future seafood ingestion rate assigned per exposure scenario only after primary verification of a candidate record. This is a real difference in emphasis between the two documents and bears on Section 6.4 rather than on this section.
6. **The three-part Schedule 3.4 structure is not a July 2026 framework proposal.** Section 4.0 of this paper attributes the three-part structure to the Phase 2 project plan. The July 2026 framework does not divide Schedule 3.4 into parts. It does support the underlying idea of adopting values from elsewhere where matrix derivation is not possible, describing in Section V.A a "Technical Procedure for Updating CSR Standards with Values Adopted from Other Jurisdictions" for substances handled as generic sediment standards "due to lack of available information necessary to derive matrix sediment standards or other considerations," but it does not separate those generic standards into a human health part and an ecological part.

The Phase 2 Project Plan builds directly on this foundation. It converts the conceptual framework into a set of technical workstreams intended to support development of the final Matrix Options Paper.
