<div id="sec-6-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 6.0 Proposed Matrix Standards Framework

The proposed framework is based on the concept that sediment standards should be organized around both the receptor to be protected and the exposure pathway of concern. This results in four matrix standard types:

*A note on ordering: the subsections below run **6.1 Pathway 3, 6.2 Pathway 4, 6.3 Pathway 1, 6.4 Pathway 2**. They follow the order in which the Phase 2 project plan lists the four - ecological direct, ecological food, human direct, human food (Task 6.1) - while the pathway numbers follow this paper's own scheme, in which Pathway 1 is human direct contact. Nothing turns on the difference.*

**Diagram summary 8.**

- Layout: grid
- Diagram note: title CSR SCHEDULE 3.4 PART 1 - THE FOUR RECEPTOR-PATHWAYS
- Node root: SEDIMENT CONTAMINATION (C_sed)
- Node p1: PATHWAY 1: HH-DIR
- Diagram note: head Human Direct Contact
- Criterion: Shoreline Wading
- Criterion: Incidental Ingestion
- Criterion: Dermal Absorption
- Criterion: Coupled with Health Canada Tolerable Daily Intake (TDI)
- Node p2: PATHWAY 2: HH-FOOD
- Diagram note: head Human Country Foods
- Criterion: Aquatic Food Consumption (single candidate IR_food pending primary verification)
- Criterion: Rate Selected per Scenario
- Criterion: Bioaccumulation (BSAF)
- Criterion: Coupled with TDI & cancer slope factors (sources vary - App H)
- Node p3: PATHWAY 3: ECO-DIR
- Diagram note: head Ecological Direct
- Criterion: Benthic Infauna Toxicity
- Criterion: Equilibrium Partitioning (EqP)
- Criterion: AVS-SEM Sulfides
- Criterion: Chronic Benthic NOEC
- Node p4: PATHWAY 4: ECO-FOOD
- Diagram note: head Ecological Food Web
- Criterion: Piscivorous Birds (Great Blue Heron)
- Criterion: Semi-Aquatic Mammal (River Otter, Mink)
- Criterion: Marine Carnivore (Harbour Seal)
- Relationship: root -> p1
- Relationship: root -> p2
- Relationship: root -> p3
- Relationship: root -> p4

*Table: THE PART 1 MATRIX, AS A MATRIX. Rough-draft proposal for discussion. The diagram above
shows the four receptor-pathways; this table shows what happens when they are crossed with the
Sediment Use Categories of Section 4.1, which is the object Part 1 of Schedule 3.4 would actually
contain. Each cell is one derivation scenario for one substance. **No cell is filled in here**:
which scenarios survive, and the exposure assumptions that would distinguish them, are open
decisions recorded beneath the table.*

| Receptor-pathway (rows) | AW - Aquatic Wildlands | AR/TH - Aquatic Recreational / Traditional Harvest | CA - Commercial Aquatic | IA - Industrial Aquatic |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Pathway 1 - Human health, direct contact** (Section 6.3) | P1 x AW | P1 x AR/TH | P1 x CA | P1 x IA |
| **Pathway 2 - Human health, food pathway** (Section 6.4) | P2 x AW | P2 x AR/TH | P2 x CA | P2 x IA |
| **Pathway 3 - Ecological health, direct contact** (Section 6.1) | P3 x AW | P3 x AR/TH | P3 x CA | P3 x IA |
| **Pathway 4 - Ecological health, food web** (Section 6.2) | P4 x AW | P4 x AR/TH | P4 x CA | P4 x IA |

**How to read this table, and the four decisions that determine what it becomes.**

1. **Sixteen scenarios is the starting point, not the answer.** Four pathways across four use
   categories give sixteen combinations for each priority substance. **Whether all sixteen are
   meaningful is Task 6.3**, and Section 18.1 carries it as an open question. Some combinations may
   prove implausible or wholly dominated by another and be dropped with a stated reason.
2. **Every cell is derived more than once, because waterbody class is a further axis.** The
   framework is intended to cover freshwater, estuarine and marine sediments. If estuarine stays
   grouped with marine, as the in-force schedule groups it, the sixteen become **thirty-two**; if
   estuarine is separated as a third class, **forty-eight** (Section 4.4.2). That choice is open,
   and it is a scoping decision with real consequences for Task 3.8.
3. **How the framework applies is settled in principle as a two-step rule.** Direct-contact
   pathways are mandatory, food pathways are conditional on applicable criteria and site conditions,
   and all standards that apply apply. The lowest applicable value may be used as a practical
   remediation focus, but it does not displace other applicable standards.
4. **Site assignment remains within the CSR framework.** This paper does not re-open who assigns a
   site to a use category or how overlapping use descriptions are handled.

**What sits outside this table.** Part 1 covers priority substances only. Substances that do not
warrant or support full matrix derivation are addressed by the generic standards of **Part 2**
(human health) and **Part 3** (ecological health), reached through the classification and
prioritization process of Sections 7.3 and 7.4. The three-part structure and the four
receptor-pathways are given by the Phase 2 project plan; the use categories and waterbody treatment remain options; applicability is settled in principle, and site assignment remains within the CSR framework (see *Status of the
framework's elements* in the Reviewer's Guide).

<div id="sec-6-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 6.1 Ecological Health, Direct Exposure (Pathway 3)
This standard type is intended to protect benthic and sediment-associated ecological receptors from direct exposure to contaminants in sediment. It builds on the historic focus of Schedule 3.4 but would use updated scientific methods, broader data, and bioavailability considerations where appropriate.

<div id="sec-6-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 6.2 Ecological Health, Food-Pathway Exposure (Pathway 4)
This standard type is intended to protect ecological receptors, including higher-trophic organisms and wildlife, from contaminants that bioaccumulate or biomagnify through aquatic food webs. This pathway addresses a major gap in the current Schedule 3.4 structure.

<div id="sec-6-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 6.3 Human Health, Direct Exposure (Pathway 1)
This standard type is intended to protect people from direct contact with contaminated sediment, including incidental ingestion and dermal exposure during relevant recreational, harvesting, occupational, or other use scenarios.

<div id="sec-6-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 6.4 Human Health, Food-Pathway Exposure (Pathway 2)
This standard type is intended to protect people from dietary exposure to sediment-associated contaminants in fish, shellfish, or other aquatic foods. It matters most where people eat a substantial quantity of locally caught aquatic food, and the exposure term that carries that is a future seafood ingestion rate ($IR_{food}$), assigned for each exposure scenario only after primary verification of a candidate record in Section 7.8.2. The pathway is not defined by any one diet: species-resolved diet composition, tissue-specific preparation and harvest-area apportionment are inputs to site-specific risk assessment rather than to the derivation of standards intended to be protective province-wide.

<div id="sec-6-5" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 6.5 Relationship Between Matrix and Generic Standards

Matrix standards are intended for priority substances where a refined, pathway-specific approach is warranted. Generic standards are intended for other substances where external values can be adopted or adapted through a transparent procedure.

This distinction allows the framework to focus intensive derivation resources on substances with the greatest potential risk while still expanding Schedule 3.4 coverage for other substances.

> **Applicability is a two-step rule.** Direct-contact pathways are mandatory within the framework. Food pathways are conditional on the applicable criteria and site conditions. All standards that apply to the site apply; the lowest applicable value may be used as a practical remediation focus, but it is not a hierarchy that displaces other applicable standards. Site assignment remains within the CSR framework. Protocols 18 and 21 are not sediment authority and may be used only as analogical models where identified as such.

<div id="sec-7-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 7.0 Phase 2 Research Topics Supporting the Matrix Options Paper

### 7.1 Bioavailability Adjustment

#### 7.1.1 The Thermodynamic and Geochemical Basis of Bioavailability
A fundamental limitation of British Columbia's legacy contaminated sediment standards (CSR Schedule 3.4) is that they rely upon empirical, bulk dry-weight total chemical concentrations. The numerical values now in Schedule 3.4 originate in the sediment criteria added to the *Contaminated Sites Regulation* as Schedule 9, *Generic Numerical Sediment Criteria*, by B.C. Reg. 324/2004, s. 70; Schedule 9 was repealed effective November 1, 2017 and replaced by Schedule 3.4, created by B.C. Reg. 253/2016, s. 17 (as amended by B.C. Reg. 196/2017, s. 3). The Schedule 3.4 now in force was re-enacted by B.C. Reg. 133/2022, App., s. 12, in force March 1, 2023 (*Contaminated Sites Regulation*, B.C. Reg. 375/96, as amended to B.C. Reg. 102/2026). Total concentrations do not predict biological effects because toxic substances are sequestered by distinct geochemical binding phases that mitigate chemical activity and trans-membrane uptake.

The geochemical controls set out below - organic carbon partitioning, sulfide binding, redox speciation and pH-dependent dissociation - operate in freshwater, estuarine and marine sediments alike, which is the range CSR Schedule 3.4 covers. The human-contact material in Section 7.1.4, by contrast, is written around intertidal harvesting and wading; a freshwater and lacustrine equivalent has not been drafted, and that is a current limitation of the draft rather than a scoping decision.

In the modernized framework this draft proposes for discussion, bioavailability adjustments would be evaluated across two co-equal exposure pathways:
1. **Ecological Direct Benthic Contact (Pathway 3):** Governed by the freely dissolved porewater concentration (C_free or C_pw), total organic carbon partitioning (K_oc * f_oc) for non-ionic organics, and Acid Volatile Sulfide (AVS) precipitation for divalent transition metals.
2. **Human Health Shoreline Direct Contact (Pathway 1):** Governed by incidental sediment ingestion and dermal absorption during recreational, commercial, and traditional harvesting activities, modified by oral in vitro bioaccessibility (IVBA) and dermal absorption factors (RAF_dermal).

Table: Geochemical Bioavailability Controls By Class.

**GEOCHEMICAL BIOAVAILABILITY CONTROLS BY CLASS**

| CONTAMINANT CLASS | PRIMARY GEOCHEMICAL CONTROLS | OPERATIONAL FORMULATION |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Non-Ionic Hydrophobic Organics (PAHs, PCBs, PCDD/Fs, Pestic.) | Equilibrium Partitioning (EqP) to Total Organic Carbon (f\_oc) & Black C. | C\_pw = C\_sed / (K\_oc\*f\_oc) Bounds: 0.2% <= f\_oc <= 10.0% |
| Cationic Divalent Metals (Cd, Cu, Ni, Pb, Zn, Ag) | Acid Volatile Sulfide (AVS) Binding and Secondary Organic Carbon Sorption. | Sigma[SEM] - [AVS] <= 0 Excess\_SEM\_OC < 130 |
| Ionizable Organic Compounds (Chlorophenols, Phenolics) | pH-Dependent Dissociation (pK\_a) between neutral and phenolate anions. | D\_oc = phi\_n \* K\_oc,n + (1 - phi\_n) \* K\_oc,ion |
| Metalloids (Arsenic, Selenium) & Organometallics (MeHg, TBT) | Redox Potential (Eh) and Iron/ Manganese Oxyhydroxide Sorption. | Insoluble Sulfides (AsS) vs. Soluble As(III). |
| Human Health Ingestion/Dermal (Pathway 1 Direct Exposure) | Gastrointestinal Extraction (IVBA) and Dermal Adherence / Diffusion. | RAF\_oral = IVBA\_site / IVBA\_default; RAF\_dermal |

#### 7.1.2 Non-Ionic Hydrophobic Organic Carbon Partitioning (EqP)
Under equilibrium partitioning theory (Di Toro et al. 1991; US EPA 2003, 2012), the chemical activity driving benthic macroinvertebrate toxicity is directly proportional to the freely dissolved porewater concentration:

$$C_{sed,EqP} = K_{oc} \times f_{oc} \times FCV_{water} \times 10^{-3}$$

Where:
- C_sed,EqP = Matrix Sediment Standard for ecological direct benthic contact (mg/kg-dw).
- K_oc = Substance-specific organic carbon partition coefficient (L/kg-OC).
- f_oc = Sediment total organic carbon fraction (default = 0.01 for 1.0% TOC at Tier 1, or site-measured at Tier 2).
- FCV_water = Provincial Water Quality Guideline or Final Chronic Value for aquatic life (ug/L).
- 10^-3 = unit conversion. FCV_water is tabulated in ug/L and K_oc in L/kg-OC, so the product is ug/kg; the factor converts it to the mg/kg-dw in which the standard is expressed. It is not an adjustment factor and must not be dropped.

##### Operational Boundary Rules:
- **Lower Bound (f_oc >= 0.002 or 0.2% TOC):** Below 0.2% TOC, non-specific mineral surface adsorption governs sorption; an absolute calculation floor of 0.2% TOC is proposed.
- **Upper Bound (f_oc <= 0.100 or 10.0% TOC):** Above 10.0% TOC, colloidal dissolved organic carbon (DOC) creates co-solvency effects; standards would be capped at the 10.0% TOC ceiling. This is a separate, lower band from the Black Carbon & Woodwaste Hard-Reject Threshold at 30.0% TOC (future calculation work, section 2.2.3(3)), where sediments containing raw woodwaste, bark, or charcoal deposits exceeding 30% TOC would instead require Tier 3 passive sampling / direct porewater / bioassay testing, or isolation of the non-humic carbon fraction, before a linear equation could be applied at all. The two bands do different things: the 10.0% TOC ceiling caps the standard produced by the equation, while Tier 3 testing is triggered only at the higher, woodwaste-specific 30.0% TOC threshold.

#### 7.1.3 Acid Volatile Sulfide / Simultaneously Extracted Metals (AVS-SEM) Framework
For divalent transition metals (Cd, Cu, Ni, Pb, Zn) and monovalent silver (Ag), toxicity in anoxic/suboxic sediment horizons is controlled by reaction with Acid Volatile Sulfide (AVS, solid-phase FeS) (US EPA 2005):

$$\Sigma[SEM] = [SEM_{Cd}] + [SEM_{Cu}] + [SEM_{Ni}] + [SEM_{Pb}] + [SEM_{Zn}] + 0.5 \times [SEM_{Ag}] \ \ (in\ \mu\text{mol}/g-dw)$$

##### Stoichiometric Decision Rules:
1. **Sigma[SEM] - [AVS] <= 0.0 umol/g:** All heavy metals are fully precipitated as insoluble metal sulfides (MeS). Freely dissolved porewater metal is zero. **Finding (model prediction): the sediment is predicted to be non-toxic *to benthic infauna* irrespective of bulk dry-weight metal concentrations.** The scope of that finding is the whole of it. It is a prediction of the AVS-SEM model about porewater exposure, it applies to the direct-contact pathway only, and it says nothing about dietary uptake, bioaccumulation into fish or wildlife, or human consumption. A sediment passing this rule can still fail Pathway 2 or Pathway 4, and where it does, the lowest pathway governs. Stated without that scope, the sentence reads as a finding that no metal concentration is of concern, which this paper does not hold. Appendix A records the rule in the same scoped terms.
2. **Sigma[SEM] - [AVS] > 0.0 umol/g:** Excess metal binds to sediment organic carbon:
   $$Excess_{SEM\_OC} = (\Sigma[SEM] - [AVS]) / f_{oc} \ \ (in\ \mu\text{mol}/g-OC)$$
   - **Excess_SEM_OC < 130 umol/g-OC:** Excess metals remain bound to organic carbon. Benthic toxicity is unlikely (protective standard met).
   - **130 <= Excess_SEM_OC <= 3,000 umol/g-OC:** Transition zone; triggers Tier 3 porewater analysis or bioassay testing.
   - **Excess_SEM_OC > 3,000 umol/g-OC:** Binding capacities are saturated; chronic/acute benthic toxicity is likely.

#### 7.1.4 Human Health Bioaccessibility Modifiers
For Pathway 1 (Direct Shoreline Ingestion and Dermal Contact), site-specific bioavailability would be adjusted using standardized in vitro oral bioaccessibility (IVBA) and dermal relative absorption factors (RAF_dermal). **[Method reference to be confirmed: the source formerly cited here for a prescriptive B.C. IVBA method ("B.C. ENV 2021 prescriptive methods") could not be verified and has been removed. A verified method reference is required before publication.]**
- **Arsenic & Lead:** Oral intake adjusted by site-specific IVBA (RAF_oral = IVBA_site / IVBA_default).
- **Dermal Exposure:** Dermal absorption adjusted by substance-specific RAF_dermal values (e.g., PAHs = 0.148, Arsenic = 0.03, Cadmium = 0.01), **taken from Health Canada TRV v4.0 (2025), Table 5.** The source-selection decision has been made: *Protocol 1* v5.0 section 4.3.1.1 requires that the relative absorption factors referenced in Health Canada (2024) be used, and Appendix H section 3.5 sets out the referral chain and the reason of quantity that supports it. **The PCB value is 0.14, not 0.03.** RAGS Part E Exhibit 3-4 gives 0.03 for *"TCDD and other dioxins"* and **0.14** for *"Aroclors 1254/1242 and other PCBs"*; 0.03 is the dioxin figure, carried onto PCBs. The correct figure is not simply substituted here because the printed dermal intakes downstream were computed on the withdrawn value and have not been recomputed - see the note at Appendix A section 6.2.

*Full thermodynamic derivations, mathematical models, analytical laboratory test requirements, and the supporting scientific bibliography are compiled in **Appendix A: Bioavailability Adjustment Research Report**, which is itself a draft research compilation that has not been reviewed or endorsed by any body.*

### 7.3 Substance Classification

> **[STATUS: QUARANTINED - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]**

The former substance-classification threshold table and category assignments are removed from the active paper surface. The displaced material is preserved in the V10 before-edit snapshot and is not relied on for any decision. No log Kow, BCF, BAF, BSAF, trophic-factor, persistence, toxicity, cancer-slope-factor, or category threshold is operative in this successor. Any future classification work requires exact primary-source verification, a documented scope and unit check, and independent review before reuse.

The paper retains only the project-level distinction between priority-substance matrix derivation and generic standards for other substances. It does not classify a substance, allocate a substance to a schedule part, or infer a regulatory consequence.

---

### 7.4 Substance Prioritization

Substance prioritization is a future work-sequencing method, not a standards derivation and not a current
regulatory allocation. The project plan identifies biomagnification, toxicity, persistence, and local
occurrence as relevant criteria. The BC Aquatic Database and the supporting evidence compilation do not
yet exist in a form that supports scoring or ranking.

This draft therefore presents unscored options only. A future method must identify the source, location,
units, applicability, and uncertainty for every input; distinguish threat priority from feasibility and
resource sequencing; test the arithmetic independently; and report sensitivity to weights and missing data.
No substance is scored, ranked, or assigned to a tranche in this Phase 2 draft.

The adoption route for substances not entering a future Part 1 matrix derivation is expressed as a
Classification Category 2 question in Appendix F. It is not derived from a suspended tranche apparatus.

### 7.6 Generic Standards Adoption Procedure

Under the three-part **CSR Schedule 3.4** structure set out in the project plan, substances that are not prioritized for full mechanistic matrix derivations under Part 1 would be addressed through **generic numerical sediment standards** in:
* **Schedule 3.4 Part 2:** Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to Protect Human Health.
* **Schedule 3.4 Part 3:** Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to Protect Ecological Health (Freshwater and Marine).

**Applicability.** CSR Schedule 3.4 today sets standards in four columns - freshwater sediment and marine and estuarine sediment, each for sensitive and typical sediment use. Parts 2 and 3 as drafted keep that separation (Section 7.6.3 carries distinct freshwater and marine columns), so the adoption procedure below is built for freshwater, estuarine and marine environments. The Part 1 material in Sections 7.5 and 7.8 has not yet been worked up on the same basis; that gap is stated there.

This architecture is intended to give non-prioritized contaminants (e.g., volatile organic compounds, chlorinated solvents, low-molecular-weight hydrocarbons, and specialty industrial chemicals) protective numerical thresholds for site investigation and remediation without requiring multi-year B.C.-specific modeling investments. Whether any such threshold is adopted, and what legal effect it would carry, is a matter for ENV's own policy, consultation and approval processes.

**Diagram summary 9.**

- Layout: flow
- Node s1: SCHEDULE 3.4 PART 1: MATRIX SEDIMENT STANDARDS (APPENDICES B, C, D, E)
- Criterion: Applied to PRIORITIZED substances (Tranches 1 & 2: PCBs, Dioxins, MeHg, PAHs, PFAS, Metals).
- Criterion: Full B.C.-specific mechanistic derivation across 4 discrete pathways & 4 Sediment Uses.
- Criterion: Explicitly models high-intake aquatic food consumption and food chains.
- Node s2: SCHEDULE 3.4 PARTS 2 & 3: GENERIC SEDIMENT STANDARDS (APPENDIX F)
- Criterion: Applied to NON-PRIORITIZED substances (Volatiles, Solvents, Low-MW Organics, Minor Inorganics).
- Criterion: Adopted/adapted from leading peer jurisdictions using a formalized 6-step screening ladder.
- Criterion: Published as single generic thresholds for Human Health (Part 2) and Ecological Life (Part 3).
- Relationship: s1 -> s2

#### 7.6.2 Step-by-Step Generic Adoption Protocol

1. **Step 1 (Scope Verification):** Confirm that the candidate substance is classified as Classification Category 2 and lacks a Part 1 matrix standard.
2. **Step 2 (Jurisdictional Search):** Search peer sources in descending hierarchical order (Level 1  ->  Level 2  ->  Level 3  ->  Level 4).
3. **Step 3 (Scientific Quality Screen):** Verify that the benchmark possesses a transparent toxicological derivation document, protects chronic biological endpoints (with 90% to 95% species protection for ecological life), and maintains clear freshwater vs. marine distinction.
4. **Step 4 (Mathematical Harmonization and Recalculation onto B.C. Assumptions):** Convert units to standard mg/kg-dry weight and normalize non-polar organic benchmarks to the 1.0% TOC baseline proposed by this draft ($f_{oc}$ = 0.01 - **a proposal of this paper, not a Protocol 28 default**). **Where the peer value embeds exposure assumptions, recalculate it onto B.C. assumptions rather than adopting the source jurisdiction's** - human-health benchmarks do embed them (body weight, consumption rate, exposure frequency), ecological direct-contact benchmarks generally do not. Adopting a peer value means recalculating it onto B.C. assumptions, not writing a new equation. The draft B.C. values are at Section 7.8, and the seafood consumption rate is the option set at Section 9.5.4:
   $$C_{sed, dry weight} = C_{OC}  *  0.01$$
5. **Step 5 (Regional Background & Feasibility Check):** Verify that the candidate benchmark is above regional ambient geochemical background and detectable by commercial B.C. laboratories. **There is at present no B.C. protocol for establishing background concentrations in sediment: Protocol 4 (Version 15, effective June 19, 2026) is *Establishing Local Background Concentrations in Soil* and applies to soil only, and Protocol 9 (Version 5) covers groundwater. A sediment equivalent would have to be developed; this is one of the gaps the project proposes to fill.**
6. **Step 6 (Documentation, Review & Publication):** Record complete derivation metadata, submit the verification rationale for review, and assign the standard to Schedule 3.4 Part 2 (Human Health) or Part 3 (Ecological Health).

> **This procedure is stated in three places and they agree.** Appendix F section 4.0 renders the same six steps as a figure, and Section 9.6 states them in the same order. Two elements are worth noting: **the recalculation onto B.C. exposure assumptions** at step 4, which is broader than unit and organic-carbon harmonisation alone and is closer to the procedure's stated purpose; and **the review step** at step 6. Adoption of any standard remains out of scope for Phase 2.

---

**A note on how the schedule must be read.** Extract it with `pdftotext -table`. The default layout mode misaligns this table by four rows, so each substance is printed against another substance's CAS number and standards - benz(a)anthracene appears with acenaphthene's figures. ### 7.7 BC Aquatic Database

A foundational scientific cornerstone for the modernization of CSR Schedule 3.4 will be the BC Aquatic Database (also referred to as the BC Sediment Database). 

**The database does not yet exist.** Under the project plan it is to be developed by the Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV) from documents submitted to the Site Remediation Program and potentially shared data sets, in an undertaking in which the partnership with the SABCS will play a key role. It would be a provincial repository of standardized empirical environmental data drawn from Detailed Risk Assessments (DRAs), Human Health and Ecological Risk Assessments (HHERAs), and receiving environment monitoring programs submitted under the *Environmental Management Act* across British Columbia. Development of the database and its source inventory is scheduled for month 7 of the plan, with QA/QC and data usability screening in the same period: **no data have yet been compiled, screened or verified.** Phase 2 is scoped to deliver a functional, QA/QC-screened database; a complete sediment database is expressly out of scope.

**Proposed structure, for TWG discussion-the database does not yet exist and none of the pillars below has been built.**

#### 7.7.1 Regulatory Mandate and Core Purpose

Once developed, the BC Aquatic Database would support work on modernized Schedule 3.4 standards across seven intended functions:

1. **Occurrence and Prevalence Analysis:** Quantifying real-world concentration distributions and co-occurrence patterns of toxic substances across marine, estuarine, and freshwater sediments throughout British Columbia. This empirical characterization would help ensure that any future numerical standards focus on primary risk drivers and reflect realistic multi-contaminant mixtures.
2. **Natural and Regional Background Baselines:** Establishing reference sediment chemistry distributions across B.C. biogeoclimatic regions to support regional background determinations for sediment. **Protocol 4 for Contaminated Sites (Version 15, effective June 19, 2026) is titled *Establishing Local Background Concentrations in Soil* and applies to soil only; Protocol 9 (Version 5) is the groundwater equivalent. There is no B.C. protocol for establishing background concentrations in sediment-a gap this project proposes to fill, not an existing authority.** Reference distributions of this kind would help ensure that any future matrix sediment standards are not set below ambient geochemical background levels.
3. **Policy-Ready Matrix Testing:** Providing paired empirical datasets (co-located sediment chemistry, total organic carbon [$f_{oc}$], acid volatile sulfide [$AVS$], simultaneously extracted metals [$SEM$], grain size distributions, laboratory toxicity bioassays, and benthic community metrics) that could later be used to test and calibrate the candidate matrix derivation equations discussed in Section 7.5 across the proposed Sediment Use Categories. **No equation has been tested, calibrated or validated against any dataset.**
4. **Triad & Risk-Based Synthesis:** Systematically compiling sediment quality triad lines of evidence (chemistry, laboratory bioassays, and in-situ benthic surveys) from historical risk assessments - **submitted under whichever protocol governs them, which this draft has not established** **[Protocol 13 (*Screening Level Risk Assessment*): content not characterized here; protocol not held; no claim about its provisions is made.] - which would establish empirical benchmarks for weight-of-evidence (WOE) risk characterization.

5. **Exceedance Screening Against the Standards in Force:** Identifying which substances are found in British Columbia sediments at concentrations above the current Schedule 3.4 standards, or above external screening values where Schedule 3.4 carries none. This is the empirical counterpart to the substance prioritization exercise at Section 7.4: prioritization scores a substance on its properties, while this would show where it is actually exceeding a standard in the province.
6. **Identifying Where the Generic Standards Are Not Doing the Work:** Recording which substances commonly drive a site to site-specific risk assessment rather than being resolved against a generic numerical standard. A substance that routinely requires site-specific assessment is a candidate for a matrix standard, since that is the pattern a matrix framework exists to address.
7. **Evaluating the Effect of Bioavailability Adjustment:** Assessing where bioavailability adjustments of the kind discussed at Section 7.1 have changed a risk management outcome in practice, as distinct from changing a calculated number. **No such evaluation has been carried out**, and the database is what would make one possible.

**The database is not expected to be complete within Phase 2.** What the project plan requires of it is that it be developed far enough to support the final Matrix Options Paper and the standards work that follows, not that it be finished. Sections 15.2 and 15.6 record what cannot be claimed until it exists: no occurrence claim in this paper has been tested against British Columbia data, and there is no provincial protocol for sediment background concentrations for it to populate.

---

#### 7.7.2 Core Environmental Data Structure

The proposed schema would integrate five environmental data categories within a unified relational structure, indexed to physical sampling locations and collection intervals. The structure below is a draft for review under the project plan's database-structure task (month 3):

**Diagram summary 12.**

- Layout: tree
- Diagram note: title ENVIRONMENTAL DATA COMPILATION MODEL
- Node sites: [SITES & WATER LOTS] (Site ID, EM Area, Waterbody Name, Waterbody Type: Marine/Estuarine/Fresh)
- Node stations: [SAMPLING STATIONS] (Station ID, Reference Status, Coordinates: Lat/Long, Precision)
- Node events: [SAMPLING EVENTS] (Event ID, Sample ID, Media Type, Date, Sampling Depth)
- Criterion: (Content-derived immutable sample identifier; attributes pending)
- Node mod: [SEDIMENT MODIFIERS]
- Criterion: Total Organic Carbon (TOC wt %, f_oc)
- Criterion: Acid Volatile Sulfide (AVS umol/g)
- Criterion: Simultaneously Extracted Metals (SEM umol/g)
- Criterion: [SEM - AVS] / f_oc
- Criterion: Grain Size Fractions (Gravel, Sand, Fines)
- Node chem: [SEDIMENT CHEMISTRY]
- Criterion: Heavy Metals
- Criterion: Polycyclic Aromatic Hydrocarbons (PAHs)
- Criterion: Polychlorinated Biphenyls (PCBs)
- Criterion: Dioxins & Furans
- Criterion: PFAS Compounds
- Criterion: Standard Units (mg/kg)
- Criterion: Qualifier Codes (<, ND, J, Estimated)
- Node bio: [SEDIMENT BIOASSAYS]
- Criterion: Test Species
- Criterion: Exposure Endpoints (Survival, Growth)
- Criterion: Percent of Control
- Criterion: Toxicity Category (Non-toxic, Toxic)
- Node benthic: [BENTHIC COMMUNITY]
- Criterion: Total Abundance (/m2)
- Criterion: Taxa Richness
- Criterion: Diversity Indices (Shannon H', Simpson)
- Criterion: Benthic Quality Score
- Criterion: Community Condition (Stressed, Reference)
- Node prov: [SOURCE PROVENANCE] (Source Document, Report Page, Table ID, Sample Coordinate Verification)
- Relationship: sites -> stations
- Relationship: stations -> events
- Relationship: events -> mod
- Relationship: events -> chem
- Relationship: events -> bio
- Relationship: events -> benthic
- Relationship: benthic -> prov

1. **Sediment Analytical Chemistry:** Bulk concentrations ($mg/kg_{dw}$) and porewater fractions for metals, PAHs, PCBs, PCDD/Fs, PFAS, and volatile organic compounds.
2. **Geochemical & Bioavailability Modifiers:** Total Organic Carbon ($f_{oc}$), Acid Volatile Sulfide ($AVS$), Simultaneously Extracted Metals ($SEM$), $[\Sigma SEM - AVS] / f_{oc}$, grain size fractions (gravel, sand, silt, clay), sediment moisture, and porewater pH.
3. **Laboratory Toxicity Bioassays:** Standardized acute and chronic biological endpoints (10-day and 28-day survival and growth for *Hyalella azteca*, *Eohaustorius estuarius*, *Chironomus dilutus*, and *Ampelisca abdita*).
4. **Benthic Community Metrics:** In-situ infaunal ecological surveys including total abundance, taxa richness, Shannon-Weiner diversity ($H'$), and Benthic Quality Index scores.
5. **Biota Tissue Chemistry:** Tissue concentrations in co-located benthic invertebrates (clams, crabs) and forage fish to calculate empirical Biota-Sediment Accumulation Factors ($BSAFs$).

---

#### 7.7.3 Proposed Data Verification and Validation Process

To ensure scientific defensibility, all environmental data would pass through the following proposed four-stage verification and validation process before analytical use. **QA/QC and data usability screening is scheduled for month 7; no data have yet passed through any stage of it:**

1. **Structural Conformance & Referential Integrity:** Validating parent-child relationships across sites, stations, and sampling events, asserting physical parameter boundaries (e.g., pH 2-12, TOC 0-100%, bioassay survival 0-100%).
2. **Chemical Nomenclature & Unit Harmonization:** Resolving laboratory synonyms to canonical substance identifiers and CAS Registry numbers via the Master Substance Registry; standardizing concentrations to $mg/kg$ dry weight.
3. **Sample Identity and Co-Location Verification:** Assigning each physical sample a content-derived, immutable identifier, so that duplicate reporting can be detected and co-located samples recognised. **The attributes that would compose that identifier have not been settled: no such scheme has been designed, specified or built.**
4. **Censored Data Standardization:** Managing laboratory non-detects ($< MDL$) using non-parametric Kaplan-Meier estimation or Robust Regression on Order Statistics (ROS) for distribution modeling.

---

#### 7.7.4 Intended Future Use of the Database in Testing Candidate Derivation Equations

Once developed and QA/QC screened, the BC Aquatic Database is intended to provide the empirical basis for testing the candidate derivation equations discussed in Section 7.5. **Nothing in Section 7.5 has been validated against any dataset, no such dataset yet exists, and no Part 1 algorithm has been established:**

* **Pathway 1 (Human Direct Contact):** Empirical grain-size distributions and moisture content from B.C. recreational and harvesting shorelines would allow the assumed intertidal sediment adherence factors ($AF_{sed} = 0.2 \text{ to } 0.3 \text{ mg/cm}^2$) to be tested.
* **Pathway 2 (Traditional Country Foods):** Co-located sediment and bivalve/crab tissue records would allow empirical Biota-Sediment Accumulation Factors ($BSAFs$) to be calculated for marine species, and seafood ingestion models would be calibrated against a future scenario-specific seafood ingestion rate assigned only after exact primary-source verification of a candidate record (candidate records are described in Section 7.8.2).
* **Pathway 3 (Ecological Direct Contact):** Equilibrium Partitioning ($EqP$) porewater algorithms and the two-stage acid-volatile-sulfide test - $\Sigma[SEM] - [AVS] \le 0$ as the non-toxic criterion, and, for any excess, its organic-carbon-normalised bands at 130 and 3,000 $\mu$mol/g-OC (Section 7.1.3; Appendix H section 7.4) - could be evaluated against 10-day amphipod (*Hyalella azteca*, *Eohaustorius estuarius*) bioassay survival records.
* **Pathway 4 (Food Chain Bioaccumulation):** Empirical Biomagnification Factors ($BMFs$) for methylmercury, PCBs, and dioxins/furans could be compared with co-located sediment-to-prey monitoring records, to test whether piscivorous wildlife (Great Blue Heron, River Otter, Harbour Seal) would be protected.

---

#### 7.7.5 Integration with Contaminated Sites Protocols

The BC Aquatic Database would interface with British Columbia's core administrative and technical protocols. Amending any protocol is out of scope for Phase 2; each point below describes a relationship, not a change:
* **Protocol 1 (*Detailed Risk Assessment*):** Version 4.0 (effective March 1, 2023) is the version in force today; Version 5.0 was signed April 13, 2026 and takes effect January 15, 2027. Detailed risk assessments submitted under Protocol 1 would be a principal source of the records the database would compile. Standardized electronic data deliverables would be one option for maintaining it, but any change to submission or reporting requirements would be a separate ENV decision outside Phase 2 scope.
* **Protocol 4 (*Establishing Local Background Concentrations in Soil*, Version 15):** Applies to soil only; Protocol 9 (Version 5) is the groundwater equivalent. **There is no B.C. protocol for establishing background concentrations in sediment.** The database could supply the empirical reference distributions (for example regional geochemical background percentiles, $P_{95}$) that a sediment equivalent would need-a gap this project proposes to fill. **The Phase 2 project plan makes explicit alignment with the statistical and spatial approaches of Protocols 4 and 9 a success criterion for this work. Those approaches-how a background dataset is assembled and tested, and how a local background area is delineated-have not yet been reviewed for transferability to sediment. That review is outstanding, and no method is proposed here. Which statistic a sediment procedure should use (a 95th percentile, an upper tolerance limit, or an upper confidence limit of the mean) is itself an open option, not a settled method.**
* **Protocol 11 (*Upper Cap Concentrations for Substances Listed in the Contaminated Sites Regulation*, Version 6.0, effective November 22, 2024):** Its Table 8 sets upper cap concentrations for Schedule 3.4 substances in four columns - freshwater sediment and marine and estuarine sediment, each for sensitive and typical sediment use. The database could supply the empirical maximum-concentration distributions relevant to those thresholds. (Title, version and effective date verified against the signed protocol.)
* **Protocol 13 (*Screening Level Risk Assessment*): content not characterized here; protocol not held; no claim about its provisions is made.**

---

# Policy-ready input categories - Phase 2 boundary

**Status:** `OPTION_OPEN` / `SOURCE_GAP`. This section names evidence
categories only. No numeric default, receptor assignment, equation, or derived
result is active.

Future records must identify the source and exact location, units, medium,
receptor, applicability, uncertainty, and independent test required before
use. Missing or incompatible records are `SOURCE_GAP` or `WITHDRAW`.

Categories include human contact, food exposure, ecological exposure, sediment
properties, tissue normalization, partitioning, toxicity benchmarks, food-web
position, site linkage, analytical feasibility, and uncertainty.

## 8.0 Evaluation Criteria

The options considered in this paper should be evaluated using a consistent set of criteria. These criteria apply to technical and policy-design options within the framework, not to the question of whether to proceed with modernization.

The same criteria are intended to apply at every layer of choice set out in Section 9.0 - sediment uses; receptors and exposure pathways; exposure assumptions and receptor characteristics; selection among the available input parameters; and equation form - so that options arising at different layers can be compared on a common basis.

<div id="sec-8-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 8.1 Scientific Defensibility
The approach should reflect current sediment science, toxicology, bioavailability, bioaccumulation, food-web transfer, exposure assessment, and data quality expectations.

<div id="sec-8-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 8.2 Protectiveness
The approach should protect ecological and human receptors across relevant direct exposure and food-pathway exposure routes.

<div id="sec-8-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 8.3 Feasibility
The approach should be implementable using available data, analytical methods, technical expertise, laboratory capacity, and regulatory resources.

<div id="sec-8-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 8.4 BC Representativeness
The approach should be relevant to BC sediment conditions, aquatic settings, species, geochemistry, contaminant occurrence patterns, and site management needs.

<div id="sec-8-5" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 8.5 Transparency
The approach should be understandable, reproducible, and clearly documented so that regulators, practitioners, Indigenous Nations, and affected parties can understand the rationale.

<div id="sec-8-6" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 8.6 Indigenous Use and Rights Considerations
The approach should be capable of considering Indigenous food-based exposure pathways, culturally important receptors, and Indigenous Knowledge where appropriate and supported through respectful engagement.

<div id="sec-8-7" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 8.7 Regulatory Alignment & Protocol Harmonization Architecture
The approach should be capable of fitting within a future Schedule 3.4 structure and supporting ENV's future policy and legal review processes. 

Any future matrix framework would need to sit alongside the director's protocols issued under section 64 of the *Environmental Management Act*. The protocols most directly engaged are set out below with their current titles. Several of them do not presently extend to sediment, which is itself part of the gap this project is examining.

> **[STATUS: QUARANTINED - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]** Numeric screening thresholds and bioaccumulation references in the protocol summary below are quoted context only. They are not adopted classification thresholds or operative derivation inputs in this successor.

* **Protocol 1 (*Detailed Risk Assessment*):** Version 4.0 (effective March 1, 2023) is the version currently in force; Version 5.0 was approved April 13, 2026 and takes effect January 15, 2027. Relevant content includes bioaccumulation screening thresholds (`log Kow >= 4.5`, `BCF >= 2,000`, `BAF >= 2,000`), food-chain modeling requirements, and sediment porewater definitions.
* **Protocol 2 (*Site-Specific Numerical Soil Standards*):** Protocol 2 applies to soil only. There is at present **no** director's protocol establishing a site-specific numerical standard procedure for sediment. Adjusting default matrix standards using site-specific organic carbon (`f_oc`), localized tissue BSAF measurements, or species-specific bioenergetic data would therefore require a new or amended protocol. Protocol work is outside the scope of Phase 2.
* **Protocol 13 (*Screening Level Risk Assessment*):** content not characterized here; protocol not held; no claim about its provisions is made.
* **Protocol 28 (*2016 Standards Derivation Methods*, Version 3.0, effective May 1, 2024):** Chapter 7 is the whole of its sediment derivation content: generic sediment standards are a CCME 1999 probable effect level multiplied by a probability factor (P20 = 0.62 freshwater; P50 = 1.2 freshwater and marine). Protocol 28 contains no organic carbon normalization, no sediment `f_oc`, and no AVS/SEM provisions, and it states that sediment standards are not considered protective of human health. It does not establish field collection procedures for TOC, AVS-SEM, black carbon, or co-located tissue sampling.
* **Note on background concentrations.** Protocol 4 (*Establishing Local Background Concentrations in Soil*, Version 15, effective June 19, 2026) is soil only, and Protocol 9 (*Establishing Local Background Concentrations in Groundwater*, Version 5) is groundwater only. **There is no B.C. protocol for sediment background concentrations.** Any sediment background screening described in this paper is a proposal by analogy, not existing authority.

<div id="sec-8-8" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 8.8 Scalability & Five-Year Review Cycle
The approach should allow new substances, methods, and data to be incorporated over time without redesigning the entire framework.

To reduce the risk of regulatory obsolescence, a five-year review cycle is recommended for consideration for any future framework. No such cycle has been adopted, and supporting technical guidance and protocols are outside the scope of Phase 2. Such a cycle could include:
1. Annual review of emerging contaminants on the Category 3 Watch-List (e.g., 6PPD-quinone, microplastics, novel PFAS).
2. Five-year re-evaluation of MCDA prioritization scores as new B.C. contaminated site monitoring data and Health Canada TRVs become available.
3. Regular updates to peer-jurisdiction generic standards in Schedule 3.4 Parts 2 & 3.

<div id="sec-8-9" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 8.9 Resource Efficiency
The approach should make effective use of documented evidence, SABCS support, SSTAC expertise, and appropriately scoped technical review.

---

<div id="sec-9-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 9.0 Technical Options Analysis

> **Editor's note - this heading's body is assembled at build time.** Everything between this heading and section 9.7 is replaced by `build_master_v14.py` with text read from `SECTION_9_0_OPTIONS_ANALYSIS_INTRO_DRAFT_TEXT.md` and `SECTION_9_1` to `SECTION_9_6`. **Prose written here does not reach any artifact.** Edit the source files instead. Section 9.7 onwards, including section 9.8, is live in this template.

<div id="sec-9-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.1 Bioavailability Adjustment Options


<div id="sec-9-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.2 Bioaccumulation Options


<div id="sec-9-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.3 Substance Classification Options

*This subsection is not yet drafted. Classification options are being developed through the Phase 2 bioaccumulation and substance classification workstream, and a synthesis of the options considered and the emerging preferred approach will be added here as that work is completed. Supporting material is in Appendix C.*

<div id="sec-9-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.4 Substance Prioritization Options

* **Option A: Hazard-First Prioritization:** This option would prioritize substances with the greatest toxicity, persistence, or bioaccumulation potential. It is protective but may not reflect BC occurrence or feasibility.
* **Option B: Occurrence-First Prioritization:** This option would prioritize substances most frequently detected or most often exceeding existing values in BC sediments. It is practical but may understate substances of high concern with incomplete occurrence data.
* **Option C: Hazard and Occurrence Matrix:** This option would prioritize substances based on the intersection of hazard and BC occurrence. It provides a more balanced approach than either factor alone.
* **Option D: Integrated Prioritization Framework:** This option would combine hazard, occurrence, feasibility, urgency, resource requirements, and availability of external values. It provides the most complete basis for determining matrix versus generic pathways.
* **Preliminary Direction:** The preliminary direction is to develop an integrated prioritization framework that includes hazard, occurrence, feasibility, urgency, and resource requirements (**Option D**). This aligns directly with the task structure set out in the Phase 2 project plan.

<div id="sec-9-5" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.5 Matrix Standards Derivation Options


<div id="sec-9-6" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.6 Generic Standards Adoption Options

* **Option A: Direct Adoption:** This option would adopt external standards without significant adjustment. It is efficient but may not account for differences in derivation methods, protection goals, species, sediment conditions, or regulatory context.
* **Option B: Adoption with Screening Review:** This option would adopt external values after a screening-level review of source, protection goal, and apparent relevance. It is more defensible than direct adoption but may still lack sufficient documentation.
* **Option C: Structured Adoption Procedure:** This option would establish a formal procedure for evaluating external values based on source jurisdiction, derivation method, protection goal, pathway, uncertainty, analytical feasibility, BC relevance, and documentation requirements.
* **Preliminary Direction:** The preliminary direction is to develop a structured adoption procedure for generic standards (**Option C**). This is necessary to support Parts 2 and 3 of the proposed Schedule 3.4 structure.

<div id="sec-9-7" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.7 Why the alternative Schedule 3.4 architectures were not pursued

> **This section does not choose the structure of Schedule 3.4.** The structure is given
> by the Phase 2 project plan, in the five places set out at Section 4.0, and nothing in
> the plan frames it as selectable. What follows sets out why the alternative
> architectures were not pursued, as context rather than as a selection.

What belongs here instead is narrower and more useful: a reader who knows the sediment
file will have their own view of how Schedule 3.4 should be shaped, and is entitled to
know why the plan's three-part structure was directed rather than one of the obvious
alternatives. That is context for a given, not a selection between candidates, and it
is offered as such. **Nothing below is a score, a ranking or a recommendation**, and
where this paper does not know something it says so rather than assigning it a grade.

**A single consolidated schedule.** The simplest alternative is one undifferentiated
table of sediment standards, which is close to what British Columbia has now. Its
attraction is real and should not be understated: it is the cheapest to produce, the
simplest to administer, and it asks the least of laboratories and practitioners. What
it cannot do is carry a derivation basis that differs between substances - a value
derived for benthic direct contact and a value derived for human dietary exposure sit
in the same column with nothing to distinguish them. It is worth being precise about
why: consolidation does not *by itself* preclude addressing bioaccumulation or
food-web transfer. A single
schedule could in principle carry bioaccumulation-based values. What it cannot do is
show which basis produced which number, or let the two be engaged separately by the
regulation - and that legibility is most of what the three-part structure buys.

**Matrix standards only.** This is the technically strongest of the alternatives, and
it should be said plainly rather than scored away: on the protection goals that
motivate this project, a schedule composed entirely of pathway-specific matrix
standards would do everything the three-part structure does for the substances it
covers. Its difficulty is coverage, not science. A matrix derivation requires
substance-specific toxicity, partitioning and bioaccumulation data, and a reviewed
parameter set for every receptor-pathway; the number of substances that can carry one
within any realistic Phase 2 or Phase 3 horizon is far smaller than the number
Schedule 3.4 must address. Everything not covered would keep its current value or none
at all. **This paper does not yet state how many substances could realistically carry
a matrix derivation, and that number is the whole argument for Parts 2 and 3.** It is
recorded at Section 10.0.1 as an open item for the project lead rather than estimated
here.

*A correction worth stating explicitly, because the earlier matrix had it backwards.*
That draft awarded "fully aligns with CSR Schedule 3.1" to the matrix-only option.
**Schedule 3.1 is itself drafted in three parts** - Part 1 per-substance matrices, Part
2 generic standards protecting human health, Part 3 generic standards protecting
ecological health (Section 4.3.1). A sediment schedule of matrices alone would align
with Schedule 3.1's Part 1 and with nothing else in it. The in-force soil precedent
supports the three-part shape, not the matrix-only one.

**Generic standards only.** Adopting values from other jurisdictions across the board
is fast and cheap, and for many substances it is what Parts 2 and 3 will in fact do.
As a whole-schedule answer it forecloses the pathway differentiation the project exists
to introduce: an adopted generic value carries the protection goal, receptor basis and
exposure assumptions of the jurisdiction that derived it, and those are rarely British
Columbia's - marine and estuarine conditions, and dietary exposure at the consumption
rates seen on this coast, are the usual points of departure. Section 9.6 sets out the
adoption procedure and the six-step verification that is meant to test exactly this
before any value is carried across.

**What the in-force Schedule 3.4 does better.** The existing schedule is the baseline
this work is measured against, and it is not a candidate to be scored - Section 8.0
confines its criteria to options within the framework, which is consistent with the
plan, where whether to modernize is settled and out of scope. But declining to re-open
the question is not a reason to be silent about what the baseline does well. It
requires one bulk dry-weight measurement per substance: no organic carbon
normalisation, no AVS-SEM determination, no tissue work, no receptor-pathway selection
and no sediment use assignment. Every one of those is a cost the modernized framework
adds, and several of them are costs this paper cannot yet quantify. **This paper holds
no British Columbia data on per-site analytical cost, laboratory capacity, turnaround
time, or how often sediment sites currently reach a risk-based standard, and no
statement here about cost or implementation burden should be read as an estimate the
project stands behind.** That gap is a limitation of this draft, not a finding about
the alternatives.

What the in-force schedule does not do is address human health. Protocol 28's Chapter
7, which is the entire published derivation basis for the current standards, says so in
its own words: *"Sediment standards are derived based on exposure scenarios for
ecological organisms and are not considered protective of human health; therefore, CSR
soil standards should be applied to sediment for risk assessment purposes."* That is
the one limitation Chapter 7 states.

One qualification belongs with that, and it comes from elsewhere in the same protocol. Chapter 7 is the whole of Protocol 28's sediment *derivation* content, but not the whole of its sediment content: the vapour chapter states that generic numerical vapour standards "are specific to human health", and then applies them by sediment use - agricultural, urban park and residential vapour standards apply "as well as freshwater or marine sediment at sensitive sediment use sites", and industrial vapour standards "as well as to freshwater or marine sediment at typical sediment use sites". **A human-health standard therefore already operates at sediment sites under the existing scheme, keyed to the same sensitive and typical split this paper's Part 1 uses - for the vapour pathway alone.** It does not qualify Chapter 7's statement about the sediment standards themselves, which stands as quoted. It does mean the gap this project addresses is the absence of human-health protection through the sediment exposure pathways, not its absence from the regulation at sediment sites altogether. The further limitations this project is concerned with - that the standards
address neither bioaccumulation nor food-web transfer - follow from the direct-contact
derivation basis and are argued in this paper at Sections 2.2 and 7.2; they are not
statements Protocol 28 makes, and are not attributed to it here.

**A note on what a three-part structure can carry.** Because Part 1 admits
receptor-pathway rows, it can carry a dietary exposure scenario at a stated consumption
rate as a substitutable input - for example a candidate adult subsistence-fisher parameter from B.C. WLRS (2023), Table 2, which is not accepted pending primary-source verification. The source itself qualifies the parameter,
in Canada after Richardson (1997), and *"is not specific to any Indigenous peoples fish
consumption habit"*. The qualification travels with the rate wherever this paper uses
it. That a structure *can* carry such a scenario is a statement about the structure. It
is not a statement that any rate has been selected, and none has - the option set is at
Section 7.8.2 and the selection is a Phase 2 decision that has not been taken.

**What is genuinely open, and where it is decided.** The plan fixes that Schedule 3.4
has three parts. It does not fix how Part 1 is dimensioned - how many sediment use
categories, which receptors, how the four pathway values combine into an applicable
standard - nor the procedure by which values enter Parts 2 and 3. Those are Tasks 6.2,
7.1 and 7.2, they are the subject of Sections 9.0 through 9.6 and Section 18.1, and
they are where a reviewer's structural comments will actually change something.

---

<div id="sec-9-8" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 9.8 Pathway 4 options for province-wide applicability

> **Status: rough draft.** This section presents options for TWG evaluation. **The options are not ranked, no option is recommended, and no numeric standard is derived here** - consistent with plan section 2.2, which places final numeric standards out of scope for Phase 2, and Task 6.1, which asks for options for equations rather than values. Nothing in this section has been reviewed, endorsed or adopted by anyone.


---


<div id="sec-9-8-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.8.1 The parameter catalog

Pathway 4 (sediment -> benthic/aquatic prey tissue -> wildlife diet) requires three input types per receptor: dietary composition (fraction of diet by prey type), food ingestion rate, and body weight. Two catalogs were checked directly; a third (Canadian) is identified structurally but its receptor-characteristic content could not be retrieved for quotation in this draft (source blocked - noted below, not filled in).

### (a) US EPA Wildlife Exposure Factors Handbook (EPA/600/R-93/187, Vol. I, Dec. 1993)
Published by EPA Office of Research and Development. Presents species-specific profiles (birds, mammals; reptiles/amphibians more sparsely) covering body weight, food/water ingestion rates, dietary composition, population dynamics, and seasonal activity patterns, compiled from the field literature for each species, not derived by EPA. Values are given as ranges/means with location and season noted per literature source, not as single province-wide defaults.

The Handbook is explicit that these are literature compilations subject to real variability, not fixed constants:

> "Dietary composition varies seasonally and by age, size, reproductive status, and habitat." (Sec. 1.4.2.1.2, Dietary Composition)

> "Food ingestion rates can vary by age, size, and sex and by seasonal changes in ambient temperature, activity levels, reproductive activities, and the type of diet consumed." (Sec. 1.4.2.1.1, Food Ingestion Rates)

> "The species included in the Handbook have broad geographic ranges, and they may exhibit different values for many of the exposure factors in different portions of their range. Some species exhibit geographic variation in body size, survival, and reproduction... Site-specific values for these parameters can be determined more accurately using published studies of local populations..." (Sec. 1.3, Uses of the Handbook)

Source: US EPA, *Wildlife Exposure Factors Handbook, Volume I of II* (EPA/600/R-93/187), mirror at rais.ornl.gov/documents/WEFHV1.PDF. Retrieved 2026-08-30.

### (b) FCSAP Ecological Risk Assessment Guidance (Environment and Climate Change Canada / DFO)
FCSAP's ERA guidance is modular. Of direct relevance to Pathway 4:
- **Module 2** - Selection or development of site-specific toxicity reference values (TRVs).
- **Module 3** - Standardization of wildlife receptor characteristics: identifies wildlife species (birds, mammals, reptiles, amphibians) commonly used in Canadian ERAs and provides receptor characteristics (the same class of inputs - diet, ingestion rate, body weight) for oral-intake exposure modelling.
- **Module 7** - Default wildlife TRVs recommended for federal contaminated sites.

The core ERA guidance (2012, with an update in press as of the 2024 SABCS conference, expected publication 2025) states its operating principle as:

> "Only conduct risk assessment to the level of detail required to support defensible management decisions" - positioned on a spectrum from "conservative and simplifying assumptions" to "more realistic and refined assumptions."

Source: L. Wilson, M. Latimer (ECCC), J. Young (DFO), *New and Updated Guidance: Ecological Risk Assessment (ERA) for Federal Contaminated Sites*, SABCS BC Conference, 26 Sept 2024, slides 4-5 (sabcs.ca). Retrieved 2026-08-30.

**Gap.** The project holds FCSAP *Ecological Risk Assessment Guidance, Module 3: Standardization of Wildlife Receptor Characteristics*, March 2012, 100 pp., Cat. no. En14-92/3-2013E-PDF. It publishes receptor-characteristic tables covering the great blue heron, river otter and mink. **It does not cover the harbour seal**, which is one of the four sentinel receptors this paper carries at Appendix H section 6.1. What remains outstanding is not retrieval but review: Module 3's tables have not been traced against the receptor values this paper uses, and no value here has been taken from it.

### (c) A Canadian/BC-specific equivalent
No BC- or Canada-wide wildlife exposure-factors handbook parallel to the EPA document was identified in this search. CCME's *Ecological Risk Assessment Guidance Document* (PN 1585, ccme.ca/en/res/eraguidance_e.pdf) is a related national ERA framework document; whether it publishes its own wildlife dietary/ingestion parameter tables (as opposed to pointing to FCSAP/EPA sources) was not checked in this draft - reported as an open item, not assumed.

---


<div id="sec-9-8-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.8.2 The problem, stated precisely

Wildlife dietary composition, ingestion rate, and body weight are not constants: the EPA Handbook records them as varying "seasonally and by age, size, reproductive status, and habitat," and separately notes that species with broad ranges "exhibit different values for many of the exposure factors in different portions of their range" - geography, not just season, moves the input. A province-wide numerical sediment standard for Pathway 4, by construction, needs one number, which means one fixed parameterization (one diet mix, one ingestion rate, one body weight) applied everywhere in BC, in every season. The catalog sources were built for site-specific ERA, where the assessor selects the receptor and season-and-region-appropriate values for the site at hand - that is the tool's designed use, not an incidental limitation. Collapsing that variability into a single province-wide input set necessarily either overprotects most sites and seasons (if the input set is drawn from the most exposed tail) or underprotects some real subset of them (if it is drawn from a mid-range average).

---


<div id="sec-9-8-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.8.3 Options for province-wide applicability

Presented for TWG evaluation, not ranked or recommended.

**(a) Bounding most-exposed receptor and diet.** Select the single receptor/diet/season/region combination expected to produce the highest sediment-normalized dose among plausible BC wildlife, and parameterize the whole province standard on it.
*Requires:* a defensible screening exercise across candidate receptors to identify the bounding case, using catalog data of the kind in Section 9.8.1.
*Protects:* the most exposed species, individuals, and seasons everywhere in the province by construction.
*Conservatism:* very high - likely the most conservative option on this list, since province-wide applicability and worst-case receptor selection compound.
*Cost:* low ongoing (single number, no site judgment needed); potentially high in remediation trigger volume if the bounding case is far from typical exposure.
*Gives up:* protectiveness proportionate to actual exposure at most sites; risk of standards driven by a receptor/season/region combination rarely present at the site being regulated.

**(b) A small set of receptor scenarios keyed to setting.** Define a limited number of standard scenarios (e.g., marine vs. freshwater; presence/absence of a piscivorous or benthivorous receptor guild appropriate to the setting) each with its own fixed parameterization, and let the applicable scenario be selected by site setting rather than by full site-specific assessment.
*Requires:* a defensible classification scheme for "setting" that can be applied without a full ERA, and catalog-supported parameter sets for each scenario.
*Protects:* receptors plausible for each setting category, more precisely than one bounding case for the whole province.
*Conservatism:* moderate, and scenario-dependent.
*Cost:* moderate - more numbers to derive and defend than (a), less analytical burden per site than full site-specific work.
*Gives up:* precision within a setting category (season, region, individual variation are still averaged out); a classification boundary problem (which scenario applies at a given site) is introduced.

**(c) Derive to a prey-tissue endpoint; leave diet to site-specific assessment.** Derive the province-wide numeric value at the sediment-to-prey-tissue step only (e.g., a tissue-residue or bioaccumulation-based benchmark), and leave the diet-composition/ingestion-rate/receptor-selection step for site-specific ERA to apply against that tissue value.
*Requires:* a defensible sediment-to-tissue relationship (bioaccumulation factor or model) that itself does not depend on the variable inputs.
*Protects:* by handing the site-specific, TRV-comparison step to the assessor who has actual local receptor and diet information - protectiveness is realized at the site-specific step, not baked into the province-wide number.
*Conservatism:* depends entirely on the sediment-to-tissue model chosen; the numeric value alone does not resolve whether wildlife are protected - that determination is deferred.
*Cost:* moderate to derive; shifts ongoing cost to site-specific practitioners for every site where Pathway 4 is potentially complete.
*Gives up:* a single Pathway 4 "pass/fail" sediment number - sites cannot be screened for wildlife protection from sediment concentration alone.

**(d) Adopt another jurisdiction's derived Pathway 4 value(s).** Use an existing external jurisdiction's food-web-derived sediment value(s) for BC, either as-is or adjusted.
*Requires:* identifying a jurisdiction whose receptor set, diet assumptions, and food-web model are defensible as applicable to BC conditions, and a documented basis for adoption or adjustment.
*Protects:* whatever that jurisdiction's derivation protects - not independently verified against BC receptors/diets unless adjusted.
*Conservatism:* inherited from the source jurisdiction, unknown until a specific value is proposed.
*Cost:* low to adopt; cost/defensibility risk lies in justifying applicability to BC without site-specific re-derivation.
*Gives up:* BC-specific accuracy for whichever receptors/diets/seasons are not represented in the adopted value's original derivation.

**(e) No province-wide Pathway 4 value; site-specific trigger or screening flag only.** Do not derive a province-wide numeric standard for Pathway 4. Instead, flag Pathway 4 as potentially complete (using qualitative or coarse screening criteria - e.g., presence of a wildlife receptor guild with plausible food-web linkage to the sediment) and route those sites to site-specific ERA using catalogs such as (1)(a)/(1)(b).
*Requires:* a screening/trigger test for "is Pathway 4 potentially complete here," not a numeric benchmark.
*Protects:* by ensuring every site where the pathway may be complete gets an actual site-specific assessment with locally appropriate inputs - no averaging-out of variability at all.
*Conservatism:* not applicable in the same sense - protectiveness depends on ERA quality and trigger sensitivity, not a fixed number.
*Cost:* highest per-site cost where the trigger fires (full ERA); lowest cost where it does not (no numeric derivation program needed).
*Gives up:* a numeric standard for this pathway - this is explicitly the "no province-wide value" option and would need to be reconciled with any Phase 2 requirement that every pathway carry a number.

**(f) Further option supported by the sources: a bounded range or distribution rather than a point value, with a site-specific position-within-range determination.** The Handbook's format - ranges/means by species, season, and location rather than single values - supports deriving a province-wide range (not a single number) and requiring a site-specific brief determination of where the site falls in that range (e.g., by season of sampling or presence/absence of a documented local population), short of a full ERA. This sits between (b) and (c)/(e) in analytical burden; it was not asked for explicitly but is a structure the catalog format itself invites.

---


<div id="sec-9-8-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

**(g) A habitat-based food-web bioaccumulation model calibrated to a named apex predator.**
Build a food-web bioaccumulation model for a defined habitat and a named upper-trophic receptor, and
back-calculate the sediment concentration corresponding to a tissue or dietary threshold for that
receptor.
*Requires:* the model itself, with its food-web structure, its bioaccumulation and biomagnification
factors, its lipid and organic-carbon normalisation basis, and the receptor's toxicity threshold - none
of which this project has yet read (see Section 9.8.4). A named receptor and a defined habitat must be
chosen before the model can be parameterised at all.
*Protects:* the modelled apex receptor in the modelled habitat, explicitly and by construction, rather
than by assuming that a benthic-invertebrate criterion is protective further up the food web.
*Applicability - and this is the option's central question:* the two values British Columbia has adopted
by this route are both for strongly hydrophobic, lipophilic, biomagnifying organic substances -
total PCBs and total PBDEs. Nothing retrieved by this project demonstrates that the approach extends
beyond that class, and the mechanisms it rests on - lipid partitioning and trophic transfer - are not
the mechanisms that govern metals, organometallics or ionisable and polar organics, which the
classification framework in Appendix C already separates on exactly this basis. **The option should
therefore be evaluated as a candidate for a defined substance class, not as a general Pathway 4
method.** For that class it may be the strongest option on this list; outside it, it may not apply at
all. Establishing which substances fall inside its domain is part of evaluating it.
*Conservatism:* high, and demonstrably so in the one B.C. case: the adopted total-PCB value sits far
below both the in-force Schedule 3.4 marine standards and the detection limit a B.C. laboratory
delivered on Burrard Inlet sediment in 2020 (Section 9.8.4). Whether an output below routine
detectability is usable as a standard, as a screening flag, or only as a management objective is a
question this option forces and does not answer.
*Cost:* high per substance - a habitat- and receptor-specific model is a research exercise, not a
lookup - but low once derived, and the derivation is reusable across sites in the same habitat type.
*Gives up:* generality. A model calibrated to one apex predator in one habitat carries no automatic
warrant for another receptor, another habitat, or another substance class.

### 9.8.4 The BC precedent

> **[STATUS: SOURCE-GAP QUARANTINE - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]** A previous draft included a
> historical British Columbia total-PCB sediment benchmark and attributed it to a Burrard Inlet
> report and Alava papers. The numerical value, derivation model, factors, receptor parameters,
> and legal or policy status are removed from the active paper surface. The exact primary sources
> must be obtained and independently checked before this precedent can support option analysis.

This section retains only the existence of an unresolved research question: whether a food-web
model based on a bounding receptor can be evaluated as a general framework option. No number,
factor, equation, or adoption conclusion is relied on here.

---
---


<div id="sec-9-8-5" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 9.8.5 What the TWG needs to choose

- A decision on whether Phase 2 requires every pathway to carry a province-wide number (constraining out option (e)), or whether a site-specific trigger is an acceptable Phase 2 deliverable for this pathway.
- Review of FCSAP Module 3's receptor-characteristic tables against the receptor values this paper carries, to know what Canadian-context inputs already exist before choosing between (a)/(b)/(c). The module is held - see Section 9.8.1(b) - so this is a reading task, not a retrieval task; it should also settle where the harbour seal's characteristics come from, since Module 3 does not cover it.
- A candidate list of BC/Canadian wildlife receptors with a plausible sediment-to-prey-to-wildlife food-web linkage, to evaluate against options (a)/(b)/(f).
- A position on achievability: whether a value below the laboratory MDL (as occurred with the PCB WSQG) is an acceptable outcome for a province-wide Pathway 4 standard, or a reason to prefer (c), (d), (e), or (f) for this pathway.
- If (d) is to be considered, identification of a specific external jurisdiction's derived value(s) and its underlying receptor/diet assumptions, for comparison against BC receptors.
- A decision on whether a sediment-to-tissue benchmark (option c) is an acceptable Phase 2 deliverable in place of a sediment concentration standard, given Task 6.1's charge to deliver "options for equations."

ENV Burrard Inlet WQO Sediment Attainment Report p. 40 (per project-supplied verified facts). CCME PN 1585 identified but not reviewed for content in this draft.*
