<div id="sec-9-9" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 9.9 Water lot use classes and their exposure scenarios - options

**This section answers a requirement of the project plan that the rest of this paper meets only
implicitly.** Task 3.7 requires the project to define, among the input parameter scope and templates,
**"water lot use classes"**. The four Sediment Use Categories proposed at Section 4.1 are this
draft's answer to that requirement, and until now the paper has stated them without saying so and
without setting out alternatives. **Both are corrected here: the categories are the water lot use
classes, and they are put as one option among several.**

**Why the term matters.** A water lot is a tenure concept - a defined area of the bed of a
watercourse or the foreshore, held under a licence, lease or grant. A sediment use category is an
exposure-scenario concept - a description of who is present, how often, and doing what. They are not
the same kind of object, and a scheme has to choose which one it is keyed to. **An option set that
does not make that choice explicit will produce categories that cannot be assigned to a real site**,
which is the failure the Reviewer's Guide asks reviewers to test at question 3.

### 9.9.1 The four options

**Option 1 - Retain the in-force pair: Sensitive Use and Typical Use.**
Schedule 3.4 as in force carries exactly two use classes, headed *"for Sensitive Use"* and *"for
Typical Use"*, across freshwater and marine and estuarine sediment. *Protocol 28* chapter 7 derives
them as a CCME probable effect level multiplied by 0.62 and 1.2 respectively.
*Exposure scenario:* benthic invertebrates in direct contact, at two protection levels. No human
receptor and no food-web receptor is carried, because the derivation does not reach them.
*Parameter set:* the smallest of the four options. Two classes times two waterbody types is four
columns, which is what the schedule prints today.
*What it costs:* the two pathways this project exists to add have nowhere to attach. Industrial
waterfronts and pristine estuaries share the "typical" column unless the site is classed sensitive.

**Option 2 - The four Sediment Use Categories proposed at Section 4.1: Aquatic Wildlands, Aquatic
Recreational / Traditional Harvest, Commercial Aquatic, Industrial Aquatic.**
*Exposure scenario, per category:* set out at Section 4.1 and parameterised at Section 7.5.2 -
incidental sediment ingestion, wetted skin surface area and a sediment adherence factor for the human
direct pathway; a governing wildlife index receptor for the food-web pathway; an ecological species
protection level running 99, 95, 90 and 80 per cent across the four.
*Receptors and pathways:* all four receptor-pathways apply in Aquatic Recreational / Traditional
Harvest; the human food pathway is the distinguishing one, since it is the category defined partly by
harvesting. Industrial Aquatic carries occupational direct contact with public access restricted.
*What it costs:* four classes times two waterbody types is eight columns per substance, and three of
the four categories have no counterpart in the in-force schedule, so every value in them is new.
**The protection-level ladder is also the weakest part of this option as drafted** - Section 15.5
records that the 90 and 80 per cent rungs have no source and that the row enters no equation.

**Option 3 - Key the classes to the upland land use, using the Contaminated Sites Regulation's own
categories.**
The regulation defines ten land uses, including wildlands, natural wildlands, urban park,
residential, commercial and industrial. A water lot would take the class of the land it adjoins or
serves, so that a foreshore lot fronting an industrial parcel is industrial and one fronting a park
is urban park.
*Exposure scenario:* inherited from the corresponding soil land use, adjusted for the wetted
condition. This is the option with the strongest claim to internal consistency, because Section 19.2
already recommends treating soil standards as the read-across basis for sediment human health, and
because *Protocol 1* v5.0 section 4.1.1 directs exactly that read-across today.
*What it costs:* it imports a land-use vocabulary into an aquatic setting where several of the classes
have no meaning - agricultural and high density residential water lots are not obviously anything -
and a water lot's tenure may have no upland counterpart at all.

**Option 4 - The in-force pair plus an industrial class.**
Sensitive, Typical and Industrial. This keeps continuity with the schedule as printed and adds the
one class the two-tier scheme most obviously lacks, since active industrial waterfronts currently sit
in the same column as ordinary sites.
*Exposure scenario:* sensitive and typical as in force; industrial adds occupational direct contact
with restricted public access and no harvesting assumption.
*What it costs:* it does not create a category keyed to harvesting, so the human food pathway would
have to be carried across all three classes rather than concentrated in one.

### 9.9.2 What the options actually differ on

**Three things, and they can be answered separately.**

1. **How many classes.** Two, three, four or ten. This drives the size of the schedule directly:
   classes times waterbody types times substances.
2. **What the class is keyed to** - the ecological sensitivity of the receiving environment
   (Options 1 and 4), the human activity taking place there (Option 2), or the tenure and its upland
   use (Option 3).
3. **Whether harvesting gets its own class.** Only Option 2 makes harvesting a class in itself. Under
   the others the human food pathway either applies everywhere or nowhere.

### 9.9.3 The exposure terms, and where they come from

**No exposure value is proposed here that is not already compiled elsewhere in this paper**, and the
terms carry the flags they carry there. For the human direct pathway the per-category terms are at
Section 7.5.2 and Appendix H section 3, and **Section 7.5.2 records that its incidental ingestion and
wetted surface area rows disagree with Appendix H**, which governs. For the food pathways the
governing wildlife receptor per category is at Section 7.5.2 and the seafood ingestion rate is an
option set at Section 7.8.2 rather than a per-category value, for the reason given there: a
consumption rate belongs to an exposure scenario, not to a category.

**The source allocation factor does not vary by class.** It is 0.2 for every human-health scenario in
every option above, for the reason at Section 9.5.3.2: a person is in contact with soil or with
sediment at a given moment, not both, so the two draw on one share of the tolerable daily intake.

**And the refinement a real site needs is not obtained here.** Where a site's exposure pattern differs
enough from the class to change the answer, the route is a risk-based standard with the scenario set
to that site, not a finer generic class. A generic numerical standard cannot carry a site's exposure
pattern, and adding classes to make it try is the wrong instrument for the problem.

### 9.9.4 What the Technical Working Group is asked to decide

**No option is selected here.** The questions are:

* which of the three keys at Section 9.9.2 the classes should follow;
* how many classes, given that each one multiplies the schedule;
* whether harvesting warrants a class of its own or is an exposure scenario applied across classes;
* and, if a class scheme other than Option 1 is chosen, what happens to the values in force, which
  were derived for two classes and cannot simply be redistributed across more.

**The freshwater gap applies to all four options.** As Section 7.5 records, the categories, index
receptors and exposure scenarios drafted so far are coastal and estuarine; no freshwater or lacustrine
receptor, diet or contact scenario has been parameterised for any of them.

<div id="sec-10-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 10.0 Emerging Recommended Framework

Based on the Phase 1 White Paper and the Phase 2 project plan, the framework this paper carries is the three-part Schedule 3.4 structure: matrix standards for priority substances in Part 1, and generic standards for broader contaminant coverage in Parts 2 and 3.

> **Reconciliation against the authoritative Phase 1 document.** This section was written against the earlier white paper. It has been checked against *Proposed Framework for Modernizing British Columbia's Sediment Standards* (SABCS and SSTAC, July 2026), and the checks and the divergences are recorded in full under Section 5.0. In summary: the hybrid direction is consistent with that framework, which proposes both matrix standards and, in Section V.A, a "Technical Procedure for Updating CSR Standards with Values Adopted from Other Jurisdictions" for substances handled as generic sediment standards "due to lack of available information necessary to derive matrix sediment standards or other considerations." The framework does not, however, divide Schedule 3.4 into parts, so the multi-part architecture described here is the contribution of this paper and the Phase 2 project plan rather than of Phase 1. On sequencing, the framework's Executive Summary states that "The recommended pathway is data-driven Phased Modernization that leverages technology to build a BC-specific evidence base before calculating new numerical standards," and its Section VII.A places the Data Compilation Project ahead of matrix standard development in priority order. Readers comparing the two documents should also note the further divergences listed under Section 5.0, in particular the framework's treatment of Indigenous Uses as a distinct pathway and its tiered assessment structure, neither of which is carried through this section.

**The three parts, stated.** "Multi-part" above is imprecise, and the structure this paper carries
is specific. It follows CSR Schedule 3.1 for soil, which is drafted in exactly three parts, and
proposes the same shape for Schedule 3.4:

* **Part 1 - matrix sediment standards.** Per-substance tables whose rows are receptor-pathway
  combinations and whose columns are the sediment use categories, for substances that warrant
  pathway-specific derivation. Section 10.1 below.
* **Part 2 - generic numerical sediment standards to protect human health.**
* **Part 3 - generic numerical sediment standards to protect ecological health.** Parts 2 and 3 are
  treated together at Section 10.2 below, but they are two parts of the schedule, not one.

Section 4.3.1 sets out the Schedule 3.1 precedent this follows, including how the *Contaminated
Sites Regulation* defines and engages the two kinds of standard. Section 9.7 explains why the alternative architectures were not pursued, as context for this
architecture against the alternatives. **Section 10.3 is not a fourth part** - it is a statement
about how the framework should be refined as Phase 2 research completes, not a component of the
schedule.

**This is a tiering of the standards themselves, not of the assessment route.** The numerical /
site-specific / detailed-risk-assessment sequence is a separate axis, governed by Protocol 1 and
discussed at Section 8.7; it operates alongside whichever schedule structure is adopted and is not
what "three-part" refers to here.

<div id="sec-10-0-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

#### 10.0.1 What the three-part architecture leaves open

> **What follows is what the three-part architecture leaves open.** The structure itself
> is given by the Phase 2 project plan (Section 4.0); Section 9.7 sets out why the
> alternatives were not pursued.

**Why the structure matters even though it is not chosen here.** The structure of
Schedule 3.4 determines what can be expressed in it. A matrix part can carry
receptor-pathway differentiation and sediment use categories; a generic part cannot.
The plan's direction therefore shapes every option in Sections 9.1 through 9.6 before
any of them is settled, which is why the structure is stated at Section 4.0 and not
left to emerge from the options analysis.

**What the three parts do, stated without comparison.** Three things follow from the
structure itself, and each is established elsewhere in this paper rather than asserted
here:

1. It carries receptor-pathway differentiation for the substances that warrant it
   (Part 1), while not forcing that cost onto substances that do not (Parts 2 and 3).
2. It separates human-health from ecological protection into distinct parts.
3. It follows drafting already in force for soil - CSR Schedule 3.1, Section 4.3.1 -
   so the regulatory form is proven and the definitions and engaging provisions have
   precedent.

The applicability rule is settled in principle as a two-step rule. Direct-contact pathways are
mandatory within the framework. Food pathways are conditional on applicable criteria and site
conditions. All standards that apply apply; the lowest applicable value may be used as a practical
remediation focus, but it does not displace other applicable standards. Site assignment remains
within the CSR framework and is not re-opened as an option in this paper.

**What adopting the three-part structure does not decide.** It leaves open: which exposure
parameter set is used (Section 9.5.3.1); whether a detectability or background floor applies
(Section 9.5); the sample-support rules - depth interval, compositing, spatial unit and compliance
statistic (Section 9.0); whether Indigenous use is carried as a distinct pathway or as
parameterisation within the human-health pathways, which Section 5.0 records as a divergence from
the Phase 1 framework still to be decided; and every numerical value. **The structure is logically
prior to all of them and presupposes none of them.**

> **What is still owed here.** The open items above are the whole of what this
> subsection now asks for, and until they are answered Section 10.0 states a
> plan-given structure and describes what it leaves open, without claiming to have
> chosen it. That is the correct position for this paper. It is not a gap being
> papered over; the gap that remains is the substance-coverage number, and it is
> named as such.

<div id="sec-10-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 10.1 Matrix Standards for Priority Substances

<div id="sec-10-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

> **No detectability or background floor is applied to the values in this section.** Section 9.5 sets out the option - Protocol 28's final-standards-selection rule, under which a calculated standard below the background estimate or the analytical detection limit is replaced by the greater concentration - together with what adopting it would change. Until that option is decided, some values in this section sit below the detection limits recorded elsewhere in this paper.
Priority substances should be identified through a structured classification and prioritization process. Matrix standards should be developed where substances warrant pathway-specific derivation and where sufficient data and input parameters are available or can be developed.

### 10.2 Generic Standards for Other Substances
Generic standards should be adopted or adapted from other jurisdictions where matrix derivation is not feasible, not necessary, or not proportionate. A structured adoption procedure is needed to ensure values are scientifically defensible and relevant to BC.

<div id="sec-10-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 10.3 Research-Driven Framework Refinement
The final recommendations in this paper should be refined as Phase 2 research is completed. Each workstream should contribute to the final Matrix Options Paper through both a body-section synthesis and a detailed appendix.

---

<div id="sec-11-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 11.0 Indigenous Engagement Is Not Within the Scope of Phase 2

Indigenous engagement is not within the scope of Phase 2. This project develops the scientific framework only; consultation with Indigenous Nations forms part of ENV's own policy, consultation and approval processes for any future regulatory change. **No Nation, Indigenous organization, or Knowledge Holder has been consulted on, has reviewed, or has endorsed any content in this paper.**

Where Indigenous consumption rates, traditional food consumption assumptions, and high-use dietary scenarios appear anywhere in this paper, they appear only as published exposure parameters taken from documents prepared for or with specific Indigenous Nations - principally the Tsleil-Waututh Nation Burrard Inlet water quality objectives work and the Lower Athabasca criteria report prepared for the Athabasca Chipewyan First Nation - and from the general technical literature. They are not the product of engagement, they do not represent the position of any Nation, and they carry no implication that any Nation has been asked or has agreed.

> **Source-gap quarantine for high-intake consumption rates.** The cited rate records and their scope, units, and applicability have not been verified against exact primary-source locations in this remediation. No consumption-rate value is accepted as a current derivation input or decision-driving parameter in this successor.
>
> **Using a published value from a Nation's own work is not engagement with that Nation, and this paper claims no more than the citation.** Neither Nation has been consulted about this paper, neither has reviewed it, and nothing here represents, records or implies the position of either. Where a source document attributes a value to a particular community, this paper reproduces that attribution only where the source itself supports it: Appendix H records one instance where the parameter catalogue's community attribution is **not** supported by the underlying report, and this paper does not adopt it.
>
> **Naming the sources is deliberate.** A reader who found that the subsistence consumption rates originated in these Nations' own work, after being told they were drawn from the technical literature, would be entitled to ask why. Accuracy about provenance is part of the disclaimer, not a qualification of it.

The technical consequence for the framework is narrow and specific: the food-pathway equations should be constructed so that consumption rates, receptor assumptions, and dietary scenarios can be substituted when ENV's own consultation processes later establish which values apply. Nothing in this paper determines those values.


<div id="sec-11-6" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 11.6 Interface with federal authorities

Sediment at many of the sites this framework addresses is also regulated
federally. Dredged material moved to a disposal site is subject to the *Canadian
Environmental Protection Act* disposal-at-sea regime; works affecting fish and
fish habitat engage the *Fisheries Act*; and sediment in a federal port is
managed by a port authority under its own governing legislation. This paper
discusses dredging throughout as a driver of sediment management decisions.

**Phase 2 proposes no relationship between the matrix framework and any of these
regimes.** No provision here is intended to trigger, satisfy, substitute for or
constrain a federal authorisation, and no federal threshold has been used in
deriving anything in this paper. A provincial sediment standard and a federal
disposal-at-sea decision remain separate determinations under separate
authorities.

That is a position, not an oversight, and it is stated so that it can be
challenged. Whether the two regimes should be aligned - and if so at which point,
on whose thresholds, and with what sequencing where a site requires both - is a
question for ENV and its federal counterparts. It is outside the scope of Phase 2
as set out in the Phase 2 project plan, and nothing in this paper should be read
as having settled it.

---

<div id="sec-12-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

<div id="sec-12-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 12.0 Governance and Roles


<div id="sec-12-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 12.1 Where This Project Sits

The Sediment Standards Project, Phase 2 (2026-2027), is a project of the **Site Remediation Program
(SRP)** within the BC Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV), carried out in partnership with the
Science Advisory Board for Contaminated Sites in British Columbia (SABCS). The plan records that
the SRP "partnered with the Science Advisory Board for Contaminated Sites (SABCS) in 2025 to
collaboratively develop a scientific framework for modernizing the CSR standards" (*plan section 1.1*).

The modernization effort is led by the **Science and Standards Technical Advisory Committee
(SSTAC)**, "established by the SABCS in partnership with ENV" (*plan section 1.1*). ENV holds the decision
authority. The plan is explicit about why the two are distinct: ENV "is responsible for both
regulating the implementation of the contaminated sites legal framework and developing and
maintaining these standards", and "will subsequently follow its internal policy, consultation, and
approval processes before recommending any regulatory changes to the CSR" (*plan section 1.1*).

This paper is a technical and scientific product. It does not exercise, anticipate or substitute for
that ENV process, and nothing in it has been decided.


<div id="sec-12-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 12.2 Roles and Decision Authority

The governance structure below is taken from *plan section 3*. Roles are stated; individuals are not named
in this draft.

| Role | Position | Function in Phase 2 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Project Sponsor** | Unit Head, Science, Standards & Risk Assessment | Accountable executive owner of the Phase 2 scope within the Site Remediation Program |
| **Project Lead** | Senior Risk Assessment Officer (RAO) | Day-to-day direction of the ten Phase 2 tasks; primary point of contact for SABCS, SSTAC and the TWG |
| **Core Project Team** | Risk Assessment Officers (RAOs) | Task delivery, technical drafting, and internal review of TWG-led outputs |
| **ENV staff** | Advisory | Subject-matter advice as required |

The plan records a temporary assignment affecting early project sequencing. This paper does not reproduce internal capacity or slack analysis.

**Decision authority.** SABCS, SSTAC, the TWG and CSAP develop technical content, evaluate options
and recommend approaches. ENV retains responsibility for all future policy, regulatory and standards
decisions. A recommendation appearing in this paper is a recommendation *to* ENV, not a decision *by*
ENV.

**A note on overlapping roles.** The ENV Project Lead also serves as a Co-Chair of the SSTAC - one
of two, the committee being co-chaired. This is disclosed here because SSTAC leads the modernization
effort while ENV holds the decision authority, and a reader is entitled to see where the two sit in
the same person. It does not alter the division of authority set out above. **Source:** the Phase 2
project plan does not record this, and nothing in it connects the two roles. The role is listed in
the Phase 1 White Paper, *Preliminary Framework for Modernizing British Columbia's Sediment
Standards* (v3.0, 2025), in its **Acknowledgements** under the Science & Standards Technical
Advisory Committee heading, as "SSTAC Co-Chair, ENV, SABCS"; that document evidences the role as it
stood in Phase 1, and the project lead has confirmed it as current.


<div id="sec-12-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 12.3 Key Advisors and Contributors

*Plan section 3* lists four bodies as Key Advisors / Contributors.

| Body | Role in Phase 2 | Basis |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **SABCS** | Partner organization; funded Phase 1 in full and hosts the volunteer model on which Phase 2 depends | *plan section 1.1, section 3* |
| **SSTAC** | Leads the modernization effort. Membership and resourcing are to be **confirmed**, and scope, deliverables and timelines **developed**, under Task 1 - Both are scheduled work rather than completed work. | *plan section 1.1, section 3, Task 1* |
| **Technical Working Group (TWG)** | Over 50 volunteer members, organised as a focused "core" sub-group plus the full membership | *plan section 1.1, section 2.1, section 3* |
| **CSAP** | "engagement at defined points" | *plan section 3* |

**How the TWG is organised.** The plan describes a two-tier structure: a sub-group "core" that
"supports with research, drafting, and editing tasks, while the full TWG supports by sharing
information and reviewing and providing feedback on draft materials" (*plan section 2.1*). Detailed TWG
workplans - objective, deliverable, timeline, focused TWG role and engagement, per task - are Task
1.8, scheduled for week 2.

**The TWG's expertise base.** The plan describes the TWG as "drawing world-class expertise from
industry, consulting, academia, government, and Indigenous organizations" (*plan section 1.1*). That is a
statement about where technical expertise is drawn from. It is not consultation, and it is not
engagement: **Indigenous engagement is not within the scope of Phase 2.** Consultation with
Indigenous Nations forms part of ENV's own policy, consultation and approval processes for any future
regulatory change, and is addressed there rather than here.

**CSAP.** The plan names CSAP as a contributor engaged "at defined points" (*plan section 3*). CSAP is the
professional body whose members would apply whatever framework results, so its input is material to
the feasibility criterion in Section 8.0.

The plan does not identify what those points are, the mechanism, or who initiates. **No CSAP
engagement has occurred, and no position is attributed to CSAP anywhere in this paper.**

**Briefings.** The plan provides for "Regular briefings with updates, progress and next steps for
ENV, the SABCS and the SSTAC", running from July 2026 and ongoing (*plan section 2.1*).


<div id="sec-12-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 12.4 Technical Feasibility and Phased Implementation

The technical options are intended to be developed through phased research, documented source review, and independent checking. Routine laboratory and data-compilation activities can be assessed against available methods and evidence; complex parameter development and numerical derivation remain Phase 3 work. Appendix L records the project sequence as a planning instrument, not as technical acceptance.

## 13.0 Phase 2 Workstreams, Schedule, and the Status of This Draft


<div id="sec-13-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 13.1 Purpose of This Chapter

This chapter exists so that a reviewer can tell, for any part of this paper, three things: which
Phase 2 task it belongs to, when that task is scheduled, and whether the content in front of them was
written on schedule, written ahead of schedule, or has not been written at all.

That distinction matters more than usual here. This draft was prepared under Task 2, "Develop
Early DRAFT Options Paper", in month 2 of a twelve-month plan. Most of the research it discusses is
scheduled for months 4 through 9. Content written ahead of its scheduled month has not been through
the workstream that is supposed to produce it, and - as of this draft - **no TWG review cycle has
occurred at all**. Section 13.4 states what that means in practice.


<div id="sec-13-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 13.2 The Twelve-Month Calendar

Phase 2 runs from July 2026 to June 2027. The month numbering used throughout the plan and this
chapter maps to calendar months as follows. This mapping is fixed by the plan itself: *plan section 2.1*
schedules "bioavailability (months 4-6)" within "October 2026 - March 2027" and "matrix framework
design (months 7-9)" within the same window, and schedules the drafting of the Matrix Options Paper
for "April - June 2027" against Tasks 9.1-9.3 at months 10-12.

| Month | Calendar | | Month | Calendar |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1 | July 2026 | | 7 | January 2027 |
| 2 | August 2026 | | 8 | February 2027 |
| 3 | September 2026 | | 9 | March 2027 |
| 4 | October 2026 | | 10 | April 2027 |
| 5 | November 2026 | | 11 | May 2027 |
| 6 | December 2026 | | 12 | June 2027 |

**This draft is dated month 2 (August 2026).**

the framework as published exposure parameters taken from documents prepared for or with specific Indigenous Nations - principally the Tsleil-Waututh Nation Burrard Inlet water quality objectives work and the Lower Athabasca criteria report prepared for the Athabasca Chipewyan First Nation - and from the general technical literature. Indigenous
engagement is not within the scope of Phase 2, and consultation forms part of ENV's own policy,
consultation and approval processes for any future regulatory change (Section 12.3).

**On Task 6.1 running early.** The plan attaches a note explaining why the equation-options work sits
at months 1-3 rather than with the rest of Task 6: "6.1 runs early (months 1-3, per Section 2 scope)
because the chosen equation forms determine which input parameters Stream B must compile, so it is a
dependency, not just a deliverable" (*plan section 4*). That dependency is the reason Sections 4 and 7 are
the focus of the current review round. It is also why the option layers put to reviewers in Block E
are weighted toward sediment uses, receptors and pathways, exposure assumptions and receptor
characteristics, and input parameter selection: those are what the equation forms consume, and for
the more straightforward pathways the equation form itself offers few genuine alternatives.

**The workstream view.** *Plan section 5* aligns the tasks into five workstreams with a single key output
each:

| Workstream | Key output |
| :--- | :--- |
| Data | BC Aquatic Database |
| Bioavailability | Scientific research to develop approaches and tools |
| Bioaccumulation | Classification system |
| Framework | Multi-pathway matrix |
| Policy integration | Schedule 3.4 multi-part concept |

These five run in parallel and are interdependent: bioavailability work affects matrix derivation,
bioaccumulation work affects classification, database development affects prioritization, input
parameters affect future standards calculation, and the generic adoption procedure affects the
proposed Schedule 3.4 structure. The plan resolves that interdependence at a single scheduled point,
the month-9 integration workshop, described in Section 13.6.


<div id="sec-13-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 13.4 What "Drafted Ahead" Means

Most of the technical content in this paper - Sections 7.1 through 7.8, Sections 9.1 through 9.7, and
Appendices A through H - is drafted ahead of the task scheduled to produce it. Reviewers should
understand the following without qualification:

* **No TWG review cycle has taken place.** Task 2.2, circulation of this draft for a two-week review
  focused on Sections 4 and 7, is the first, and it has not happened yet. Nothing in this paper
  carries TWG endorsement, SSTAC endorsement, SABCS Board endorsement, or ENV approval.
* **Drafted-ahead content is unreviewed and unvalidated, not merely provisional.** It has not been
  through the literature review, the applicability assessment, the data-requirements analysis or the
  internal technical review that the corresponding task specifies. Where a section reads as a finding,
  it should be read as a candidate for discussion.
* **Any option identified as preferred or emerging is a drafting position only.** No option has been
  selected, and the selection process - options analysis against the Section 8.0 criteria, then
  integration at month 9 - has not been run.
* **No worked derived outputs are part of this paper.** Candidate equations and input parameters remain as structural options. Future numerical testing is Phase 3 work and requires source verification, parameter provenance, and independent review.
* **The deliverable of Phase 2 is the framework and its options, not standards.**

<div id="sec-13-6" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 13.6 The Month-9 Integration Workshop

The plan makes the integration workshop both a task and a success criterion. As Task 8.1 it
is "Conduct integration workshop (all TWGs + internal)", scheduled for month 9 - March 2027
(*plan section 4*). As the third of five Success Criteria it is:

> "Successful Workstream Integration: Facilitation of a comprehensive integration workshop (scheduled
> for month 9) that successfully aligns outputs across all parallel research streams, including
> bioavailability, bioaccumulation, data, and matrix framework design, resolving core technical gaps
> before final drafting." (*plan section 8*)

**Purpose.** Task 8 has three subtasks, all at month 9: conduct the workshop (8.1), "Align outputs
across workstreams (data, frameworks, classification, equations)" (8.2), and "Resolve gaps and
inconsistencies" (8.3). The workshop is therefore the single scheduled point at which the five
parallel workstreams of Section 13.3 are reconciled with each other.

**Why it sits where it does.** Month 9 is the last month before final drafting begins: Task 9.1,
"Draft final Matrix Options Paper", starts at month 10 (April 2027). Every research task feeding the
paper closes between months 7 and 9 - bioavailability at 4.5 (months 6-7), bioaccumulation and classification
at 5.5 (month 8), input parameter QA at 3.9 (month 8), the generic adoption procedure at 7.2 (months
7-8), internal policy/legal review at 7.6 (month 8), and matrix framework refinement at 6.6 (months
8-9). The plan identifies this as the project's tightest point: "The constraint is the month 7-9
convergence, where the research outputs, matrix design, integration workshop, and start of final
drafting all land together" (*plan section 4*). If the inconsistencies between workstreams are not resolved
at month 9, they are carried into the final deliverable.

**Open questions in this paper scheduled to be settled there.** The plan does not enumerate the
questions the workshop will take. The following are the open questions in *this draft* that fall
within the terms of subtasks 8.2 and 8.3 - aligning outputs across workstreams, and resolving gaps
and inconsistencies - and are therefore proposed for the workshop agenda, subject to the Project
Lead's confirmation.

1. **Which of the sixteen exposure scenarios carry forward.** Four receptor-pathways across four
   Sediment Use Categories give $4 \times 4 = 16$ exposure scenarios as the starting point. The
   workshop is where the set is confirmed, and where any scenario that proves implausible, or is
   wholly dominated by another, is dropped with a stated reason.
2. **How the framework applies across freshwater, estuarine and marine sediments.** The framework is
   intended to cover all three. Exposure assumptions, receptor characteristics and input parameters
   differ across them, and the parameter sets are not equally developed. Reconciling the three is a
   data-workstream and framework-workstream question jointly (8.2).
3. **Where bioavailability adjustment attaches to the framework.** Whether it belongs inside
   derivation, as a site-specific adjustment, or as a tier of assessment, is the principal interface
   between Task 4 and Task 6. It cannot be settled inside either workstream alone.
4. **Which bioaccumulation model form the two food pathways use, and how it aligns with the
   classification criteria.** Task 5 produces both the model review and the classification criteria;
   Task 6 consumes them. Any mismatch surfaces here.
5. **Reconciliation of the classification and prioritization criteria.** The plan names biomagnification,
   toxicity, persistence and local occurrence (*plan section 1.4*). Where this draft carries a different or
   expanded criteria set, the two must be reconciled - and the prioritization output validated
   against BC occurrence data (Task 5.4), which is only possible once the BC Aquatic Database exists.
6. **Which input parameters the selected equation forms actually require.** Task 6.1 fixes the
   equation options; Stream B compiles against them (Task 3.8) and QA's the compilation at Task 3.9,
   month 8. The workshop is the first point at which the compiled set can be checked against the
   framework it is meant to serve.
7. **The boundary between Part 1 matrix substances and Parts 2 and 3 generic-adopted substances.**
   Task 7.1 defines the scope of the three-part expansion at month 6; the prioritization framework
   (Task 5.3) decides which substances fall on which side. The two must agree.
8. **Whether the candidate derivation inputs and equations are adequately specified.** The paper records the equations, parameters, source gaps, and review questions needed before Phase 3 numerical derivation. No worked output or calculator check case is carried in this package.

Task 8.1 specifies "all TWGs + internal" and month 9. **Nothing further about this workshop is
fixed, and no invitation has been issued.**

<div id="sec-14-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 14.0 Risks, Constraints, and Mitigation

<div id="sec-14-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 14.1 Data Gaps
Data gaps may limit the ability to derive matrix standards or classify substances with confidence. Mitigation includes developing the BC Aquatic Database, identifying data quality limitations, and using generic standards or research-needed categories where matrix derivation is not currently feasible.

<div id="sec-14-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 14.2 Technical Feasibility

The feasibility question for this paper is whether each candidate method can be specified, sourced, independently checked, and carried into a later phase. Laboratory capability, parameter provenance, and method validation remain evidence questions; this paper does not estimate staffing or reviewer capacity.

<div id="sec-14-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 14.3 Phased Implementation

The phased implementation sequence is set out in the project plan at Appendix L. Research outputs are to be integrated through the working draft and Month 9 workshop, while numerical derivation and regulatory implementation remain outside this Phase 2 paper.

<div id="sec-14-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 14.4 Scope Creep
Phase 2 could expand beyond its intended scope if final numerical standards, regulatory amendments, or final technical guidance are added prematurely. Mitigation includes maintaining the Phase 2 scope boundary set out in the Phase 2 project plan and clearly identifying future-phase work.

<div id="sec-14-5" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 14.5 Laboratory and Analytical Constraints
Some bioavailability and bioaccumulation methods may not be routinely available or standardized. Mitigation includes evaluating analytical feasibility, engaging laboratories where needed, and reserving specialized tools for higher-tier or substance-specific applications.

<div id="sec-14-6" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 14.6 Indigenous Food-Pathway Parameter Adequacy
Indigenous engagement is not within the scope of Phase 2 (see Section 11.0). The technical risk that remains is that the framework may not be built to accommodate community-specific and high-consumption dietary scenarios at the point where ENV's own consultation processes later establish which values apply. Mitigation includes designing the food-pathway equations so that consumption rates and receptor assumptions can be substituted, using only already-published exposure parameters in this phase - including values from documents prepared for specific Nations, cited as such, and avoiding unsupported assumptions about Indigenous use.

<div id="sec-14-7" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 14.7 Schedule Compression
Several Phase 2 tasks converge during later project months, including classification, prioritization, matrix framework design, integration, and final drafting. Mitigation includes early development of the paper structure and iterative refinement as research products are completed.


<div id="sec-14-8" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 14.8 Defensibility of Standards Derived Through This Framework
Sections 14.1 through 14.7 are risks to delivering Phase 2. This one is a risk carried by what
Phase 2 delivers. Standards derived through the framework proposed here would take effect in a
setting where they can be contested - in site-specific approvals, in cost allocation between
parties, and on review - and the grounds on which they could be contested are ones this draft
already records against itself:

- **Values that sit below the analytical floor.** Several governing values fall below the
  detection limits routinely delivered by B.C. laboratories, and no detectability or background
  floor is applied. Section 9.5 puts adopting one as an option rather than settling it. A value
  that cannot be measured is difficult to demonstrate compliance against and difficult to enforce.
- **Conventions that carry weight but carry no source.** The organic-carbon fraction of 1.0 % and
  the 0.20 hazard-quotient allocation are working conventions of this draft, not sourced values,
  and printed numbers depend on both.
- **A derivation surface still under review.** Several candidate inputs and conventions require source verification or a documented selection basis. Those gaps are not converted into numerical outputs in this paper.

**This is not a legal opinion and no assessment of legal exposure has been obtained.** It is
recorded because the items above are the technical decisions that would be examined first, and
because the mitigation for all three is technical rather than legal: settle the floor question
instead of leaving it open, source or withdraw each convention that bears weight, and close the
reproduction failures in Section 15.0 before any value is proposed for adoption. That work is
substantially cheaper in Phase 2 than after a number is in force.

The further mitigation is the one already in place: Sections 1.0 and 16.0 state plainly that this
draft has not been reviewed or endorsed and that no value in it is a standard, so that nothing here
is relied on before it has been through the review it is being written for.

---

<div id="sec-15-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 15.0 Limitations of This Draft

This section states what this draft does not yet support. It is written for the Technical Working Group
and the SSTAC, who are being asked to review the framework, and for ENV, to whom the final Matrix
Options Paper will be submitted. The limitations below are the current state of a body of work that is
two months into a twelve-month plan. They are not defects to be excused; they are the work that is
scheduled, and each one is mapped in Section 15.9 to the plan task that closes it.


<div id="sec-15-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 15.1 Nothing here has been reviewed, endorsed, adopted or decided

This draft was prepared under Task 2 of the Phase 2 project plan, "Develop Early DRAFT Options Paper".
It sets out a framework and the options within it. It is not a set of standards and it is not a
recommendation that has been agreed by anyone.

* No Technical Working Group review has been completed and no comment has been received. The first
  review point in the plan is Task 2.2 -- circulate the conceptual draft for two weeks, with focus on
  Sections 4 and 7 -- and this draft is prepared for it.
* No SSTAC review has taken place. The plan schedules full TWG and SSTAC review at Task 9.2, in month 11.
* No position in this draft is attributed to ENV, to the SABCS, to the SSTAC, to any TWG member, to
  CSAP or to any other body, and none should be inferred.
* ENV is responsible for the Contaminated Sites Regulation and for the standards within it. ENV will
  follow its own policy, consultation and approval processes before recommending any regulatory change.
  Nothing in this draft anticipates or substitutes for those processes.

The plan places four things outside Phase 2: final numeric standards; regulatory amendments; supporting
technical guidance or protocol; and a complete sediment database. It also names reinforcing the "no
standards derivation" boundary as the mitigation for its scope-creep risk. Phase 3 is where the
framework, once settled, would be used to derive and calculate standards for a subset of prioritised
substances. Every equation and every number in this draft should be read against that boundary.


<div id="sec-15-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 15.2 Nothing in this draft has been tested against British Columbia occurrence data

The BC Aquatic Database does not exist yet. Under the plan it is a Task 3 Stream A deliverable: reports
identified in month 1, database structure reviewed and confirmed in month 3, data compilation from
month 4 onward, QA/QC and data usability screening in month 7, and the database and source inventory
built in month 7. Validation of the substance classification and prioritisation work against BC
occurrence data is Task 5.4, in month 8. Today is month 2.

The consequence is specific and it runs through the whole draft:

* No statement about which substances occur in BC sediments, at what concentrations, at what frequency,
  or at how many sites, rests on compiled BC data. Where the draft carries such a statement -- for
  example the occurrence scores in the substance prioritisation worksheets -- it is a placeholder
  pending Task 3.4 and Task 5.4, and the worksheets say so.
* No candidate priority substance list has been tested against **compiled** BC occurrence data. The plan's
  four prioritisation criteria are biomagnification, toxicity, persistence and local occurrence, and local
  occurrence is the one that no literature review alone can settle. **It is not, however, unknowable in the
  meantime.** Site investigation reports, published studies, monitoring records and the harbour, pulp mill,
  mine and shipyard investigations already named in the prioritisation dimension are evidence of *potential*
  local occurrence and exist now; what this draft requires is that any such claim **name the evidence behind
  it** (Section 7.4.1). Federal risk assessments under the *Canadian Environmental Protection Act*, and the
  scientific supporting documents behind Canadian and B.C. sediment quality guidelines, are admissible
  supplemental evidence for this purpose. What the BC Aquatic Database will add is systematic, compiled and quality-screened
  coverage - and it will carry its own limits when it arrives, bounded by whatever has actually been
  submitted and compiled. A further caution belongs here: evidence assembled from investigation records is
  **opportunistic**, reflecting where investigation has happened rather than where a substance occurs, so it
  supports a claim of potential presence far better than it supports absence.
* No background-concentration proposal can yet be made. The plan's own objective is that concentration
  ranges across BC are needed "to ensure standards are not below natural background concentrations".
  Until the database exists, this draft has no basis on which to say where that floor sits, for any
  substance, in any region.
* No statement can yet be made about how often risk-based standards are used on BC sediment sites, or
  how often bioavailability adjustment is what carried a site to compliance.


<div id="sec-15-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 15.3 Material has been drafted ahead of its scheduled task, and is unreviewed

The plan's research is phased across months 4 to 9: bioavailability in months 4 to 6, bioaccumulation in
months 5 to 7, substance classification in months 6 to 8, and matrix framework design in months 7 to 9.
The substance prioritisation framework and the generic-standards adoption procedure are scheduled for
January to March 2027. Several technical appendices to this draft are early sketches of those outputs,
written at month 2.

Drafting ahead is deliberate: it gives the TWG something concrete to react to rather than a blank page,
and Task 6.1 (options for the derivation equations, months 1 to 3) is genuinely due now because the
chosen equation forms determine which input parameters Task 3.8 must compile. But the material carries
two limitations that a reader must hold onto:

1. **It is one drafter's starting point, not a research finding.** Where an appendix reads as though a
   question has been settled, it has not been. The research that settles it is the working group's, and
   it has not started.
2. **It has had no technical review of any kind.** No section of this draft has been checked by a second
   pair of eyes with subject-matter expertise.

Any appendix whose scheduled task has not begun should be read as a proposal to be replaced by that
task's output, not as an input the task must accommodate.


<div id="sec-15-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 15.4 Framework findings retained after calculation withdrawal

No worked derived outputs are part of this Phase 2 paper. The paper retains the non-arithmetic framework findings: the need to reconcile source conventions, the absence of a held species-sensitivity distribution, the need for documented parameter provenance, and the requirement for independent review before any numerical derivation. Future calculation work is a separate Phase 3 activity and is not a dependency of this review package.

### 15.5 Governing input evidence gaps

[STATUS: SOURCE_GAP] The active Phase 2 paper does not carry a numerical input
registry for derivation. Future work must verify each parameter, equation,
receptor, normalization basis, and decision rule against a held source and
record its exact location, units, applicability, and uncertainty.

[STATUS: WITHDRAW] Earlier provenance tables and worked-output crosswalks are
preserved in the dated source-successor archive. They are not active inputs,
results, recommendations, or review dependencies.

### 15.6 There is no British Columbia protocol for sediment background concentrations

The plan makes establishment of the BC Aquatic Database a Phase 2 success criterion, and requires that
it be "explicitly aligned with the statistical and spatial approaches of Protocols 4 and 9 which
establish background concentrations for soil and groundwater in BC". Both protocols were opened and
checked for this draft:

* **Protocol 4 for Contaminated Sites, "Establishing Local Background Concentrations in Soil", Version
  15**, approved 10 June 2026, effective 19 June 2026. Twelve pages. The word "sediment" does not appear
  in it once.
* **Protocol 9 for Contaminated Sites, "Establishing Local Background Concentrations in Groundwater",
  Version 5**, approved 10 June 2026, effective 19 June 2026. Ten pages. "Sediment" appears once,
  meaning suspended sediment in a monitoring well.

There is therefore no existing British Columbia protocol for establishing background concentrations in
sediment. Anything this draft says about sediment background is a proposal to carry the statistical and
spatial method of Protocols 4 and 9 across to a medium neither of them covers. That is what the plan
asks for, and it is a legitimate proposal. It is not existing authority, it must not be cited as though
it were, and the extension itself -- what changes when the medium is sediment, a mobile and depositional
medium with a different sampling geometry -- is unexamined work. Where this draft cites Protocol 4, it
must say which part of the method it is borrowing and acknowledge that the borrowing is the proposal.


<div id="sec-15-7" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 15.7 What the in-force Schedule 3.4 derivation method does, and what it does not address

The current generic numerical sediment standards were derived by a method set out in Protocol 28 for
Contaminated Sites, "2016 Standards Derivation Methods", Version 3.0, approved 30 April 2024,
effective 1 May 2024. Its Chapter 7, "Derivation of Generic Sediment Standards", is the entire published
basis, and it is two short sections on one page. In its own words:

> "Generic numerical sediment standards are derived by multiplying a probable effect level from the
> Canadian Council for Ministers of the Environment, 1999, Environmental Quality Guidelines, for a
> substance by a defined probability of observing an EC20 in selected toxicity tests."

The selected tests were a 28 to 42-day test for the freshwater amphipod *Hyalella azteca*, and 10-day
tests for the marine amphipods *Ampelisca abdita* and *Rhepoxynius abronius*. The probability factor
depends on sediment use: for sensitive sediment, a 20% probability of observing an EC20, equal to 0.62
for freshwater, with none derived for marine; for typical sediment, a 50% probability, equal to 1.2 for
both freshwater and marine. Chapter 7 closes by stating that sediment standards

> "are derived based on exposure scenarios for ecological organisms and are not considered protective of
> human health; therefore, CSR soil standards should be applied to sediment for risk assessment
> purposes".

Four things follow, and they bound what this draft can claim:

1. **The method does not address bioaccumulation, food pathways or human health.** Across all 211 pages
   of Protocol 28, "bioaccumulation", "biomagnification" and "food web" do not appear at all, and
   neither does organic carbon normalisation or acid volatile sulfide. This is not an oversight being
   alleged by this draft; it is the scope Protocol 28 states for itself, and it is precisely the gap the
   Phase 2 vision of a multi-part Schedule 3.4 exists to close.
2. **The marine sensitive-use column has no marine probability factor.** Protocol 28 records that no P20
   was derived for marine. Whatever else is decided, the marine sensitive column of the in-force
   schedule rests on a factor that the published method says does not exist for marine.
3. **Comparisons between values derived under this method and values derived under the framework in this
   draft are not like-for-like.** Where this draft compares a candidate value against an in-force
   Schedule 3.4 value and characterises the difference as over- or under-protection, it is comparing
   outputs of two methods with different endpoints, different receptors and different pathways. Those
   comparisons are informative about the size of the gap, not about the error of the in-force value
   against its own purpose. They must be labelled that way.
4. **The existing derivation is not silent on freshwater -- it is better documented there.** Freshwater
   is the setting with a published probability factor and a chronic (28 to 42-day) test basis. That is
   the opposite of the balance in this draft's own supporting material, which is the subject of
   Section 4.4.


<div id="sec-15-8" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 15.8 Waterbody coverage

The framework is intended to apply to freshwater, estuarine and marine sediments. The supporting
material in this draft is overwhelmingly coastal and estuarine. Section 4.4 states the applicability,
measures what the current material actually supports, and sets out what is needed to close the gap.


<div id="sec-15-9" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 15.9 What closes each limitation

Every limitation above is scheduled work, not an open-ended unknown.

**These limitations are not equally serious, and they do not all mean the same thing for a
reviewer.** An unselected option is a normal condition of an options paper; worked values that do
not reproduce across the document are not. The middle column below says what each limitation means
for *this* review, so that the ones which should give a reviewer pause are distinguishable from the
ones which should not.

| Limitation | What it means for this review | Closed by | Plan timing |
|---|---|---|---|
| 15.1 No review | **Process condition - this review is what closes it.** Not a defect and nothing to work around. | Task 2.2 circulation; Task 9.2 full TWG and SSTAC review | Week 3; month 11 |
| 15.2 No BC data | **Evidence gap.** Direction can be discussed; no local-occurrence claim can be validated, and no substance can be prioritised on B.C. data. | Task 3.1 to 3.6 (BC Aquatic Database); Task 5.4 validation | Months 1 to 7; month 8 |
| 15.3 Drafted ahead of schedule | **Planned work.** The material is early, not wrong. Comment on it is welcome but not required for this review. | Tasks 4, 5, 6 and 7 research streams replace the early sketches | Months 4 to 9 |
| 15.4 Surfaces disagree | **Quality-assurance inconsistency - the strongest caution in this table.** Worked values do not reproduce across the document, so **no figure should be quoted, compared or used to weigh one option against another.** | Reconciliation to a single arithmetic; then Task 8.3, "resolve gaps and inconsistencies" | Now; month 9 |
| 15.5 Parameter provenance | **Evidence gap.** Direction can be discussed; a parameter cannot yet be selected. | Task 3.7 scope and templates; Task 3.8 compilation; Task 3.9 review and QA | Months 1 to 2; month 3 onward; month 8 |
| 15.6 No sediment background protocol | **Out of scope.** Protocol development sits outside Phase 2; the absence is a project boundary, not a deficiency in this draft. | Task 3.2 and 3.3 information needs and structure, aligned to Protocols 4 and 9 | Month 3 |
| 15.7 Legacy method comparisons | **Open option.** What the in-force method does and does not do bears on the equation-form and scope choices, and reviewers can advise on it directly. | Task 6.1 equation options; Task 7.1 Schedule 3.4 expansion scope | Months 1 to 3; month 6 |
| 15.8 Waterbody coverage | **Open option, with an evidence gap behind it.** The two-versus-three waterbody-class structure is a live choice reviewers can advise on; the freshwater parameters needed to populate it are not yet compiled. | Task 6.2, "define structure for the four pathways"; Task 3.8 parameter compilation | Months 6 to 7; month 3 onward |

**Read the classifications, not just the count.** Three of the eight - 15.1, 15.3 and 15.6 - should
not constrain a reviewer at all: one is closed by the act of reviewing, one marks material that is
early rather than unsound, and one is a deliberate project boundary. Two are open choices this
paper exists to put to reviewers. **The two that genuinely limit what can be relied on are 15.2 and
15.5, both evidence gaps, and 15.4, which is the reason no worked number in this draft should be
quoted.**

None of this work has been done. Saying so plainly is the point of this section.

<div id="sec-16-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 16.0 Phase 2 Deliverables and Technical Appendices

> **What would change this draft's status.** The deliverables listed above are the products Phase 2 is scoped to produce; none of them is complete, and this paper is the working draft of one of them. A document that could carry a recommendation still requires technical review, completed source and citation adjudication, and resolution of the policy and applicability questions carried forward in Section 18. Until those are addressed, no number in this paper is a proposed standard and none should be applied to a site.


The final Matrix Options Paper will be supported by detailed technical appendices that document the research completed during Phase 2. The main body of the paper is intended to provide synthesis, options analysis, and recommendations. The appendices are intended to provide the detailed technical basis for those conclusions.

This structure reflects the purpose of the Matrix Options Paper as the final Phase 2 deliverable. The paper will summarize research findings, progress made, options considered, and the rationale supporting recommendations, while the appendices will preserve the detailed technical work completed by ENV, SABCS, SSTAC, and TWG contributors.

The appendices should be developed iteratively as Phase 2 work progresses. Early versions may contain outlines, preliminary findings, literature summaries, jurisdictional comparisons, or draft decision logic. As the workstreams are completed, these appendices should be updated to include finalized technical findings, options considered, uncertainties, and recommended approaches.

<div id="sec-16-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 16.1 Relationship Between Report Body and Appendices
The report body should answer the following questions:
* What issue is being addressed?
* What options were considered?
* How were the options evaluated?
* What are the key findings?
* What is the recommended or emerging preferred approach?
* What uncertainties or future work remain?

The appendices should answer the more detailed technical questions, including:
* What literature, data, guidance, or jurisdictional examples were reviewed?
* What methods were considered?
* What assumptions were used?
* What technical constraints were identified?
* What data gaps remain?
* What rationale supports the recommended approach?
* What information should be carried forward into Phase 3 standards derivation?

<div id="sec-16-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 16.2 Planned Technical Appendices
The following appendices are planned for the final Matrix Options Paper:
* **Appendix A: Bioavailability Adjustment Research Report**
* **Appendix B: Bioaccumulation Research Report**
* **Appendix C: Substance Classification Framework**
* **Appendix D: Substance Prioritization Framework**
* **Appendix E: Matrix Standards Derivation Options Review**
* **Appendix F: Generic Standards Adoption Procedure**
* **Appendix G: Proposed BC Aquatic Database: Design and Requirements**
* **Appendix H: Input Parameter Inventory and Selection Options**
* **Appendix I: Engagement Summary**
* **Appendix K: Jurisdictional Derivation Basis Compendium**

<div id="sec-16-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

- **Appendix L** - Phase 2 Project Plan V2 (approved working draft; planning instrument only)

### 16.3 Appendix Development Process
The appendices should be developed in parallel with the Phase 2 workstreams. Each appendix should initially be created as a structured working document, then updated as research findings are completed and reviewed. Where a workstream produces a standalone technical report, that report may be inserted directly as the relevant appendix or summarized in the appendix with the full report attached as supporting material.

The final Matrix Options Paper should not duplicate all technical details in the main body. Instead, the body should synthesize the technical appendices and clearly explain how the findings support the options analysis and recommendations.

<div id="sec-17-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 17.0 Phased Path Forward

The sediment standards modernization work is being advanced through a phased process. This paper should distinguish clearly between completed work, current Phase 2 work, and future standards derivation work.

<div id="sec-17-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 17.1 Phase 1: Project Initiation and Scoping
Phase 1 was completed through preliminary literature review, jurisdictional scan, engagement, and development of the Phase 1 White Paper.

<div id="sec-17-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 17.2 Phase 2: Research, Framework Development, and Matrix Options Paper
Phase 2 is the current phase. It focuses on developing the research, framework architecture, classification and prioritization methods, database foundation, input parameter compilation, generic standards procedure, and final Matrix Options Paper.

<div id="sec-17-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 17.3 Phase 3: Application of Framework and Standards Derivation
A future phase is expected to use the Phase 2 framework, data, and input parameters to calculate preliminary toxicity-based standards for prioritized substances.

Future ENV policy, consultation, approval, and regulatory processes would occur separately from the technical scope of this Phase 2 Options Paper.
