<div id="sec-18-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 18.0 Preliminary Regulatory Integration Outline

> **Provenance and status of the legislative content in this section.** The statutory and regulatory references below - section numbers of the *Contaminated Sites Regulation* and the *Environmental Management Act*, B.C. Reg. numbers, dates in force, and quoted statutory text - were read from the official BC Laws consolidations during drafting. **They have not been checked by legislative counsel, and nothing in this section is legal advice.** The section describes how the concept in Section 4.0 *could* be carried into the regulation; it does not state that any amendment has been drafted, proposed, approved or scheduled, and no such step has occurred. **Every statutory citation in this section must be verified by legislative counsel against the consolidation current at the time of review, before this draft is circulated outside the project.**

This section outlines how the three-part Schedule 3.4 concept described in Section 4.0 could be
carried into the *Contaminated Sites Regulation* (CSR), which provisions of the existing legal
framework it would touch, and what a future regulatory package would need from this project. It is
an outline of the route, not a proposal of regulatory text.

Nothing in this section has been reviewed, endorsed, adopted or decided. Regulatory amendments are
expressly out of scope for Phase 2 (Project Plan section 2.2, "Out of Scope: Regulatory
amendments"), as are final numeric standards and supporting technical guidance or protocols. The
decision whether to amend the CSR, the content and timing of any amendment, and the policy,
consultation and approval processes that would precede it belong to ENV. This paper recommends only.
Where a question below cannot be answered from the project record it is stated rather than
resolved, and carried forward in the consolidated list at Section 18.6.


<div id="sec-18-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 18.1 The three-part structure and how its parts relate

The Phase 2 Project Plan's vision (plan section 1.3) is a Schedule 3.4 in three parts:

| Part | What it would contain | Which substances | How values would be produced |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Part 1** | Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards protecting human and ecological health, over four receptor-pathways: human health / direct exposure; human health / exposure in food; ecological health / direct exposure; ecological health / exposure in food | Priority substances that pose a threat requiring a high duty of care, identified by considering biomagnification, toxicity, persistence and local occurrence | Derived using the equations, input parameters and exposure assumptions this project is developing (Tasks 3.7-3.9, 6.1-6.6), applied in a later phase |
| **Part 2** | Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to protect human health | All other substances | Adopted or adapted from other jurisdictions (for example United States, Australia, New Zealand) through the procedure being developed under Task 7.2 |
| **Part 3** | Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to protect ecological health | All other substances | The same adoption procedure as Part 2 |

The relationship between the parts is one of coverage versus refinement. Matrix standards are the
refined, pathway-specific route, and are costly: each requires substance-specific toxicity,
bioaccumulation, partitioning and exposure information. Generic standards are the coverage route,
extending Schedule 3.4 to substances for which matrix derivation is not feasible, not warranted, or
not supported by adequate data. Neither part alone produces a workable schedule.

**The CSR already contains this architecture, for soil.** Schedule 3.1 is drafted in exactly three
parts: Part 1 is a set of per-substance matrix tables headed "MATRIX *n* - NUMERICAL SOIL STANDARDS";
Part 2 is "GENERIC NUMERICAL SOIL STANDARDS TO PROTECT HUMAN HEALTH"; Part 3 is "GENERIC NUMERICAL
SOIL STANDARDS TO PROTECT ECOLOGICAL HEALTH". The operative plumbing is already drafted around it:
CSR s. 1 defines "matrix numerical soil standard" by reference to Part 1 of Schedule 3.1 and "generic
numerical soil standard" by reference to Part 2 or 3; and ss. 11(1)(a) and 17(1)(a) each engage
"(i) the applicable generic numerical soil standard, or (ii) the lowest value of the applicable
matrix numerical soil standards".

Each Schedule 3.1 Part 1 matrix is a single substance-specific table whose **rows** are site-specific
factors grouped under the headings "HUMAN HEALTH PROTECTION" and "ENVIRONMENTAL PROTECTION" (for
example "Intake of contaminated soil", "Groundwater used for drinking water", "Toxicity to soil
invertebrates and plants", "Livestock ingesting soil and fodder") and whose **columns** are the eight
land uses. That is the same shape as the sediment matrix this project is designing: four
receptor-pathway rows against the proposed Sediment Use Category columns.

This has a practical consequence worth stating plainly for ENV: **the three-part concept is not novel
regulatory drafting.** It is the extension to sediment of a structure the CSR already uses for soil
and has used since 1 November 2017. The drafting question is whether Schedule 3.4 should follow the
Schedule 3.1 model, not whether such a model can be drafted.

Two design parameters are fixed for this working draft; a third is not resolved:

* **Dimensioning.** Four receptor-pathways against the four Sediment Use Categories proposed in
  Section 4.1 gives 16 exposure scenarios per substance as the initial working set. This is the
  starting point for the options analysis, not a conclusion; the number of scenarios that survive
  will depend on Task 6.3 (triggers and decision logic) and on which combinations are found to be
  meaningful.
* **Waterbody types.** Standards are intended to apply to freshwater, estuarine and marine
  sediments. Schedule 3.4 today is a four-column table: Freshwater Sediment Standard for Sensitive
  Use, Freshwater for Typical Use, Marine and Estuarine for Sensitive Use, Marine and Estuarine for
  Typical Use. Estuarine sediment is presently grouped with marine.
* **How the two dimensions combine in a printed schedule is not resolved.** If waterbody type
  multiplies the 16 scenarios, a per-substance Part 1 table becomes large; if estuarine continues
  to be grouped with marine, the reason for that grouping should be stated rather than inherited.
  This is a Task 7.3 / 7.4 question ("Align matrix framework outputs with CSR structure"; "Develop
  concept for multi-pathway standards structure"), and is carried forward for TWG and SSTAC as
  question 2 in Section 18.6.

Applicability is settled in principle as a two-step rule. Direct-contact pathways are mandatory within the framework. Food pathways are conditional on the applicable criteria and site conditions. All standards that apply apply; the lowest applicable value may be used as a practical remediation focus, but it is not a hierarchy that displaces other applicable standards. Site assignment remains within the CSR framework and is not re-opened as an option in this paper. Protocols 18 and 21 are not sediment authority and may be used only as analogical models where clearly identified as such.


<div id="sec-18-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 18.2 The statutory framework that sediment standards sit inside

The instrument is the ***Contaminated Sites Regulation*, B.C. Reg. 375/96**, made under the
*Environmental Management Act*, S.B.C. 2003, c. 53, ss. 62, 63, 138 and 139; deposited 16 December
1996 and effective 1 April 1997. [STATUS: SOURCE GAP - current currency and amendment details require direct verification against the in-force B.C. Laws instrument; no legal conclusion is relied on here.] Schedule 3.4 is titled "Generic Numerical Sediment Standards" and carries
the editorial note *[en. B.C. Reg. 133/2022, App., s. 12, as am. B.C. Reg. 2/2023, s. 1 (e).]* Its
own footnotes state that the freshwater values "are specific to the protection of freshwater life"
and the marine and estuarine values "are specific to the protection of marine and estuarine aquatic
life" - which is the schedule's own confirmation of the coverage gap this project exists to address.

The provisions that a change to Schedule 3.4 would engage:

| Provision | Marginal heading | What it does | Why it matters here |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **CSR s. 11(1)(c)** | Definition of contaminated site | A site is contaminated if "the concentration of any substance in sediment at the site is greater than the applicable generic numerical sediment standard" | Schedule 3.4 is the site-determination trigger. Any change to it changes which sites are contaminated sites |
| **CSR s. 11(1)(d)** | Definition of contaminated site | For a substance **not** specified in Schedule 3.1, 3.2, 3.3 or 3.4, the trigger is the concentration specified for that substance (and use, if specified with one) in a director's interim standard | The route by which unscheduled substances can be brought into site determination without amending the schedule |
| **CSR s. 11(2), (3)** | Definition of contaminated site | s. 11(1) does not apply where the concentration is not greater than the applicable site-specific numerical standard, or not greater than the local background concentration | Connects the standards to the background work in Task 3 and to the plan's requirement that standards not sit below natural background |
| **CSR s. 17(1)(b.1)** | Application of numerical standards for remediation | A site is remediated to the numerical standards if "the sediment at the site does not contain any substance with a concentration greater than the generic numerical sediment standard for that substance for the applicable sediment use of the site" | Schedule 3.4 is also the remediation endpoint. The same values do two jobs |
| **CSR s. 17(1)(c)** | Application of numerical standards for remediation | The same test against "the concentration specified for that substance and use in a director's interim standard" | Interim standards can serve as a remediation endpoint as well as a determination trigger |
| **CSR s. 18** | Application of risk-based standards for remediation | The alternative route: remediation standards are met where lifetime cancer risk and hazard index are acceptable, either on a medical health officer's recommendation (s. 18(1)) or, without that review, at $\le 1 \times 10^{-5}$ incremental lifetime cancer risk and a hazard index $\le 1$ (s. 18(3)) | This is the route much sediment work uses today precisely because the numerical standards are pathway-limited. Improving Schedule 3.4 is intended to reduce, not remove, reliance on it |
| **CSR s. 68** | Review of numerical standards | The minister "may, within 5 years of this section coming into force and once every 5 years after that date, consider the recommendations by a director made under subsection (2) ... and conduct a review of the numerical standards". Those recommendations "are to be developed in accordance with a protocol established under (a) section 64 (2) (n) of the Act, and (b) section 67 (e) of this regulation" | The standing statutory review cycle. It is the natural destination for this project's output, and it names the vehicle: a director's recommendation developed under a protocol |
| **CSR s. 67(e)** | Director's protocols | A director may establish protocols in respect of "developing soil, sediment, water or vapour numerical standards for a substance" | Sediment is named. This is the existing authority under which a sediment standards derivation methodology could be published |
| **EMA s. 64(2)(n)** | Director's protocols | Protocols may be established in respect of "developing soil, sediment and water numerical standards" | A parallel head to CSR s. 67(e), not its source. CSR s. 67 is made under s. 64(1), and s. 67(e) is the wider of the two - it adds "or vapour" and "for a substance". CSR s. 68(2) requires a protocol established under both |

**The Act does not confine sediment standards to ecological receptors.** The absence of human
health sediment standards is a choice made in the regulation and in the protocol, not a limit imposed
by the *Environmental Management Act*. Section 39 brings sediment inside the definition of a
contaminated site - "an area of the land in which the soil or any groundwater lying beneath it, **or
the water or the underlying sediment**, contains a prescribed substance in quantities or
concentrations exceeding prescribed risk based or numerical (a) criteria, (b) standards, or (c)
conditions". Section 1 (1) defines "remediation" as action to counteract or remove a contaminant "or
the adverse effects on the environment **or human health** of any contaminant", and "environment" as
"air, land, water and all other external conditions or influences under which **humans**, animals and
plants live or are developed". Section 56 (1) (a) makes "any potential for adverse effects on **human
health** or for pollution of the environment" a mandatory factor in selecting remediation options.
Neither of the two regulation-making heads that produce sediment standards carries a receptor
qualifier: s. 63 (1) (n) authorises "prescribing substances and risk based or numerical criteria,
standards and conditions for the purposes of the definition of 'contaminated site' in section 39",
and s. 64 (2) (n) authorises protocols for "developing soil, sediment and water numerical standards".
**On the face of these provisions the constraint is regulatory and protocol-level, not statutory.**

CSR s. 11(1)(d) is limited to substances *not* specified in Schedules 3.1 to 3.4, while
s. 17(1)(c) contains no equivalent limitation on its face. Whether a director's interim standard
can operate for a substance that is already in Schedule 3.4 is a question of construction this
paper does not attempt to answer. It is carried forward as question 7 in Section 18.6, for the
internal policy and legal review at Task 7.6.


<div id="sec-18-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 18.3 How a change to Schedule 3.4 would actually be made

Schedule 3.4 is part of a regulation, not a statute. It is changed by making a regulation, and it has
been changed by regulation twice.

1. **Creation.** Schedule 3.4 was created by B.C. Reg. 253/2016, s. 17 - "The attached
   Schedules 2.1, 3.1, 3.2, 3.3 and 3.4 are added" - as amended by B.C. Reg. 196/2017 (Ministerial
   Order M366/2017), effective 1 November 2017. The same instrument, at s. 18, repealed
   "Schedules 4 to 7 and 9 to 11", which removed the former Schedule 9, "Generic Numerical Sediment
   Criteria" (added in 2004 by B.C. Reg. 324/2004, s. 70). The same instrument also added CSR
   s. 67(e), CSR s. 68 (review of numerical standards) and CSR s. 70 (transition).
2. **Re-enactment.** The Schedule 3.4 now in force was substituted by B.C. Reg. 133/2022, App.,
   s. 12 - "Schedules 3.1 to 3.4 are repealed and the attached Schedules 3.1 to 3.4 are
   substituted" - as amended by B.C. Reg. 2/2023, s. 1 (e), effective 1 March 2023. B.C. Reg.
   133/2022 was deposited 6 June 2022 under the *Environmental Management Act*, ss. 63 and 139, by
   Ministerial Order M164/2022.

Four points follow, and they are the substance of what "policy-ready" means for this project.

**One possible mechanism is whole-schedule substitution by ministerial order; the instrument route remains for ENV policy and legislative-counsel confirmation.** Both times, the schedule was
replaced entire rather than amended value by value, on the authority of the minister under EMA
s. 63(1)(n) ("prescribing substances and risk based or numerical criteria, standards and conditions
for the purposes of the definition of 'contaminated site' in section 39"), with EMA s. 139 applying
to the making of the regulation. A three-part Schedule 3.4 would be a substitution of the same kind.
That is why the structural work under Tasks 7.1, 7.3 and 7.4 matters as much as the numbers: the
schedule would be re-drafted as a whole, so its architecture has to be settled before any values are
placed in it.

**There is a standing review cycle to feed.** CSR s. 68 gives the minister a recurring five-year
opportunity to review the numerical standards on a director's recommendation. It is the cleanest
statement of ENV's existing mandate to revisit Schedule 3.4, and it is the reason this work does not
depend on a new authority being created. Where the current five-year cycle stands, and whether a review is
anticipated within a period relevant to Phase 3, must come from ENV; this paper assumes no date.
This is carried forward as question 5 in Section 18.6.

**A protocol is likely a companion, not an alternative.** CSR s. 68(2) requires the director's
recommendations to be developed in accordance with a protocol established under **both**
EMA s. 64(2)(n) - "developing soil, sediment and water numerical standards" - and CSR
s. 67(e) - "developing soil, sediment, water or vapour numerical standards for a
substance". The two are parallel heads rather than one deriving from the other, and the
CSR head is the wider of them.
Standards derivation methods for the CSR are already published in that form, as Protocol 28.
Producing or amending such a protocol is expressly out of scope for Phase 2, but the framework this
project develops is the kind of content that would sit in one. Whether the sediment derivation methodology is intended to
be published as a protocol, and if so under which protocol number, is for ENV to determine, and is
carried forward as question 6 in Section 18.6.

**Transition is a real drafting item.** CSR s. 70 shows the form used last time: submissions made in
substantial compliance before 1 November 2017 could still be assessed against the standards then in
effect. A future amendment would need its own transition treatment, and its effect on site
determinations, approvals in principle, certificates of compliance and remediation already in
progress would have to be worked through. Transition treatment is for ENV with legislative counsel and is out
of scope for Phase 2, as Section 16.3 records and as Section 18.6 restates among the things Phase 2
will not produce. It is set out here so that it is not discovered late.


<div id="sec-18-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 18.4 Director's Interim Standards

The Project Plan names Director's Interim Standards twice - in the Part 2 objective (plan section
1.4) and in Task 7.2 - as work the generic adoption procedure can leverage. The mechanism is set out
below because the paper has not previously described it.

* **The power.** *Environmental Management Act* **s. 63.1(1)**: "A director may make regulations
  prescribing substances and risk based or numerical criteria, standards and conditions for the
  purpose of the definition of 'contaminated site' in section 39 if the director considers it
  necessary in the public interest."
* **Priority.** s. 63.1(2): in the event of a conflict with a regulation under s. 63(1)(n) - the
  power under which Schedule 3.4 is made - "the regulation under this section prevails".
* **Duration.** s. 63.1(3): "A regulation under subsection (1) ceases to have effect one year after
  the date it was made." ENV's published description is consistent: temporary standards "valid for
  one year unless incorporated into the regulation".
* **The CSR hooks.** CSR s. 1 defines "director's interim standards" by reference to EMA s. 63.1 and
  includes them in the defined term "numerical standards". They operate at **s. 11(1)(d)** (site
  determination, for substances not specified in Schedules 3.1 to 3.4) and **s. 17(1)(c)**
  (remediation).

Why this matters to Task 7.2. An interim standard is the same kind of act the generic adoption
procedure is being designed to support: a director settling a numerical value for a substance that
Schedule 3.4 does not cover. The reasoning behind existing interim standards - which source
jurisdiction was used, at what protection level, for which species, and what adjustment was applied -
is therefore directly relevant precedent for the Parts 2 and 3 adoption procedure, and the interim
standard is a mechanism by which a value can operate while the schedule catches up. The one-year
expiry in s. 63.1(3) means an interim standard is a bridge, not a destination.

**The list of Director's Interim Standards currently or recently in effect, and the supporting
rationale for each, must come from ENV, and Task 7.2 cannot be completed without it.** No
published register was located: CSR s. 69 prescribes a manner of publication for *protocols*
only, and neither ENV's "Standards for contaminated sites" page nor its "Selecting numerical
standards" page links to a list. It has to be obtained internally rather than inferred, and until
it is, **this paper does not characterise what interim standards exist, how many there are, or
what methods they used.** This is carried forward as question 8 in Section 18.6.


<div id="sec-18-5" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 18.5 What a future regulatory package would need, and where it comes from

The table maps each component a regulatory package would require to the Phase 2 task that produces
it. Timings are the plan's own task timings and are planning estimates, not commitments.

| Component of a future package | Phase 2 task | Plan timing |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Scope of the three-part structure (parts, pathways, substances) | 7.1 | Month 6 |
| Alignment of matrix framework outputs with CSR structure | 7.3 | Month 6 |
| Concept for the multi-pathway standards structure | 7.4 | Month 6 |
| Equation options for each of the four receptor-pathways | 6.1 | Months 1-3 |
| Structure defined for the four pathways | 6.2 | Months 6-7 |
| Triggers and decision logic (applicability) per pathway | 6.3 | Month 7 |
| Integrated matrix framework architecture (equations, structure, triggers) | 6.5 | Month 8 |
| Input parameter scope and templates (TRVs for human and ecological health, grain size thresholds, receptor characteristics, exposure assumptions, water lot use classes) | 3.7 | Months 1-2 |
| Compiled input parameter values, information and references | 3.8 | Month 3 onward |
| Review and QA of the input parameter compilation | 3.9 | Month 8 |
| Bioavailability adjustment approach for BC sediments | 4.1-4.5 | Months 4-7 |
| Classification criteria and substance prioritization framework | 5.2-5.5 | Months 6-8 |
| Procedure for adopting generic values for Parts 2 and 3 | 7.2 | Months 7-8 |
| Occurrence and background data to test that standards are not set below natural background | 3.1-3.6 | Month 1 onward; database at Month 7 |
| This outline | 7.5 | Month 7 |
| Internal policy and legal review of the above | 7.6 | Month 8 |
| Cross-workstream integration and resolution of gaps | 8.1-8.3 | Month 9 |
| Final Matrix Options Paper | 9.1-9.3 | Months 10-12 |

What Phase 2 will **not** produce, and who would have to:

* **The numbers.** Phase 2 derives no standards. The plan's risk register directs the project to
  "reinforce the 'no standards derivation' boundary", and plan section 1.1 anticipates that a later
  phase "will utilize this framework to derive and calculate the new numerical standards for a
  subset of prioritized substances".
* **Regulation text and drafting instructions.** ENV, with legislative counsel.
* **Any consultation ENV determines is required.** ENV's own policy, consultation and approval
  processes, which are separate from and subsequent to this technical work.
* **A supporting protocol or technical guidance.** Out of scope (plan section 2.2); see section 16.3.
* **Transition provisions.** ENV, with legislative counsel; see section 16.3.
* **Analytical and reporting method specification.** Schedule 3.4 already relies on a director's
  protocol for at least one substance group (the dioxin and furan toxicity equivalent). Matrix
  standards expressed on a normalised basis would require the normalising measurement to be
  specified somewhere. Where that specification belongs is carried forward as question 10 in
  Section 18.6, for the internal policy and legal review at Task 7.6.
* **A regulatory impact or cost analysis, if one is required.** Whether a CSR schedule amendment
  requires any form of regulatory impact, cost or administrative burden analysis, in what form,
  and at what point, is not something the project record can answer; it must be confirmed
  internally with ENV. This is carried forward as question 9 in Section 18.6.

**The standing of future calculation work, for TWG and SSTAC.** No worked derived outputs are included in this Phase 2 paper. Candidate equations and input parameters are retained as options, with source gaps and review questions identified for later Phase 3 work. The separately preserved calculation material is not a current paper dependency and is not a regulatory package.


<div id="sec-18-6" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 18.6 Open questions carried forward

These are recorded here rather than resolved. Items 1 to 4 are for TWG and SSTAC discussion;
items 5 to 10 are for ENV, several of them for the internal policy and legal review at Task 7.6.

1. Which criteria and site conditions trigger each food pathway, after the mandatory direct-contact pathways are applied? All standards that apply apply; the lowest applicable value may be a practical remediation focus only. (Section 18.1)
2. How do the four Sediment Use Categories and the freshwater / estuarine / marine distinction
   combine in a printed schedule, and does the present grouping of estuarine with marine survive?
   (Section 18.1)
3. Do all 16 exposure scenarios survive Task 6.3, or does the decision logic reduce them?
   (Section 18.1)
4. Within the three-part structure the plan fixes, should Schedule 3.4 follow the Schedule 3.1
   **drafting** model - per-substance matrix tables in Part 1, generic tables in Parts 2 and 3?
   **The three-part structure itself is not in question** (Section 4.0); what is open is how it
   is rendered on the page. (Section 18.1)
5. Where does the CSR s. 68 five-year review cycle currently stand? (Section 18.3)
6. Is the sediment derivation methodology intended to be published as a director's protocol under
   CSR s. 67(e), and if so, as which protocol? (Section 18.3)
7. Can a director's interim standard operate for a substance already specified in Schedule 3.4,
   given the limitation in s. 11(1)(d) and its absence from s. 17(1)(c)? (Section 18.2)
8. What Director's Interim Standards are currently in effect, and what adoption reasoning supports
   them? No published register was located; this must come from ENV. (Section 18.4)
9. Does a CSR schedule amendment require a regulatory impact or cost analysis, and in what form?
   (Section 18.5)
10. Where must the normalising measurement be specified, for any standard expressed on a
    normalised basis - in the schedule itself, or in a director's protocol? Schedule 3.4 already
    relies on a director's protocol for one substance group. (Section 18.5)

---

<div id="sec-19-0" class="section-anchor"></div>

## 19.0 Conclusions and Next Steps


<div id="sec-19-1" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 19.1 What this paper concludes

British Columbia's current sediment standards do not provide a comprehensive framework for modern
sediment risk management. They address benthic direct exposure and do not systematically address
bioavailability, bioaccumulation, human health pathways, Indigenous food-based exposure, emerging
contaminants, or a structured approach to prioritizing substances.

**The gap is specific, and British Columbia's own record documents it.** *Protocol 28*, Chapter 7.2,
states that the in-force sediment standards "are derived based on exposure scenarios for ecological
organisms and are not considered protective of human health; therefore, CSR soil standards should be
applied to sediment for risk assessment purposes." *Protocol 1*, Version 5.0, section 4.1.1 states
the same requirement independently: "As there are currently no sediment standards protective of human
health, Schedule 3.1 Soil Standards for the protection of human health (Parts 1 and 2) must be used
to identify human health COPCs in sediment." Schedule 3.4's own footnotes confine every value to the
protection of aquatic life.

The standards therefore protect benthic organisms through direct contact; human health at sediment
sites is handled at the site-assessment layer rather than in the generic standard; and protection of
higher-trophic ecological receptors through the sediment food web is not addressed by the generic
standards at all. **Closing those two gaps is what a matrix framework is for, and this paper
concludes that it can be built.**

**The gap was identified, explained and deferred in 2003, by the programme that produced the
criteria.** This paper is not reporting an oversight. The *Technical Appendix* states at section 3.1
that the effects-based criteria "may not provide an adequate basis for assessing risks to piscivorous
wildlife or human health that are associated with food web transfer of persistent, bioaccumulative
substances", that "bioaccumulation-based SedQC are also needed", and that "such SedQC values are
largely unavailable at this time". The contracted derivation report is more explicit still: it
identifies effects-based criteria as the highest priority, records that bioaccumulative substances
were "targeted for establishing bioaccumulation-based SedQC and/or tissue residue criteria", and
states that "the SedQC for bioaccumulative substances will be established at a later date". The
method intended for them - the tissue residue approach, $SQG = TRG / BSAF$ - was one of the seven
evaluated in 2003 and is the algebra this framework's food pathways use. **What was unavailable then
was the input data, not the method**, and the 2003 Preface committed the agencies to "a periodic
review of these criteria, and the approach used to establish them, in order to ensure that they are
consistent with the best scientific information available at the time." **This project is that
review.** Appendix H sets the 2003 record out in full.

**The gap is not statutory.** As Section 18.2 sets out, the *Environmental Management Act* brings
sediment within the definition of a contaminated site, defines remediation by reference to adverse
effects "on the environment or human health", and attaches no receptor qualifier to either of the
regulation-making heads that produce sediment standards. The confinement to ecological receptors is
made in the regulation and in the protocol. **Extending Schedule 3.4 to human health pathways is a
regulatory and protocol-level change, not one that requires an amendment to the Act.**

**Several of the framework's inputs do not yet exist.** Section 15 records the limitations in full and
classifies each by its effect on a reviewer. Three bear on every number printed here: the worked
values were computed on conventions that do not correspond to the British Columbia risk basis and
have not been recomputed; a number of exposure and bioaccumulation parameters are carried without a
traceable source, and are identified as such where they appear; and the conventions a *generic*
standard requires - hours on site and exposure frequency among them - are ones the federal guidance
expressly declines to supply, so this framework must declare and defend its own. **No number in this
paper is a proposed standard, and none should be read as one.**


<div id="sec-19-2" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 19.2 What this paper recommends

**The recommendation is methodological. It concerns how modernized sediment standards should be
derived, not what any standard should be.** Each element below rests on an instrument or a source
identified in the body of this paper.

1. **Adopt a four-pathway architecture** - human health direct contact, human health country foods,
   ecological direct contact, and ecological food-web bioaccumulation - as the structure within which
   sediment standards are derived. The two pathways absent from the present schedule are the two the
   record identifies as missing.

2. **Carry the derivation in a three-part Schedule 3.4**: matrix numerical standards for priority
   substances, generic numerical standards for human health, and generic numerical standards for
   ecological health. This preserves the existing ecological standards rather than displacing them.

3. **Treat soil standards as the default read-across basis for sediment human health**, and state the
   reasoning explicitly. Both *Protocol 28* section 7.2 and *Protocol 1* v5.0 section 4.1.1 already
   direct that CSR soil standards be applied to sediment for human health purposes. A receptor is
   exposed to soil or to sediment at a given location, not to both simultaneously, so applying the
   same logic to sediment that British Columbia applies to soil is the position that requires the
   least new assumption. Where a sediment-specific basis is better supported, it should displace the
   default on stated evidence.

4. **Carry the source allocation factor as an explicit, separately named term in the derivation
   equation**, rather than folding it into the toxicity reference value. The toxicological quantity
   and the policy allocation are different things, are set by different processes, and should each be
   variable without obscuring the other. *Protocol 28* applies an apportionment to soil; the same
   treatment applied to sediment is set out at Section 9.5.3.2, and the equations at Section 9.5 carry
   it as a distinct factor for that reason.

5. **Adjust for background as a separate downstream stage**, applied to the preliminary
   toxicity-based standard, rather than by subtracting an estimated daily intake from the reference
   value. This follows the three-stage structure *Protocol 28* uses for soil. Section 15.6 records
   that no British Columbia protocol for sediment background concentrations exists to support that
   stage, and that a transferability review of the soil and groundwater methods is the work required.

6. **Where British Columbia publishes no parameter, take the Health Canada value, and record the
   referral chain.** This is not a preference. *Protocol 1* v5.0 section 4.3.1.1 requires that the
   relative absorption factors referenced in Health Canada (2024) be used; that document directs the
   reader to the Health Canada dermal relative absorption factor table. *Protocol 1* v5.0 section
   4.4.1.2 lists the Health Canada toxicity reference values ahead of the United States sources.
   **The ordering this paper applies is the ordering British Columbia's current protocol sets out.**

7. **Publish the derivation methodology as a protocol.** CSR s. 68(2) requires that a director's
   recommendation for a review of numerical standards be developed in accordance with a protocol
   established under EMA s. 64(2)(n) and CSR s. 67(e). Standards derivation methods for the CSR are
   already published in that form, as *Protocol 28*. The framework this project develops is the kind
   of content that would sit in one. Producing or amending such a protocol is outside the scope of
   Phase 2; what is recommended here is the destination, not that this phase carry out the work.

**What supports this recommendation, and what does not.** Items 1 and 2 are structural proposals tested against the project plan and the existing schedule. Items 3 through 7 require the cited instruments and primary-source verification. No worked derived output supports the recommendation; future numerical work is separate and cannot substitute for source verification.


<div id="sec-19-3" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 19.3 What this paper does not recommend

**No numerical selection is recommended.** The option sets in Section 9 carry preliminary selections
and the reasons given for them; a preliminary selection is not a recommendation, and none is promoted
to one here. That choice belongs to the sediment standards lead, informed by the Technical Working
Group and by the review this paper is written to gather.

**Applicability is settled in principle as a two-step rule.** Direct-contact pathways are mandatory within the framework. Food pathways are conditional on applicable criteria and site conditions. All standards that apply apply; the lowest applicable value may be used as a practical remediation focus, but it does not displace other applicable standards. Site assignment remains within the CSR framework and is not re-opened as an option in this paper.

**Nor are the number of waterbody classes, the prioritization weighting, or the species that
parameterize the food pathways.** Each is identified in Section 18.6 and listed below.


<div id="sec-19-4" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 19.4 What reviewers and the Technical Working Group are asked to test

The paper's value depends on these being contested rather than accepted:

1. **The parameter authority for human direct contact.** Section 9.5 sets out three options and
   states a preliminary selection. Two of the option sets apply soil parameters under sediment
   labels; the third adopts the federal sediment guidance and requires a change to the intake
   expression.
2. **The apportionment convention.** Section 9.5 and Appendix H set out how much of the tolerable
   intake one exposure route may consume, on what basis, and what British Columbia's practice for
   other media has been.
3. **The structure of the ecological food pathway**, and which receptors it protects.
4. **Whether the schedule carries two waterbody classes or three**, discussed at Section 4.4.2, which
   changes the size of the parameter set.
5. **The prioritization weighting.** No numerical weighting set is adopted. Any future prioritization method must identify sources, dimensions, weights, thresholds, missing-data rules, and sensitivity tests before review.
6. **The conventions this framework must declare for itself**, where no published default exists.


<div id="sec-19-5" class="section-anchor"></div>

### 19.5 What happens after this paper, and what is outside its scope

This paper sets out options and carries a recommendation on how to approach developing modernized
sediment standards. Deriving the values themselves is subsequent work. Public and Indigenous
consultation on any resulting standards occur through the ministry's policy development framework,
**not through this project**, and nothing in this paper substitutes for them. **This paper is an
input to that sequence, not a step in it.**

**The boundary this paper sits on.** The procedures and options set out here are intended to support
a science-based recommendation. They are not intended to be the sole basis on which standards are
established: the standards ultimately adopted may weigh feasibility, cost, implementation capacity
and the outcome of consultation alongside the science, and those are legitimate considerations that
this paper does not attempt to settle. What the paper does ask is that wherever such a judgement is
made, it is made visibly - that the science-based value and the adopted value are both on the record,
along with the reason for any difference between them.

---

# Technical Appendices Compendium
## Comprehensive Research Monographs Supporting the Matrix Options Paper

The following appendices provide the detailed scientific, empirical, and mathematical foundation supporting the Matrix Options Paper:

---

<div id="app-a" class="section-anchor"></div>

# Appendix A: Bioavailability Research Report

> **[STATUS: SOURCE-GAP QUARANTINE - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]** Source-specific claims involving Di Toro, Ghosh, cancer slope factors, and other decision-driving scientific inputs in this appendix are not accepted as verified inputs. The appendix remains a research record and option map only; exact primary-source locations, scope, units, and applicability must be verified before reuse.
## Mechanistic Foundations, Geochemical Phase Partitioning, and Tiered Bioavailability Implementation for Modernized British Columbia Matrix Sediment Standards

> **Purpose.** The geochemical and mathematical basis for treating sediment standards as bioavailability-informed rather than bulk-concentration values.
> **Supports.** Sections 7.1 and 9.1.  **Material.** Evidentiary support.
> **What to look at.** Section 1.2's foundational findings and Section 8's three-tier architecture options - these are what the body draws on.
> **Principal limitations, in this appendix's own words.** Material resting on coastal-marine literature and test species has *not been checked against B.C. freshwater systems*; the AVS-SEM result is a Pathway 3 finding only and says nothing about Pathways 2 or 4; several citations remain flagged in-text as unconfirmed.


**Document type:** Supporting technical appendix to the Matrix Sediment Standards Options Paper (Sections 7.1 and 9.1).  
**Status: DRAFT.** Prepared under Task 2 of the Sediment Standards Project, Phase 2 (2026-2027), an ENV Site Remediation Program project in partnership with SABCS. **Nothing in this appendix has been reviewed, endorsed, validated or adopted by anyone** - not ENV, not SABCS, not the SSTAC, not the Technical Working Group. No review cycle has taken place.  
**How to read this appendix:** the decision rules, tier definitions, operating rules and worked values set out below are draft proposals for discussion. They are not current regulatory requirements, and no numerical value in this appendix is a proposed or final standard.

## 1.0 Executive Summary and Scope

**Chapter status.** The scope and objective subsection and the summary of foundational scientific findings are drafted. A structure-and-roadmap subsection is not yet written.

### 1.1 Scope and Objective of the Bioavailability Monograph
The British Columbia Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV), in partnership with the Science Advisory Board for Contaminated Sites (SABCS), is carrying out Phase 2 (2026-2027) of the Sediment Standards Project under the *Environmental Management Act* (EMA, S.B.C. 2003, c. 53) and the *Contaminated Sites Regulation* (CSR, B.C. Reg. 375/96, as amended to B.C. Reg. 102/2026). **Phase 2's deliverable is the framework, including options for that framework - not the standards themselves.** ENV will subsequently follow its internal policy, consultation, and approval processes before recommending any regulatory changes to the CSR.

**Environments covered.** B.C.'s sediment standards apply to freshwater, estuarine and marine environments, and this appendix is written for all three. Where the material below rests on coastal-marine literature, examples or test species, that limitation is real and is noted at the point of use: section 3.5 (black carbon in B.C. harbours and estuaries), section 7.5 (RSET marine bioaccumulation test species), section 10.0 (cold-water desorption in coastal fjords and deep marine water) and section 6.3 (seafood preparation) draw on coastal-marine sources and have not been checked against B.C. freshwater systems.

The primary objective of this monograph is to provide the exhaustive scientific, geochemical, and mathematical foundation for modernizing CSR Schedule 3.4 from an outdated single-list empirical table into a scientifically defensible Matrix Sediment Standards System. 

Bulk dry-weight contaminant concentrations in aquatic sediment fail to predict biological effects because toxic substances are sequestered by distinct geochemical binding phases. For non-ionic organic contaminants, toxicity is governed by the freely dissolved porewater concentration ($C_{free}$ or $C_{pw}$), which is controlled by partitioning to total organic carbon ($f_{oc}$) and carbonaceous geosorbents (black carbon). For cationic divalent metals, toxicity in anoxic/suboxic horizons is neutralized by stoichiometric precipitation with Acid Volatile Sulfide (AVS) and secondary binding to organic carbon. For human health exposure pathways, biological uptake is modified by in vitro oral bioaccessibility ($IVBA$), dermal permeability ($RAF_{dermal}$), and culinary preparation factors.

This monograph establishes the quantitative algorithms, operational boundary conditions, analytical verification and validation standards, and regulatory decision rules supporting Section 7.1 (Bioavailability Adjustment) and Section 9.1 (Bioavailability Adjustment Options) of the Master Options Paper.

- Criterion: Black Carbon (BC) Freundlich Sorption
- Criterion: Divalent Metals: AVS-SEM Stoichiometry Sigma[SEM] - [AVS] <= 0 ==> Non-Toxic
- Criterion: Excess SEM Normalization: (Sigma[SEM]-AVS)/f_oc

- Criterion: Dermal Absorption: RAF_dermal Modifiers

### 1.2 Summary of Foundational Scientific Findings
1. **The Empirical Fallacy:** British Columbia's current sediment standards - derived in the early 2000s from CCME (1999) probable effect levels and carried into CSR Schedule 3.4 in 2017 with the numbers unchanged - rest on empirical co-occurrence benchmarks that do not account for bioavailability. This produces widespread false positives (triggering costly and ecologically damaging dredging where contaminants are inertly bound in sulfide/carbon matrices) and dangerous false negatives (failing to protect against bioaccumulation in low-TOC or oxic environments).
2. **Equilibrium Partitioning (EqP) Validation:** Over three decades of peer-reviewed toxicological research (Di Toro et al. 1991; US EPA 2003, 2012; McGrath et al. 2019) demonstrate that biological responses in benthic infauna correlate directly with freely dissolved porewater concentrations ($C_{free}$) and interstitial chemical activity. Organic carbon normalization ($f_{oc}$) is valid across the empirical domain of $0.2\% \le f_{oc} \le 10.0\%$.
3. **AVS-SEM Stoichiometry for Cationic Metals:** For divalent transition metals ($Cd^{2+}, Cu^{2+}, Ni^{2+}, Pb^{2+}, Zn^{2+}$) and monovalent silver ($Ag^+$), when the molar concentration of Acid Volatile Sulfide exceeds Simultaneously Extracted Metals ($\Sigma\text{SEM} - \text{AVS} \le 0$), porewater metal activities are thermodynamically suppressed to near-zero levels, preventing acute and chronic benthic toxicity (US EPA 2005). When SEM exceeds AVS, toxicity is prevented if excess SEM normalized to organic carbon is below $130\text{ }\mu\text{mol/g-OC}$.
4. **Black Carbon Dual-Mode Sorption:** In urban, industrial, and historical combustion-impacted waterways, soot, charcoal, and coal particles exhibit sorption affinities ($K_{BC}$) that are 10 to 100 times stronger than amorphous organic carbon ($K_{AOC}$), dramatically reducing bioavailability below generic EqP predictions (Accardi-Dey and Gschwend 2002; Cornelissen et al. 2005; Ghosh et al. 2014).
5. **Passive Sampling Verification:** In situ and ex situ polymer passive sampling (SPME, POM, LDPE) directly measures $C_{free}$ without matrix disruption, serving as the gold-standard Tier 3 verification tool.
6. **Draft preferred option for discussion (Option B - Tiered Hybrid Architecture):** A 3-Tier implementation framework combining Tier 1 Generic Matrix Standards (derived at default $1.0\%$ TOC and baseline AVS), Tier 2 Site-Specific Normalization (using routine commercial lab TOC and AVS-SEM data within defined bounds), and Tier 3 Advanced Site-Specific Assessment (passive sampling, porewater extraction, Biotic Ligand Modeling, and sediment bioassays).

## 2.0 Historical Baseline, Problem Formulation & The Empirical Fallacy

### 2.1 The Waste Management Act (WMA) Groundwork (1999)
The earliest formal effort to develop sediment quality criteria in British Columbia was carried out under the former *Waste Management Act* and documented in *Criteria for managing contaminated sediment in British Columbia* (MacDonald, D.D. and Macfarlane, M., 1999, prepared pursuant to section 26(a) of the *Waste Management Act* for the B.C. Ministry of Environment, Lands and Parks), updated by Macfarlane and MacDonald (2002).

That work recognized the theoretical importance of equilibrium partitioning and organic carbon normalization for non-ionic organic substances. However, given the data available at the time regarding organic carbon partition coefficients ($K_{oc}$), laboratory standardization of Acid Volatile Sulfides, and field bioaccumulation models, generic EqP standards were not established across all substances. Interim biological effects screening values based on co-occurrence databases (the National Status and Trends Program [NSTP] and Environment Canada BEDS database) were used instead. **[Checked. The characterisation has been verified against the B.C. Ministry of Water, Land and Air Protection *Technical Appendix* (2003), the official record of the derivation. That document describes seven approaches evaluated and a four-step strategy developed by the Federal/Provincial Technical Committee (section 3.3); it does not describe a default to co-occurrence databases, does not mention the BEDS database anywhere, and cites the National Status and Trends Program only as a bibliography entry. The earlier attribution to a November 1995 report of that title is confirmed unlocatable and has been removed throughout.]**

### 2.2 The 2003-2004 Derivation: Bioavailability and Food Chains Outside Its Scope
British Columbia's generic numerical sediment criteria were developed in the early 2000s and documented in *Criteria for Managing Contaminated Sediment in British Columbia: Technical Appendix* (B.C. Ministry of Water, Land and Air Protection [MWLAP], 2003). They entered the *Contaminated Sites Regulation* as Schedule 9, Generic Numerical Sediment Criteria, added by B.C. Reg. 324/2004, s. 70, under the *Environmental Management Act*. The derivation took the Canadian Council of Ministers of the Environment (CCME) 1999 **Probable Effect Levels (PELs)** as preliminary benchmarks and refined them using mean PEL-quotients and logistic regression against the Biological Effects Database for Sediments (BEDS): PELs were multiplied by 0.62 (the mean PEL-Q corresponding to a 20 % probability of observing toxicity in freshwater amphipods, applied to both freshwater and marine values because no marine P20 could be determined) for "Sensitive" sites, and by 1.2 (the freshwater and marine average at a 50 % probability) for "Typical" sites.

*(Citation confirmed against the held copy, and this flag withdrawn. The sub-title "Technical Appendix" is printed on the title page. The year is 2003 on the document's own evidence: its bibliography self-cites as "Macfarlane, M.W., D.D. MacDonald, and C.G. Ingersoll. 2003. Criteria for managing contaminated sediment in British Columbia. Technical Appendix", and it cites the four guidance-manual volumes, every one of which is dated November 2003, so it cannot pre-date them. The date 1 February 2002, previously treated as this document's latest internal date, is the consolidation date of a Contaminated Sites Regulation citation in that same bibliography, not a date of authorship. The document is a third, distinct member of this family - Macfarlane, MacDonald and Ingersoll (2003) - and not either of the two previously offered as candidates for it. One inconsistency in the above is the source document's own and is not resolved here: its title page carries *Ministry of Water, Land and Air Protection*, the name used in the paragraph above, while its bibliography self-cites to *Ministry of Environment, Lands and Parks*. The ministry was renamed in this period and the document straddles the change. Both forms are quoted as printed, and neither is corrected to the other.)*

Critically, the cited document **contains an explicit limitation**:
- The refinement dataset was single-species amphipod co-occurrence testing: *Hyalella azteca* (freshwater, 28 to 42-day tests) and *Ampelisca abdita* and *Rhepoxynius abronius* (marine, 10-day tests). The endpoints were growth, reproduction or mortality, not lethality alone.
- The derivation did not directly or systematically incorporate bioavailability, geochemical binding, bioaccumulation, biomagnification through aquatic food webs, or human consumption of harvested seafood; bioaccumulation was left to separate, non-integrated tissue residue guidelines (MWLAP 2003).

### 2.3 The Empirical Co-Occurrence Fallacy: False Positives vs. False Negatives
Continued reliance on empirical co-occurrence values creates two failure modes at B.C. aquatic sites. Neither has been quantified for this project:

**THE EMPIRICAL CO-OCCURRENCE FALLACY IN B.C. SEDIMENTS**

1. **FALSE POSITIVES (OVER-REGULATION & REMEDIATION PARALYSIS):**
   - Contaminants tightly sequestered in organic-rich (high TOC) or anoxic sulfidic (high AVS) sediments exceed dry-weight numerical standards despite exhibiting ZERO porewater activity.
   - Triggers unnecessary, costly, and ecologically destructive dredging that destroys benthic habitats and resuspends buried contaminants.
2. **FALSE NEGATIVES (UNDER-PROTECTION OF HIGHER TROPHIC RECEPTORS):**
   - In low-TOC (< 0.5%) or oxic sediments, hydrophobic and bioaccumulative contaminants (PCBs, Dioxins/Furans, MeHg, PFAS) partition aggressively into porewater and biota.
   - Contaminant concentrations that pass the 2003 amphipod lethality screen are not thereby screened for reproductive effects in piscivorous wildlife or for cancer risk to human consumers. The screen measures direct-contact toxicity to a single benthic species over a fixed exposure and evaluates neither food-web transfer nor human dietary exposure, so passing it is not evidence about those risks in either direction. Whether such concentrations do cause those effects at a given site is a site-specific question that this framework is intended to make assessable, and is not asserted here.

### 2.4 From Schedule 9 Criteria to Schedule 3.4 Standards (2004 to 2023)
Schedule 9 was repealed and replaced by Schedule 3.4 in the 2017 CSR omnibus amendment (B.C. Reg. 253/2016, s. 17 creating Schedule 3.4 and s. 18 repealing Schedule 9, as amended by B.C. Reg. 196/2017, s. 3, in force 1 November 2017), which renamed the sediment "criteria" as sediment "standards"; the numerical values were carried over unchanged. The Schedule 3.4 now in force was re-enacted by B.C. Reg. 133/2022, App., s. 12 (as amended by B.C. Reg. 2/2023), in force 1 March 2023. The derivation basis of the values is set out in Protocol 28, *2016 Standards Derivation Methods*, Version 3.0 (effective 1 May 2024), whose Chapter 7 derives sediment standards as a CCME (1999) probable effect level multiplied by a probability factor (P20 = 0.62 freshwater; P50 = 1.2 freshwater and marine), with no organic carbon normalization and no AVS-SEM adjustment. Bioavailability therefore sits outside the current derivation, which is the gap this appendix examines.

### 2.5 The Two Exposure Pathway Groups: Ecological Benthic Contact and Human Health
Modernized sediment standards would need to evaluate bioavailability across both major exposure pathways:
1. **Ecological Direct Benthic Contact (Pathway 3):** Governed by porewater chemical activity, $K_{oc} \cdot f_{oc}$ organic carbon partitioning, and $\Sigma\text{SEM} - \text{AVS}$ sulfide precipitation.
2. **Human Health Shoreline Direct Contact (Pathway 1):** Governed by incidental sediment ingestion and dermal absorption during recreational, commercial, and traditional harvesting activities, modified by oral bioaccessibility ($IVBA$) and dermal adherence/absorption factors ($AF_{sed}, RAF_{dermal}$).

---

## 3.0 Non-Ionic Hydrophobic Organic Carbon Partitioning (EqP) Thermodynamics

### 3.1 Fugacity Theory, Chemical Activity, and Equilibrium Partitioning
The physical-chemical behavior of non-ionic organic contaminants (e.g., polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons [PAHs], polychlorinated biphenyls [PCBs], polychlorinated dibenzo-p-dioxins and dibenzofurans [PCDD/Fs], organochlorine pesticides, chlorobenzenes) in aquatic sediments is governed by thermodynamic equilibrium partitioning (EqP) theory (Di Toro et al. 1991; US EPA 2003, 2012).

Under fugacity theory (Mackay 2001), when a chemical achieves thermodynamic equilibrium across environmental compartments, its chemical potential (mu_i) and fugacity (f, in Pa) are equal in the solid sediment organic phase, the interstitial porewater phase, and the lipid membranes of benthic organisms:
$$f_{sed} = f_{porewater} = f_{lipid}$$
Chemical activity (a = f / f^0) drives diffusive flux across biological membranes. Organisms living in sediment are exposed primarily through two concurrent pathways: (1) direct trans-membrane bioconcentration from freely dissolved porewater (C_free or C_pw), and (2) ingestion of sediment particles whose internal pore fluids are in equilibrium with porewater. Because porewater and organism lipids are in continuous thermodynamic equilibrium, **the biological response (toxicity and bioaccumulation) is uniquely determined by the freely dissolved porewater concentration (C_pw), not the total dry-weight bulk sediment concentration (C_sed,dw)**.

**Diagram summary 2.**

- Layout: tree
- Diagram note: title EQUILIBRIUM PARTITIONING (EqP) PHASE DISTRIBUTION
- Node bulk: BULK SEDIMENT MATRIX
- Node solid: SOLID SEDIMENT PHASE
- Criterion: 1. Amorphous Organic Carbon (AOC)
- Criterion: Linear Absorption (K_oc)
- Criterion: 2. Black Carbon / Soot (BC)
