### 3.2 Partition Coefficients (K_oc, K_ow) and Organic Carbon Normalization (f_oc)
In natural sediments, non-polar hydrophobic organic compounds sorb predominantly to amorphous natural organic matter. The solid-water distribution coefficient (K_d, in L/kg-dw) is directly proportional to the fraction of total organic carbon (f_oc):
$$K_d = C_{sed,dw} / C_{pw} = K_{oc} \times f_{oc}$$
Where:
- C_sed,dw = Total contaminant concentration in bulk dry sediment (mg/kg-dw).
- C_pw = Freely dissolved contaminant concentration in interstitial porewater (mg/L).
- K_oc = Organic carbon-water partition coefficient (L/kg-OC).
- f_oc = Mass fraction of total organic carbon in dry sediment (kg-OC/kg-sediment dw, where 1.0% TOC ==> f_oc = 0.01).

Where empirical substance-specific K_oc values are unavailable, K_oc is calculated from the octanol-water partition coefficient (K_ow) using standardized thermodynamic linear free energy relationships (LFERs) approved by US EPA (2012) and Di Toro et al. (1991):
$$\log_{10}(K_{oc}) = 0.00028 + 0.983 \times \log_{10}(K_{ow})$$
For polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs), this appendix carries a refined empirical regression - **no source is established for the PAH-specific relation below.** The generic relation above reproduces exactly in two held US EPA documents; this refinement does not appear in either source it was credited to, and the PAH-specific $K_{oc}$ values used downstream rest on it.
$$\log_{10}(K_{oc}) = 0.98 \times \log_{10}(K_{ow}) - 0.07$$

### 3.3 Quantitative Derivation of the Ecological Direct Contact Standard (C_sed,EqP)
To protect benthic macroinvertebrate communities from chronic and acute chemical toxicity, the freely dissolved porewater concentration must not exceed the water quality Final Chronic Value (FCV_water, in mg/L) established for aquatic life protection. Setting C_pw = FCV_water and substituting into the partitioning equation yields the master derivation formula for **Pathway 3 (Ecological Direct Contact - Organics)**:

$$C_{sed,EqP} = K_{oc} \times f_{oc} \times FCV_{water} \times 10^{-3}$$

Where:
- C_sed,EqP = Matrix Sediment Standard for ecological direct contact (mg/kg-dw).
- K_oc = Substance-specific organic carbon partition coefficient (L/kg-OC).
- f_oc = Sediment organic carbon fraction (default = 0.01 for 1.0% TOC at Tier 1, or site-measured at Tier 2).
- FCV_water = Provincial Water Quality Guideline or Final Chronic Value for aquatic life (ug/L).
- 10^-3 = unit conversion. FCV_water is tabulated in ug/L and K_oc in L/kg-OC, so the product is ug/kg; the factor converts it to the mg/kg-dw in which the standard is expressed. It is not an adjustment factor and must not be dropped.

### 3.4 Operational Domain and Boundary Conditions (0.2% <= f_oc <= 10.0%)
Extensive empirical investigations across North American estuaries and freshwater systems (US EPA 2003, 2012; Washington Department of Ecology 2013) demonstrate that simple linear organic carbon normalization is scientifically defensible only within bounded geochemical limits:

**OPERATIONAL BOUNDARY CONDITIONS FOR TOC NORMALIZATION**

1. **LOWER BOUND: f_oc >= 0.002 (0.2% TOC or 2,000 mg/kg TOC)**
   - Below 0.2% TOC (e.g., coarse quartz sands, gravels, high-energy beach environments), hydrophobic partitioning to organic carbon ceases to be the sole dominant sorption mechanism.
   - Non-specific mineral surface adsorption to quartz, feldspars, and aluminosilicate clays becomes significant. Simple f_oc normalization divides by an extremely small denominator, producing artificially low, mathematically unstable sediment standards.
   - OPERATING RULE: For sediments with measured TOC < 0.2%, the calculation floor of 0.2% (f_oc = 0.002) MUST be applied, or dry-weight standards utilized.
2. **UPPER BOUND: f_oc <= 0.100 (10.0% TOC or 100,000 mg/kg TOC)** **- unsourced.** No source has been located for a 10.0% ceiling; the 0.2% lower bound, by contrast, is verified verbatim in two held US EPA documents. The mechanistic reasoning below is not in question; the number is.
   - Above 10.0% TOC (e.g., peats, wood-waste deposits, pulp mill log-sort basins, organic mucks), interstitial porewaters contain high concentrations of dissolved and colloidal organic carbon (DOC/DOM).
   - DOC complexes with hydrophobic organics, acting as a competitive co-solvent and altering apparent partitioning kinetics. Furthermore, matrix steric hindrance and non-linear absorption limit further linear sequestration.
   - PROPOSED OPERATING RULE, **not a requirement**: for sediments with measured TOC above the ceiling, a standard could be capped at that ceiling ($f_{oc}$ = 0.100), or the assessment could proceed to Tier 3 direct porewater or bioassay testing. This draft proposes nothing binding, and a "MUST" resting on a ceiling with no located source is a stronger claim than the number can carry. Which rule applies above the domain, and where the domain ends, are decisions for the Technical Working Group.

### 3.5 Dual-Mode Sorption & Black Carbon (BC) Geochemical Controls
In urban, industrial, port, and historical industrial waterways across British Columbia (e.g., Burrard Inlet, False Creek, Fraser River estuary, Victoria Harbour, Esquimalt Harbour), sediment organic carbon is not composed solely of amorphous biogenic carbon. A substantial fraction consists of carbonaceous geosorbents, including soot, charcoal, coal dust, ash, and biochar, collectively designated as **Black Carbon (BC)**.

Black carbon possesses a condensed, highly aromatic, porous planar structure that exhibits adsorption affinities (K_BC) that are 10 to 100 times higher than amorphous biogenic organic carbon (K_AOC) (Accardi-Dey and Gschwend 2002, 2003; Cornelissen et al. 2005; Ghosh et al. 2014). Sorption to black carbon follows a non-linear Freundlich isotherm. The comprehensive **Dual-Mode Sorption Model** is expressed as:

$$C_{sed} = f_{AOC} \times K_{AOC} \times C_{pw} + f_{BC} \times K_{BC} \times (C_{pw})^{n}$$

Where:
- f_AOC = Mass fraction of amorphous biogenic organic carbon (f_AOC ~ f_oc - f_BC).
- K_AOC = Partition coefficient for amorphous organic carbon (~ K_oc).
- f_BC = Mass fraction of black carbon in sediment.
- K_BC = Black carbon-water distribution coefficient (L/kg-BC).
- n = Freundlich non-linearity exponent (0.6 <= n <= 0.8).

**Regulatory Consequence:** Standard linear f_oc normalization ignores black carbon, substantially over-predicting freely dissolved porewater concentrations and bioaccumulation in urban and industrial sediments. At Tier 3 site assessments, direct measurement of C_free via passive samplers resolves this conservatism.

### 3.6 Freely Dissolved Porewater Concentrations (C_free) and Passive Sampling (SPME, POM, LDPE)
Because direct centrifugal extraction or suction peeper sampling of porewater frequently isolates dissolved organic matter (DOM) colloids containing bound, non-bioavailable contaminant fractions, passive sampling devices (PSDs) have emerged as the international standard for measuring C_free (US EPA 2012, 2017; Ghosh et al. 2014; Mayer et al. 2003, 2014).

**PASSIVE SAMPLING POLYMER TECHNOLOGIES FOR SEDIMENT**

1. **SOLID-PHASE MICROEXTRACTION (SPME):**
   - Glass fibers coated with Polydimethylsiloxane (PDMS) (typically 10 to 100 um coating).
   - Extremely fast equilibration kinetics (hours to days); ideal for small-volume laboratory ex situ sediment slurry and core-slice testing.
2. **POLYOXYMETHYLENE (POM):**
   - Solid thermoplastic sheets (typically 17 to 76 um thickness).
   - Smooth surface prevents biofouling; robust for both in situ deployments and ex situ testing.
3. **LOW-DENSITY POLYETHYLENE (LDPE):**
   - High-capacity polymer strips (typically 25 to 50 um thickness).
   - Excellent for highly hydrophobic contaminants (PCBs, Dioxins/Furans); utilizes Performance Reference Compounds (PRCs) to correct for non-equilibrium field deployments.

#### Governing Passive Sampling Equations:
At thermodynamic equilibrium between the polymer and sediment porewater:
$$C_{free} = C_{polymer} / K_{polymer}$$
Where:
- C_free = Freely dissolved contaminant concentration in interstitial porewater (ug/L).
- C_polymer = Contaminant mass accumulated per unit mass of polymer (ug/kg-polymer).
- K_polymer = Experimentally calibrated polymer-water partition coefficient (L/kg-polymer).

For in situ field deployments where equilibrium is not fully achieved, the fractional approach to equilibrium (f_eq) is quantified using pre-loaded deuterated Performance Reference Compounds (PRCs):
$$f_{eq} = 1 - (C_{PRC,t} / C_{PRC,0})$$

$$C_{free} = C_{polymer,t} / (K_{polymer} \times f_{eq})$$

### 3.7 Toxic Unit (TU) Additivity and Mixture Toxicity for Polycyclic Aromatic Hydrocarbons (PAHs)
Polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) invariably occur in sediments as complex multi-component mixtures (petrogenic crude oils, refined fuels, pyrogenic creosotes, coal tars, urban runoff). PAHs exert toxicity through non-specific non-polar narcosis (membrane disruption).

Under US EPA (2003) and McGrath et al. (2019), the benchmark for PAH mixtures is based on Toxic Unit (TU) Additivity across 34 parent and alkylated PAHs (Sigma TU_34):

$$TU_i = C_{pw,i} / FCV_i = C_{sed,dw,i} / (K_{oc,i} \times f_{oc} \times FCV_i)$$

$$\Sigma\ TU_{34} = Sum(TU_i)\ for\ i = 1\ to\ 34$$

#### Decision Rules for PAH Mixtures:
- **Sigma TU_34 <= 1.0:** The sediment PAH mixture is non-toxic to benthic macroinvertebrate communities.
- **Sigma TU_34 > 1.0:** Cumulative narcosis threshold is exceeded; chronic benthic toxicity is predicted.
- **Alkylated Homologue Multipliers.** A 16-parent-PAH analysis misses alkylated homologues and therefore under-states petrogenic PAH toxicity. **No sourced under-estimation factor or correction multiplier is carried here**: the figures previously given appear in none of the three sources cited for this paragraph, and the multiplier is an input still to be established. Site-specific quantification of alkylated homologues (C1-C4 naphthalenes, phenanthrenes, fluorenes, chrysenes) avoids the question. **[neither figure is in the sources cited for this paragraph.** neither the 1.5-to-3.0 under-estimation factor nor the CF_alkyl = 2.0 multiplier appears in any of them. The underlying point - that a 16-parent-PAH analysis misses alkylated homologues and therefore under-states petrogenic PAH toxicity - is well established and is not what is in question; the two numbers are. **The imperative below is withdrawn with them.**]: When analytical data are limited to the 16 EPA Priority Parent PAHs, total PAH toxicity is under-estimated by a factor of 1.5 to 3.0 in petrogenic sediments. A correction multiplier (CF_alkyl = 2.0) would be applied unless site-specific alkylated homologues (C1-C4 naphthalenes, phenanthrenes, fluorenes, chrysenes) are quantified.

---

## 4.0 Cationic Divalent Metals & Acid Volatile Sulfide / Simultaneously Extracted Metals (AVS-SEM)

### 4.1 Biogeochemical Mechanisms of Acid Volatile Sulfide Precipitation
In anoxic and suboxic aquatic sediments, microbial decomposition of organic matter by sulfate-reducing bacteria (e.g., *Desulfovibrio*, *Desulfobacter*) generates hydrogen sulfide (H2S / HS^-):
$$SO_4^{(2-)} + 2\ CH_2O \; -> \; H_2S + 2\ HCO_3^{-}$$
Dissolved sulfide reacts immediately with abundant solid-phase iron oxyhydroxides and porewater Fe^(2+) to form amorphous iron monosulfide (FeS, mackinawite, greigite), operationally defined as **Acid Volatile Sulfide (AVS)** (Di Toro et al. 1990, 1992, 2005; US EPA 2005):
$$Fe^{(2+)} + HS^{-} \; -> \; FeS_{(s)} + H^{+}$$

**Diagram summary 3.**

- Layout: tree
- Diagram note: title AVS-SEM METAL DISPLACEMENT AND NEUTRALIZATION
- Node sed: ANOXIC / SUBOXIC SEDIMENT
- Node avs: ACID VOLATILE SULFIDE (AVS)
- Diagram note: head Solid-phase FeS Pool
- Node sem: SIMULTANEOUSLY EXTRACTED METALS (SEM)
- Diagram note: head Cold 1.0 M HCl Extractable Pool
- Node disp: Me^(2+) + FeS(s) ---> MeS(s) [PRECIPITATED] + Fe^(2+)
- Node case1: CASE 1: SULFIDE EXCESS
- Criterion: Sigma[SEM] - [AVS] <= 0
- Criterion: Porewater Me^(2+) = 0
- Criterion: TOXICITY: ZERO (NON-TOXIC)
- Node case2: CASE 2: SEM EXCESS
- Criterion: Sigma[SEM] - [AVS] > 0
- Criterion: Excess Metal Binds to Organic Carbon
- Criterion: Excess_SEM_OC = (Sigma[SEM]-AVS)/f_oc
- Criterion: < 130 umol/g-OC: UNLIKELY TOXIC
- Criterion: > 3000 umol/g-OC: LIKELY TOXIC
- Relationship: sed -> avs
- Relationship: sed -> sem
- Relationship: sed -> disp
- Diagram note: label THERMODYNAMIC DISPLACEMENT
- Relationship: disp -> case1
- Relationship: disp -> case2

### 4.2 Stoichiometric Metal Sulfide Displacement & Solubility Product (K_sp) Hierarchy
Because the solubility product constants (K_sp) of divalent heavy metal sulfides are many orders of magnitude lower than that of iron sulfide (FeS), transition metal cations stoichiometrically displace iron, precipitating as insoluble crystalline or amorphous metal sulfides:

Table: Stoichiometric Metal Sulfide Displacement & Solubility Product (K_sp) Hierarchy.

| METAL SULFIDE SPECIES | LOG10(K_MS / K_FeS) | THERMODYNAMIC DISPLACEMENT REACTION |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Iron Monosulfide (FeS) | baseline (reference phase) | Baseline AVS Reservoir Matrix |
| Nickel Sulfide (NiS) | -5.59 | Ni^(2+) + FeS(s)  ->  NiS(s) + Fe^(2+) |
| Zinc Sulfide (ZnS) | -6.00 | Zn^(2+) + FeS(s)  ->  ZnS(s) + Fe^(2+) |
| Cadmium Sulfide (CdS) | -10.46 | Cd^(2+) + FeS(s)  ->  CdS(s) + Fe^(2+) |
| Lead Sulfide (PbS) | -11.03 | Pb^(2+) + FeS(s)  ->  PbS(s) + Fe^(2+) |
| Copper Sulfide (CuS) | -18.55 | Cu^(2+) + FeS(s)  ->  CuS(s) + Fe^(2+) |
| Silver Sulfide (Ag2S) | -32.32 | 2 Ag^(+) + FeS(s) ->  Ag2S(s) + Fe^(2+) |

> **Source.** The ratios above are the exchange-reaction column of U.S. EPA. 2005. *Procedures for the Derivation of Equilibrium Partitioning Sediment Benchmarks (ESBs) for the Protection of Benthic Organisms: Metal Mixtures (Cadmium, Copper, Lead, Nickel, Silver and Zinc)*, EPA-600-R-02-011, Table 2-2 (p. 2-13), which tabulates the solubility product of each metal sulfide relative to that of iron monosulfide - the quantity that governs whether a divalent metal displaces iron from FeS. **These are the dissolution-reaction constants.** A different set (-19.4 to -50.1) matches no column of EPA Table 2-2, and it ranked CdS as less soluble than PbS; EPA has PbS the less soluble of the two. The separately preserved Phase 3 evidence record documents the reaction distinction; no worked derived output is carried in this paper.

### 4.3 Simultaneously Extracted Metals (Sigma[SEM]) and Silver Valency Correction (0.5 * SEM_Ag)
Simultaneously Extracted Metals (SEM) are the molar concentrations of heavy metals liberated during the cold 1.0 M HCl extraction used to measure AVS. The molar sum of SEM (Sigma[SEM], in umol/g dry sediment) is defined across the six toxicologically significant metals:

$$\Sigma[SEM] = [SEM_{Cd}] + [SEM_{Cu}] + [SEM_{Ni}] + [SEM_{Pb}] + [SEM_{Zn}] + 0.5 \times [SEM_{Ag}]$$

Where each metal concentration is converted from mg/kg-dw to umol/g-dw by dividing by its atomic weight (MW in g/mol):
$$[SEM_i]\ (\mu\text{mol}/g) = C_{metal,i}\ (mg/kg) / MW_i$$
- MW_Cd = 112.41
- MW_Cu = 63.55
- MW_Ni = 58.69
- MW_Pb = 207.20
- MW_Zn = 65.38
- MW_Ag = 107.87

**Silver Valency Correction (0.5 * SEM_Ag):** Because silver is a monovalent cation (Ag^+), two silver atoms bind to one sulfide ion (Ag2S). Therefore, silver contributes only half a mole of sulfide demand per mole of silver extracted (0.5 * SEM_Ag).

### 4.4 Stoichiometric Non-Toxicity Decision Rules (Sigma[SEM] - [AVS] <= 0)
The thermodynamic difference between total Simultaneously Extracted Metals and Acid Volatile Sulfide determines the presence of freely dissolved porewater metals:

$$\Delta[SEM-AVS] = \Sigma[SEM] - [AVS] \ \ (in\ \mu\text{mol}/g\ dry\ sediment)$$

#### Decision Rules:
1. **Delta[SEM-AVS] <= 0.0 umol/g (or Sigma[SEM] / [AVS] <= 1.0):**
   - Sulfide binding capacity exceeds total heavy metal demand.
   - All divalent metal cations are completely precipitated as insoluble metal sulfides.
   - Interstitial porewater metal activities are effectively zero (C_pw,metal ~ 0).
   - **Verdict (model prediction):** Under the AVS-SEM model, porewater metal activity is predicted to be negligible and the sediment is predicted to be non-toxic to benthic infauna irrespective of bulk dry-weight metal concentrations. This is a prediction of the model, not an observed result, and it is a **Pathway 3 (ecological direct contact)** prediction only: the AVS-SEM rule governs metal activity in porewater and does not address metals taken up through the diet. Sediment predicted non-toxic on this test has not been screened on Pathway 4 (ecological food web) or Pathway 2 (human country foods), and the AVS-SEM result should not be read as a finding about either.

2. **Delta[SEM-AVS] > 0.0 umol/g:**
   - Metal concentrations exceed primary sulfide binding capacity.
   - Toxicity is NOT automatic; excess metals partition to secondary sediment binding phases (organic carbon and iron/manganese oxyhydroxides).

### 4.5 Excess SEM Organic Carbon Normalization ((Sigma[SEM] - [AVS])/f_oc) and Risk Tiers
When Sigma[SEM] > [AVS], excess metal binds to phenolic, carboxylic, and amine functional groups on sediment organic matter. US EPA (2005) establishes the Organic Carbon-Normalized Excess SEM parameter (Excess_SEM_OC, in umol/g-OC):

$$Excess_{SEM\_OC} = (\Sigma[SEM] - [AVS]) / f_{oc}$$

#### US EPA (2005) Empirical Risk Tiers:
- **Tier 1 (Protective / Non-Toxic): Excess_SEM_OC < 130 umol/g-OC**
  - Excess metals are completely bound by secondary organic carbon ligands.
  - Freely dissolved porewater concentrations remain below chronic water quality thresholds.
  - **Verdict:** Adverse benthic effects are unlikely.
- **Tier 2 (Uncertainty / Transition Zone): 130 umol/g-OC <= Excess_SEM_OC <= 3,000 umol/g-OC**
  - Organic carbon binding capacity is partially saturated. Toxicity depends on pH, hardness, and Fe/Mn oxide concentrations.
  - **Verdict:** Adverse effects are uncertain; triggers Tier 3 direct porewater extraction or laboratory bioassay testing.
- **Tier 3 (Toxic / High Risk): Excess_SEM_OC > 3,000 umol/g-OC**
  - All sediment binding phases (sulfide and organic carbon) are exhausted.
  - High concentrations of freely dissolved divalent metal cations exist in porewater.
  - **Verdict:** Acute and chronic benthic toxicity is highly likely.

### 4.6 Redox Stratification, Surficial Oxic Horizons (0-2 cm), Bioturbation, and Seasonal Oxidation
AVS formation occurs exclusively under reducing conditions. In dynamic aquatic ecosystems:
1. **Surficial Oxic Layer (0 to 2 cm):** Hydrodynamic oxygen exchange and wave mixing maintain an oxic surficial layer where AVS is rapidly oxidized to sulfate (SO4^(2-)) and Fe(OH)3. In this layer, metal bioavailability is governed by f_oc and iron/manganese oxyhydroxides, not AVS.
2. **Biologically Active Infaunal Zone (0 to 10 cm):** Deep-burrowing benthos (*Polychaeta*, *Bivalvia*, *Amphipoda*) inhabit the 2 to 10 cm suboxic horizon where AVS is stable and actively controls porewater toxicity.
3. **Sampling implication (draft proposal, not a current requirement):** Field sediment investigations in B.C. would need to avoid compositing deep anoxic sediment (> 10 cm) with surficial oxic sediment (0 to 2 cm), to prevent artificial dilution or false over-estimation of surficial AVS capacity.

---

## 5.0 Ionizable Organics, Metalloids & Organometallic Speciation

### 5.1 Ionizable Organics & pH-Dependent Dissociation (Chlorophenols, Phenolics, Organic Acids)
Unlike neutral non-polar organics, ionizable organic substances dissociate into neutral molecules (HA) and conjugate charged anions (A^-) based on ambient porewater pH and the substance-specific acid dissociation constant (pK_a):

$$pH = pK_{a} + \log_{10}([A^{-}] / [HA])$$

For chlorinated phenolics (e.g., pentachlorophenol [PCP], 2,4,6-trichlorophenol, tetrachlorophenols), the conjugate phenolate anion (A^-) is more water-soluble and possesses a much lower organic carbon partition coefficient (K_oc,ionized) than the neutral phenol (K_oc,neutral). The apparent distribution coefficient (D_oc) across the pH gradient is modeled as:

$$D_{oc}(pH) = \phi_{neutral} \times K_{oc,neutral} + (1 - \phi_{neutral}) \times K_{oc,ionized}$$

$$\phi_{neutral} = 1 / (1 + 10^{(pH - pK_{a})})$$

#### Empirical Example for Pentachlorophenol (pK_a = 4.75):
- At pH 5.0 (acidic bog/freshwater): phi_neutral ~ 0.360 (36% neutral). D_oc is high; substance is tightly bound to sediment organic carbon.
- At pH 8.0 (marine/estuarine B.C. coastal waters): phi_neutral ~ 0.00056 (> 99.94% ionized phenolate anion). D_oc drops dramatically by over two orders of magnitude, increasing porewater partitioning, mobility, and benthic bioavailability.
- **Draft operating rule (proposal for discussion):** matrix standards for ionizable substances would need to include pH-dependent lookup tables or site-specific pH adjustment formulas. The Schedule 3.4 now in force contains no such provision.

### 5.2 Metalloids & Oxyanions (Arsenic, Antimony, Selenium): Iron Oxyhydroxide vs. Sulfide Controls
Metalloids and oxyanions do not follow the divalent AVS-SEM precipitation stoichiometry:
1. **Arsenic (As):**
   - Under oxic conditions (Eh > +100 mV), Arsenate (As(V), H2AsO4^-) adsorbs strongly to amorphous ferric oxyhydroxides (Fe(OH)3).
   - Under moderately reducing conditions (Eh 0 to -150 mV), microbial reduction dissolves Fe(OH)3, reducing Arsenate to Arsenite (As(III), H3AsO3), which is substantially more mobile and toxic.
   - Under strongly reducing, sulfidic conditions (Eh < -200 mV), arsenic precipitates as insoluble realgar (AsS), orpiment (As2S3), or co-precipitates with pyrite (Fe(As)S2).
2. **Selenium (Se) & Antimony (Sb):**
   - Selenium exists as selenate (SeO4^(2-)), selenite (SeO3^(2-)), or organo-selenium (selenomethionine). Bioaccumulation into benthic detritivores and trophic transfer into fish eggs/ovary tissue is the primary driver of toxicity, requiring tissue residue guidelines rather than EqP standards.

### 5.3 Organometallics: Monomethylmercury (MeHg) and Tributyltin (TBT)
1. **Monomethylmercury (CH3Hg^+):**
   - Methylated in suboxic sediment horizons by sulfate- and iron-reducing bacteria (dsrA / hgcAB gene pathways).
   - MeHg binds with extreme affinity to sulfhydryl (thiol, -SH) groups on dissolved organic matter and structural proteins. It does not partition purely by K_ow, but accumulates aggressively into benthic invertebrates (BSAF 1.0 to 10.0 kg-OC/kg-lipid) and biomagnifies exponentially through marine food webs (TMF > 3.0).
2. **Tributyltin (TBT):**
   - Exhibits mixed hydrophobic and cation-exchange sorption properties. Cationic TBT^+ adsorbs to negatively charged clay surfaces and organic matter. Partitioning decreases in acidic porewaters due to protonation and increases with salinity.

### 5.4 Per- and Polyfluoroalkyl Substances (PFAS): Anionic Surfactant Sorption and Protein Binding
Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (e.g., PFOS, PFOA, PFNA, PFHxS) possess hydrophobic, oleophobic fluorinated alkyl tails and hydrophilic charged headgroups (sulfonates, carboxylates).
- At environmental pH (6.0 to 8.5), perfluoroalkyl acids exist as anions.
- Sorption to sediment is driven by a combination of hydrophobic partitioning to organic carbon and electrostatic interaction with mineral charges (K_d ~ 2 to 50 L/kg). **[Source to be confirmed.** No citation is given at this point of use, and the range does not appear in the one PFAS document the project holds. PFAS sorption is genuinely variable and a range is the right form for it, but this particular range has no located basis.**]**
- **Protein Binding:** In aquatic organisms, PFAS bioaccumulates by binding specifically to liver fatty acid-binding proteins (L-FABP) and serum albumin, rather than partitioning into neutral lipids. **Standard lipid-normalized BSAF and EqP equations MUST NOT be applied to PFAS.**

---

## 6.0 Human Health Shoreline Exposure & Bioaccessibility Modifiers

### 6.1 Oral In Vitro Bioaccessibility (IVBA) and Gastrointestinal Absorption (RAF_oral)
For Human Health Shoreline Exposure (Pathway 1: Incidental Sediment Ingestion), total metal concentration over-estimates absorbed dose because mineral-bound metals do not fully dissolve in the human gastrointestinal tract.

The oral relative absorption factor ($RAF_{oral}$) adjusts generic intake for the fraction of a substance that is actually bioaccessible: **no source is established for this expression.** The held Health Canada 2017 oral-bioavailability guidance does not define this ratio: its equation 2.3 expresses relative bioavailability as the absorbed fraction from soil over the absorbed fraction from the dosing medium used in the toxicity reference value study, with no $IVBA_{default}$ term. No value in this paper is derived from the expression below, and nothing may rest on it until a source is established.

$$RAF_{oral} = IVBA_{site} / IVBA_{default}$$

Where IVBA is the fraction extracted in synthetic gastric fluid (pH 1.5, glycine buffer, 37 degrees C) via the standardized US EPA Method 1340 / ASTM E3138 protocol. Mineralized forms (e.g., lead sulfide [galena], arsenic in arsenopyrite or slag matrices) exhibit IVBA < 20%, whereas soluble salts exhibit IVBA > 80%.

### 6.2 Dermal Absorption Mechanics & Relative Absorption Factors (RAF_dermal)

> **[STATUS: SOURCE-GAP QUARANTINE - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]**

The former dermal-factor table and its source attributions are preserved in the V10 before-edit snapshot and removed from the active paper surface. No dermal absorption factor, cancer slope factor, or Health Canada or U.S. EPA attribution from that displaced table is accepted as a verified derivation input in this successor. Any future use requires exact primary-source location, scope, units, and applicability verification followed by independent review.

This appendix therefore records the need for a dermal-factor input inventory without selecting a value or asserting that a cited factor is current, applicable to sediment, or suitable for a Matrix standard.

### 6.3 Seafood Culinary Preparation and Bioavailability Reductions (LOSS)
For human consumption of aquatic country foods (Pathway 2), culinary preparation (boiling, steaming, trimming, fat removal, smoking) reduces contaminant residues:
- Organic Lipophilic Contaminants (PCBs, Dioxins, PAHs): Trimming crab hepatopancreas ("crab butter") and skinning fish filets reduces contaminant intake by 20% to 70% (LOSS = 0.20 to 0.70).
- Heavy Metals (Methylmercury, Lead, Arsenic): Tightly bound to muscle proteins; cooking loss is minimal (LOSS ~ 0.0).
- **Conservative default:** Where published country-foods exposure parameters describe whole-organ and traditional culinary preparations (e.g., whole-crab consumption, eulachon grease processing) - for example the parameter set published by Olsgard et al. (2023) for the Lower Athabasca Region in Alberta - this appendix applies no cooking loss (LOSS = 0.0). Those parameters are used here only as published literature values. That report is an Alberta study prepared for three First Nations; it is not a British Columbia instrument and has no status in B.C. This project includes no Indigenous engagement and none is in scope. Site-specific food-basket exposure is outside the scope of deriving numerical standards protective province-wide; the parameterisation of Pathway 2 is an open question for the framework, not a settled input.

## 7.0 Jurisdictional Precedents & Comparative Framework Analysis

Table: Summary Of Leading Jurisdictional Bioavailability Frameworks.

**SUMMARY OF LEADING JURISDICTIONAL BIOAVAILABILITY FRAMEWORKS**

| JURISDICTION / GUIDELINE | PRIMARY BIOAVAILABILITY TOOLS | OPERATIONAL RULES |
| --- | --- | --- |
| US EPA ESBs (2003, 2005, 2012) | EqP for non-polar organics; AVS-SEM and OC-excess for metals; SPME/LDPE. | 0.2% <= f\_oc <= 10.0% Excess\_SEM\_OC tiers. |
| Washington Ecology SMS (2013) (WAC Chapter 173-204) | TOC normalization for organics; AVS-SEM rules; bioaccumulation testing. | 0.5% <= TOC <= 4.0% dry weight fallback. **Not located in WAC 173-204** - the only TOC-percent table in that chapter is an unrelated finfish-facility monitoring provision. Washington practice may apply such a window, but it is not sourced to this instrument. |
| Oregon DEQ (2007, 2021) | Tiered Bioaccumulative Chemicals of Concern (BCCs) screening levels. | Explicit wildlife & human seafood models. |
| ANZG (Australia/NZ 2018) | 4-Tier Hierarchical Framework (DGV -> TOC/AVS Normalization -> Porewater -> Bioassays). | Gold-standard tiered decision tree. |
| RSET Pacific Northwest (2018) | Chemistry -> 28-day Bioaccumulation -> Amphipod Bioassays. | Regional dredging & remediation standard. |
| Atlantic RBCA (2021, 2023) | **Adopted values, not EqP-derived** - predominantly CCME PEL/ISQG; no Health Canada sediment standards exist in the framework (see section 7.6). | **Procedural** precedent (documented adoption hierarchy), **not** a derivation or multi-pathway precedent. |

### 7.1 US EPA Equilibrium Partitioning Sediment Benchmarks (ESBs)
The US EPA Office of Research and Development (ORD) published a landmark series of technical documents establishing Equilibrium Partitioning Sediment Benchmarks (ESBs) for non-ionic organics (2003, 2012), metal mixtures (2005), and PAH mixtures (2003). The ESBs provide derivation models and thermodynamic constants that this appendix examines as a candidate basis for a proposed Schedule 3.4 Part 1. B.C. has not adopted them, and Schedule 3.4 Part 1 does not exist.

### 7.2 Washington State Department of Ecology Sediment Management Standards (SMS)
Washington State's *Sediment Management Standards* (WAC Chapter 173-204, revised 2013) provide the closest regional regulatory analogue to British Columbia. Washington Ecology enforces TOC normalization for non-polar organics within an empirical operational window of 0.5% to 4.0% TOC. If sediment TOC falls outside this range, site managers must revert to dry-weight chemical criteria. Washington also incorporates AVS-SEM rules for metal mixtures and narrative bioaccumulation criteria to protect human seafood harvesters and higher-trophic wildlife.

### 7.3 Oregon Department of Environmental Quality (DEQ) Bioavailability Guidance
Oregon DEQ's *Guidance for Assessing Bioaccumulative Chemicals of Concern in Sediment* (2007, 2021) provides tiered bioaccumulation screening levels (SLs) based on back-calculating protective sediment targets from tissue residue thresholds in fish and shellfish. Its human-health tissue-level equations cite US EPA (1989, 1997) federal guidance only: a full-text search of the held document returns no occurrence of "Health Canada", "seafood" or "harvest".

### 7.4 Australian and New Zealand Guidelines (ANZG 2018) Hierarchical Decision Framework
ANZG (2018) established the premier international hierarchical decision tree for sediment quality assessment:
1. **Tier 1 (Default Guideline Values - DGV):** Screening against generic total concentration guidelines.
2. **Tier 2 (Bioavailability Normalization):** Normalization to TOC for non-ionic organics, and dilute acid extraction / AVS-SEM for metals.
3. **Tier 3 (Porewater / Passive Sampling):** Direct chemical measurement of freely dissolved porewater concentrations (C_free).
4. **Tier 4 (Toxicity Bioassays & Weight of Evidence):** Spiked-sediment bioassays (*Amphipoda*, *Polychaeta*, *Microtox*) and benthic community assessments.

### 7.5 Regional Sediment Evaluation Team (RSET 2016/2018) Pacific Northwest Framework
The *Sediment Evaluation Framework for the Pacific Northwest* (RSET 2018) governs dredging and in-water sediment management across Washington, Oregon, and Idaho. It standardizes 28-day steady-state laboratory bioaccumulation testing using *Macoma nasuta* (bent-nose clam) and *Nephtys caecoides* / *Neanthes arenaceodentata* (marine polychaetes) under ASTM E1688-10 protocols.

### 7.6 Atlantic RBCA Tier 1 & Tier 2 Sediment Environmental Quality Standards
The Atlantic Partnership in RBCA Implementation (Atlantic PIRI 2021, 2023) publishes Tier I ecological Environmental Quality Standards and Tier II Pathway-Specific Standards for sediment across four provinces, with separate freshwater and marine values.

> **What the Atlantic RBCA precedent is, and is not.** Against the held *Atlantic RBCA Environmental Quality Standards: Rationale and Guidance Document* (July 2021, updated June 2023) and the Tier I and Tier II sediment tables:
>
> * **The standards were not established by applying EqP.** Section 4.2 states that "de novo derivation or development of specific provincial standards was not warranted" and that "all EQS and PSS values are adopted values that have been derived by CCME and other regulatory authorities." Equilibrium partitioning is named conceptually and deferred to USEPA (2008); the Tier II table's own Reference column cites predominantly **CCME PEL/ISQG**.
> * **There are no Health Canada-based sediment standards in the framework.** The rationale states there are "no human health-based guidelines that are specifically derived for the assessment of human sediment exposure". Health Canada (2017) guidance is used to screen sediment against **residential soil** values for direct contact only. The ecological EQS protect **benthic invertebrates alone**, one value per substance.
> * **Tier I and Tier II carry the same numbers.** The two tables' values match wherever spot-checked; Tier II adds Comments and Reference columns, not pathway-differentiated standards. Atlantic RBCA is therefore **not a worked precedent for multi-pathway sediment standards**, which is the specific thing this paper proposes.
> * **The precedent is partly circular.** Tier II's Reference column cites **BCMOECCS and CSR Schedule 3.4** among its sources - so B.C. adopting from Atlantic RBCA would in part be adopting B.C.'s own values back again.
>
> **What survives is still worth something, and it is worth stating precisely:** Atlantic RBCA is a Canadian, multi-province precedent for **publishing tiered sediment standards with an explicit, documented adoption hierarchy and a per-value reference column** - which is a procedural precedent for Appendix F, not a derivation precedent for Appendix K. See Appendix K's Atlantic RBCA row.

---

## 8.0 Comprehensive Options Analysis & Draft B.C. Tiered Hybrid Architecture

### 8.1 Option A: Default Generic Total Concentration (Status Quo / Screening Only)
- **Description:** Maintain unadjusted, bulk dry-weight total chemical concentrations (the single-list empirical approach of the Schedule 3.4 now in force, whose values were carried over unchanged from the former Schedule 9; Schedule 3.4 was created by B.C. Reg. 253/2016, s. 17, in force 1 November 2017, and re-enacted by B.C. Reg. 133/2022, App., s. 12, in force 1 March 2023 - it did not exist in 2003).
- **Pros:** Minimal analytical testing costs; simple administrative enforcement.
- **Cons:** Scientifically weak. Generates false positives (unnecessary dredging of bound, non-toxic sediment) and false negatives (failure to detect bioaccumulation in low-TOC sediments).
- **Draft assessment (for discussion):** not carried forward in this draft.

### 8.2 Option B: Tiered Mechanistic Adjustments (Draft Preferred Hybrid Strategy)
- **Description:** Establish a three-tier implementation framework:
  - **Tier 1 (Generic Matrix Standards):** Conservative numerical standards derived at default 1.0% TOC (f_oc = 0.01) and baseline AVS assumptions.
  - **Tier 2 (Site-Specific Mechanistic Normalization):** Routine adjustment using commercial laboratory TOC (0.2% <= f_oc <= 10.0%) and AVS-SEM data (Sigma[SEM] - [AVS] <= 0 or Excess_SEM_OC < 130 umol/g-OC).
  - **Tier 3 (Advanced Detailed Quantitative Risk Assessment - DQRA):** Direct porewater extraction (peepers, centrifugation), passive sampling (C_free via SPME/POM/LDPE), Biotic Ligand Modeling (BLM), and laboratory spiked-sediment bioassays under Protocol 1.
- **Pros:** Improves scientific defensibility, reduces false positives and false negatives, uses existing commercial laboratory capacity, provides predictable regulatory off-ramps, and allows human health exposure to be assessed with published country-foods consumption parameters.
- **Cons:** Requires training for Qualified Professionals on AVS-SEM sampling and TOC boundary rules.
- **Draft assessment (for discussion):** the preferred option in this draft. It is a proposal for discussion, not a provincial strategy and not an ENV decision.

### 8.3 Option C: Mandatory Universal Porewater / Passive Sampling & Bioassays
- **Description:** Mandate direct porewater extraction, passive sampling, or 28-day laboratory bioaccumulation testing on every aquatic contaminated site.
- **Pros:** Highest thermodynamic and site-specific fidelity.
- **Cons:** Laboratory capacity bottlenecks; high analytical costs; schedule delays on routine small dredging and remediation projects.
- **Draft assessment (for discussion):** not proposed as a universal requirement; retained as a Tier 3 option.

---

### 8.4 Multi-Dimensional Evaluation Matrix across 6 Core Policy Criteria

**Illustrative only - one example of how these options could be compared, not an evaluation that has been carried out.** The ratings below are the drafters' unweighted qualitative judgements, offered so that a comparison method can be discussed. They rest on no scoring exercise, no data, and no review, and the criteria themselves are open: under the framework-first scope of Phase 2 the options analysis has still to be structured around sediment uses, receptors and exposure pathways, exposure assumptions and receptor characteristics, and the selection among available input parameters, with equation form only one layer among these. No option has been selected.

Table: Multi-Dimensional Evaluation Matrix across 6 Core Policy Criteria.

| EVALUATION CRITERION | OPTION A (STATUS QUO) | OPTION B (TIERED HYB) | OPTION C (UNIVERSAL) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Scientific Defensibility | VERY LOW (Empirical) | VERY HIGH (EqP/AVS) | VERY HIGH (Direct C_pw) |
| 2. Ecological & Human Protection | LOW (False Negatives) | VERY HIGH (4 Pathways) | VERY HIGH (Measured) |
| 3. Commercial Lab Feasibility | HIGH (Bulk Dry-Weight) | HIGH (Routine TOC/AVS) | LOW (Capacity Limits) |
| 4. Cost-Effectiveness & Efficiency | LOW (Over-Remediation) | HIGH (Tiered Screening | VERY LOW (High Cost) |
| 5. Regulatory Predictability | HIGH (Single Number) | HIGH (Defined Rules) | MODERATE (Complex) |
| 6. High-Intake Country Foods | VERY LOW (Omitted) | VERY HIGH (Harmonized) | HIGH (Site-Specific) |
| OVERALL RANKING | 3rd (UNACCEPTABLE) | 1st (PREFERRED) | 2nd (TIER 3 TOOL) |

**On the Cost-Effectiveness & Efficiency row, criterion 4.** This scoring is a qualitative judgement carrying no costing. Nowhere in this paper is a per-site incremental cost, an analytical unit cost, or a turnaround time stated for any option; the word "turnaround" appears nowhere in the paper. Rating Option C "VERY LOW (High Cost)" is an assertion about costs borne by the regulated community that this paper has not quantified. Pricing it would require analytical cost and turnaround-time data that this project does not hold. The scores above are left unchanged.

## 9.0 Analytical Laboratory Test Requirements, Verification Protocols & Field Sampling SOPs

### 9.1 Standardized Analytical Protocols
For full commercial laboratory readiness across B.C., analytical testing would need to use standardized, accredited test methods such as the following. These are draft proposals, not current requirements:
1. **Total Organic Carbon (TOC):** High-temperature combustion / catalytic oxidation with non-dispersive infrared (NDIR) detection following acidification to remove inorganic carbonates (ASTM D4129 / EPA Method 9060A).
2. **Acid Volatile Sulfide & Simultaneously Extracted Metals (AVS-SEM):** Cold 1.0 M HCl purge-and-trap acid digestion at room temperature (20 to 22 degrees C) for 60 minutes. Sulfide captured in 0.5 M NaOH and quantified via ion-selective electrode or colorimetry (methylene blue; EPA Method 6830 / Allen et al. 1993). Liberated SEM metals (Cd, Cu, Ni, Pb, Zn, Ag) filtered (0.45 um) and quantified via ICP-MS.
3. **Passive Sampler Extraction:** Solvent extraction (acetonitrile, dichloromethane, or hexane:acetone) with ultrasonic agitation, followed by GC-MS or LC-MS/MS quantification.

### 9.2 Verification and Validation Process Protocols
Bioavailability data would need to satisfy verification and validation standards such as the following. These are draft proposals, not current submission requirements:
- **Laboratory Matrix Spikes & Duplicates:** Method blanks, matrix spike recoveries within 70% to 130%, and relative percent differences (RPDs) <= 20% for duplicate TOC/SEM analyses.
- **AVS Sample Handling:** AVS is highly oxygen-sensitive. Samples must be collected with zero headspace in airtight containers, immediately chilled to 4 degrees C, purged with nitrogen (N2) or argon gas, and analyzed within 14 days of collection.
- **Field Blind Duplicates:** Minimum of 1 field duplicate per 10 sediment sample cores.

### 9.3 Field Sediment Core and Surficial Horizon Sampling Protocols
Sampling would need to resolve the redox horizons that control bioavailability. The following are draft proposals, not current requirements:
1. **Surficial Biologically Active Zone (0 to 5 cm):** Collected using Teflon-coated Van Veen, Ponar, or box corers for surface benthic screening, TOC, grain size, and direct contact evaluation.
2. **Suboxic Profile Cores (0 to 10 cm and 10 to 30 cm):** Collected using core tubes (gravity or piston corers) for AVS-SEM vertical profiling. Cores would need to be sectioned under inert nitrogen atmosphere where precise redox boundary resolution is required.

---

## 10.0 Uncertainties, Data Gaps & Five-Year Research Roadmap

**Chapter status.** This chapter is drafted as a single numbered list of research needs rather than as numbered subsections. The four needs listed below - marine cold-water desorption kinetics, dissolved organic carbon and colloidal facilitated transport, climate change and sediment resuspension, and analytical harmonization across B.C. commercial laboratories - are all drafted.

1. **Marine Cold-Water Desorption Kinetics:** The vast majority of international EqP and AVS-SEM calibration datasets were derived at 20 degrees C. B.C. coastal fjord and deep marine waters frequently exhibit bottom temperatures of 4 to 8 degrees C. Lower temperatures decrease desorption rates (k_off) and increase sorption affinity (K_oc), making temperate EqP standards slightly conservative. Cold-water thermodynamic temperature corrections (Delta H_sorption) remain an open research need; no Phase 3 work has been scoped or approved.
2. **Dissolved Organic Carbon (DOC) and Colloidal Transport:** In estuaries with heavy riverine humic inputs (e.g., Skeena and Fraser River plumes), high porewater DOC (> 20 mg/L) binds hydrophobic contaminants, reducing bioavailable C_free below standard EqP predictions.
3. **Climate Change & Estuarine Resuspension:** Increased storm surge frequencies, sea-level rise, and dredging events can oxidize AVS-bound metal reservoirs. Long-term benthic recovery in dynamic sediment regimes is an open research need; the *Contaminated Sites Regulation*'s five-year standards review cycle (CSR s. 68) is one possible vehicle for revisiting it.
4. **Laboratory Capacity Harmonization:** Inter-laboratory round-robin studies with B.C. commercial laboratories would be needed to standardize AVS-SEM extraction protocols. No such study has been carried out or committed to, and no laboratory has been approached for this project.

---

# Matrix Options evidence and method boundary

**Status:** REVIEW_READY_NOT_GREEN. This is a Phase 2 options paper. It does not
adopt a standard, score a substance, select a parameter, or release a worked
calculation. Every future method choice requires a source location, units,
applicability statement, and independent review.

Status vocabulary: `VERIFIED_PRIMARY`, `VERIFIED_SECONDARY`,
`WORKING_CONVENTION`, `OPTION_OPEN`, `SOURCE_GAP`, and `WITHDRAW`.

## Appendix B: Bioaccumulation evidence inventory

### B.1 Purpose

This appendix identifies evidence needed to evaluate sediment-to-tissue and
food-web methods. It is not a parameter table and does not carry executable
equations or numerical outputs.

### B.2 Evidence categories

[STATUS: OPTION_OPEN] Candidate evidence categories include paired sediment and
tissue observations, species and tissue identity, lipid or protein basis,
organic-carbon basis, food-web position, exposure duration, metabolism, and
site linkage.

[STATUS: SOURCE_GAP] The underlying studies and databases must be held and
checked at the record level before any factor is used. A literature review or
database label alone does not establish a value, unit, receptor, or transfer
anchor.

### B.3 Method options

[STATUS: OPTION_OPEN] Reviewers may compare empirical transfer, tissue residue,
mechanistic food-web, and tiered methods. They must verify or replace each
method with a source location and dimensional review.

### B.4 Quarantine boundary

[STATUS: WITHDRAW] Earlier BSAF, TMF, FCM, SUF, trophic-level, receptor, and
worked-calculation tables were removed from active Phase 2 text because their
provenance or cross-surface reconciliation was incomplete. Their exact prior
text is preserved in the dated source-successor archive.

# Matrix Options evidence and method boundary

**Status:** REVIEW_READY_NOT_GREEN. This is a Phase 2 options paper. It does not

## Appendix C: Substance classification framework

### C.1 Purpose and boundary

Classification is an evidence-routing function. This appendix does not score,
rank, allocate, or assign a substance to a regulatory category.

### C.2 Evidence gates

[STATUS: OPTION_OPEN] Future gates may consider substance characteristics,
bioaccumulation evidence, persistence, potency, analytical feasibility, and
exposure pathways. Each gate requires a held source, source location, units,
applicability, and an independently reviewable decision rule.

[STATUS: SOURCE_GAP] The former candidate allocation tables contained mixed
bases and unverified factors. They are withdrawn from active Phase 2 use. No
BAF, BCF, BSAF, TMF, FCM, threshold, pass/fail result, or category assignment
is active in this appendix.

### C.3 Open review questions

Reviewers should verify, reject, or replace the proposed evidence categories,
gate order, missing-data treatment, and category definitions, citing the exact
source location for each decision.

# Appendix D: Substance Prioritization Framework
## Unscored options and evidence requirements for Phase 2

**Status: DRAFT.** This appendix defines options for ordering future work. It does not score substances,
allocate tranches, rank candidates, or assign any substance to a Schedule 3.4 part. The former scored
apparatus and allocation table were suspended because the occurrence dataset, evidence attribution,
weights, thresholds, arithmetic, and sensitivity analysis were not validated. The complete pre-edit
apparatus is preserved in the adjacent remediation record and remains Phase 3 or later working material.

## 1.0 Purpose and boundary

Prioritization is a work-sequencing function separate from substance classification and standards
derivation. Phase 2 may compare candidate methods and define the evidence needed to apply one. It may
not present a numeric priority score, a tranche assignment, or a completed priority list as an output.

## 2.0 Options for consideration

### Option A: hazard-first ordering

Order future research by persistence, toxicity, bioaccumulation potential, and exposure consequence.
This is transparent but may overlook local occurrence and feasibility.

### Option B: occurrence-first ordering

Order future research by documented occurrence in a quality-screened B.C. sediment dataset. The BC Aquatic
Database does not yet exist, so no occurrence ranking is available in this draft.

### Option C: evidence-gated multi-criteria ordering

Use the plan's biomagnification, toxicity, persistence, and local-occurrence criteria after each input has
an identified source, unit, applicability, and evidence status. Feasibility and resource burden should be
shown as a separate sequencing filter unless the TWG expressly decides otherwise. This is an open option,
not a selected method.

## 3.0 Evidence requirements before any scoring

Before any future score is calculated, the project should hold and document:

1. a quality-screened B.C. occurrence dataset and its coverage limits;
2. a source and location for each dimension input;
3. the selected weights and the reason for each weight;
4. thresholds and category rules that reproduce from the stated method;
5. an arithmetic test set and independent recalculation; and
6. sensitivity analysis showing how alternative weights and missing data change ordering.

Until these conditions are met, candidate substances may be described as research candidates only. No
candidate is assigned to a tranche, and the former tranche label is not an entry condition anywhere in the
Phase 2 adoption workflow. The adoption workflow uses Classification Category 2 as its proposed category
gate, subject to review and not as a prioritization result.

## 4.0 Open review questions

The TWG should decide whether prioritization should use the plan's four criteria directly, whether a
separate feasibility filter is appropriate, how missing evidence is represented, and what evidence is
enough to support a future ordering. These questions are transferred to the front-end review package;
the front end must solicit evidence and objections, not endorsement of unsupported scores.

## Mathematical Formulations, Exposure Parameterization, and Derivation Algorithms for British Columbia Matrix Sediment Standards (Focusing on Sections E.3, E.4, and E.5)

> **Purpose.** The candidate derivation formulations and exposure parameters a matrix standard could be built on - as candidates, not proposals.
> **[STATUS: SOURCE-GAP QUARANTINE - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]** Rate records, Di Toro and related authority claims, crab modifiers, polymer coefficients, and other decision-driving inputs below are not accepted as verified inputs. Exact primary-source locations, scope, units, and applicability must be verified before reuse.
> **Supports.** Section 7.5.  **Material.** Technical specification.
> **What to look at.** The equation forms at E.5.1 and the option set at E.4.2, and the recorded disagreement between the E.5.3 Pathway 2 values and the other surfaces that carry the same quantity.
> **Principal limitations, in this appendix's own words.** The Pathway 2 values here **disagree with every other surface that carries the same quantity, and the cause is unresolved**; the trophic levels used in Pathway 4 are not sourced; no consumption rate is fixed per sediment use category.


**Document type:** Supporting technical appendix to the Matrix Sediment Standards Options Paper (Section 7.5).  
**Status: DRAFT.** Prepared under Task 2 of the Sediment Standards Project, Phase 2 (2026-2027), an ENV Site Remediation Program project in partnership with SABCS. **Nothing in this appendix has been reviewed, endorsed, validated or adopted by anyone** - not ENV, not SABCS, not the SSTAC, not the Technical Working Group. No review cycle has taken place.  
**Draft date:** August 2026.  
**Applicability:** British Columbia's sediment standards apply to freshwater, estuarine and marine environments. The formulations set out below are intended to be read across all three. Where a parameter, receptor or protection level is salinity-specific, that is a parameterization choice within the formulation, not a restriction on where the framework applies.  

**Applicability boundary.** Direct-contact pathways are mandatory within the framework. Food pathways are conditional on applicable criteria and site conditions. All standards that apply apply; the lowest applicable value is a practical remediation focus only, not a hierarchy. Site assignment remains within the CSR framework. Protocols 18 and 21 are not sediment authority and are not presented here as such.

**What this appendix contains, and what it does not.**  
Phase 2's deliverable is the framework, including the options for that framework - not standards. What follows is **the set of derivation formulations currently under consideration**: the mathematical structures that a matrix standard could be built on, together with the parameters each one demands. They are candidates for discussion, not selections.

The options that need to be evaluated for this framework sit in several overlapping layers, of which equation form is only one - and for several pathways the equation form is largely settled by the physics, so there are few genuine alternatives to weigh. ## E.1.0 Mathematical Architecture of the Four-Pathway Matrix System

### E.1.1 The Four Receptor-Pathway Combinations
The approach examined in this appendix would replace the empirical co-occurrence basis of CSR Schedule 3.4 with mechanistically derived values for prioritized substances under a proposed Schedule 3.4 Part 1. Schedule 3.4 Part 1 does not exist, and no such replacement has been decided on.

Standards are calculated independently across **four discrete receptor-pathway combinations**:
1. **Pathway 1 ($C_{sed, HH-direct}$):** Human Health Direct Contact (Incidental ingestion and dermal adherence during wading, recreation, and commercial work).
2. **Pathway 2 ($C_{sed, HH-food}$):** Human Health Food-Based Bioaccumulation (Ingestion of harvested seafood, parameterized by a candidate seafood-ingestion record for $IR_{food}$, to be assigned only after primary verification - see E.4.2).
3. **Pathway 3 ($C_{sed, eco-direct}$):** Ecological Direct Contact (Toxicity to benthic macroinvertebrates via interstitial porewater equilibrium partitioning).
4. **Pathway 4 ($C_{sed, eco-food}$):** Ecological Food-Based Bioaccumulation (Dietary biomagnification in piscivorous birds and semi-aquatic mammals).

### E.1.2 The Four Sediment Use Categories
Values are parameterized across **four rough-draft Sediment Use Categories proposed for discussion**. They are not defined in law and do not appear in the *Contaminated Sites Regulation*. Each category can occur in freshwater, estuarine or marine settings; none of the four is confined to one salinity regime.

**Each category is defined by its setting and by the receptors present. None of the four is defined by a consumption rate.** Where Pathway 2 applies, a future scenario-specific seafood ingestion rate $IR_{food}$ would be assigned only after primary verification of a candidate record in E.4.2, and the category does not fix it. The four categories themselves are unchanged in name, number and order.
1. **Aquatic Wildlands (AW):** Pristine conservation, protected riparian reserves, and baseline traditional indigenous harvesting areas. Governed by high ecological protection (99% species protection) and by the presence of country-food harvesting receptors. No baseline consumption rate is fixed for this category; where Pathway 2 applies, $IR_{food}$ would be assigned only after primary verification of a candidate record in E.4.2.
2. **Aquatic Recreational / Traditional Harvest (AR/TH):** Shoreline zones, public beaches, estuaries, intertidal clam flats, and primary First Nations food, social, and ceremonial (FSC) harvesting beds. Governed by the presence of high-intake human harvesting receptors alongside ecological receptors. Where Pathway 2 applies, $IR_{food}$ is selected for the scenario from the option set at E.4.2; a candidate subsistence-fisher option from B.C. WLRS (2023), Table 2, is described but not exercised or accepted pending primary-source verification. Species-resolved guild apportionment (E.4.3) and crab hepatopancreas modifiers (E.4.4) are site-specific risk assessment material, not derivation inputs, and are not attached to this category.
3. **Commercial Aquatic (CA):** Active commercial shipping channels, marinas, urban waterfronts, and commercial fishing zones. Governed by standard ecological protection (90-95% species protection) and by general-population rather than subsistence human receptors. The 40 g/day rate is **not carried** - it has no catalog record and its Health Canada PQRA attribution was never confirmed - and **no substitute has been chosen**; where Pathway 2 applies, $IR_{food}$ would be assigned only after primary verification of a candidate record in E.4.2.
4. **Industrial Aquatic (IA):** Heavily modified industrial berths, pulp mill log-handling basins, chemical terminals, and deep dredging shipways. Governed by worker direct contact protection and direct benthic protection (80-90% species protection). Industrial Aquatic settings can be freshwater, estuarine or marine, which is why the category is not named for any one of them.

### E.1.3 Default Sediment Organic Carbon Baseline ($f_{oc}$ = 0.01 / 1.0% TOC)
This appendix uses a working baseline of 1.0% Total Organic Carbon (TOC) ($f_{oc}$ = 0.01 g-OC/g-sediment) for organic carbon normalization, with site-specific adjustment proposed within bounded limits. This is a proposal of this project, not an existing provincial default: **Protocol 28, *2016 Standards Derivation Methods*, Version 3.0 (effective 1 May 2024)** contains no sediment organic carbon normalization and no sediment $f_{oc}$ - its only $f_{oc}$, 0.5%, is a soil-to-groundwater model default - and its Chapter 7 derives sediment standards as a CCME (1999) probable effect level multiplied by a probability factor (P20 = 0.62 freshwater; P50 = 1.2 freshwater and marine).

---

## E.2.0 Ecological Direct Contact Synthesis (Pathway 3)

*Drafted: E.2.1 and E.2.2. Not drafted: a species sensitivity distribution (SSD) and water quality criteria final chronic value (FCV) treatment. That is the one genuine equation-form alternative identified for Pathway 3, and its absence is a known gap.*

*What would close it, and what would not.* The project holds the complete **U.S. EPA ECOTOX ASCII release of March 2025** - 1,206,189 results from 722,487 tests - together with the EnviroTox database and working code for fitting species sensitivity distributions. **That material can support the FCV half of this gap and cannot support the SSD half.** Only 45 of the 722,487 tests use sediment as the exposure medium, across 14 substances, so no benthic SSD can be fitted from it; the freshwater and salt-water records, which number in the hundreds of thousands, are the evidence base for a water-column chronic value that equilibrium partitioning then converts to a sediment benchmark. **Drafting the FCV treatment is therefore a tractable piece of work with the material already in hand. Drafting the sediment SSD treatment is not, and would require benthic bioassay data this project does not hold.**

### E.2.1 Equilibrium Partitioning (EqP) for Non-Ionic Organics
For non-polar hydrophobic organic contaminants, toxicity to benthic macroinvertebrates is governed by chemical activity in interstitial porewater. The sediment standard is calculated by:

$$C_{sed, eco-direct} = K_{oc} \times f_{oc, sed} \times FCV_{water} \times 10^{-3}$$

Where:
- $K_{oc}$ = Organic carbon-water partition coefficient (L/kg-OC).
- $f_{oc, sed}$ = Sediment organic carbon fraction (0.01 g-OC/g-sediment default).
- $FCV_{water}$ = Final Chronic Value from Ambient Water Quality Guidelines (mg/L).

### E.2.2 Acid Volatile Sulfides & Simultaneously Extracted Metals (AVS-SEM)
For cationic divalent transition metals (Cd^(2+), Cu^(2+), Ni^(2+), Pb^(2+), Zn^(2+)), direct benthic toxicity is prevented when Acid Volatile Sulfide molar concentrations equal or exceed simultaneously extracted metal concentrations:

$$\sum [SEM_i] - [AVS] \le 0 \quad (\mu\text{mol/g-dw})$$

When SUM [SEM] - [AVS] > 0, excess bioavailable metal partitions into porewater, triggering EqP-based porewater toxicity thresholds.

# Appendix F: Generic Standards Adoption Procedure

> **[STATUS: SOURCE-GAP QUARANTINE - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]** Current CSR currency, amendment, and peer-jurisdiction comparison claims in this appendix are not accepted as verified regulatory inputs. The procedure is a draft option set only until exact primary-source instruments and table locations are independently checked.
## A Standardized Protocol for Adopting and Adapting Peer Jurisdictional Sediment Quality Benchmarks into British Columbia CSR Schedule 3.4 Parts 2 and 3

> **Purpose.** A draft protocol and jurisdictional hierarchy for adopting peer-jurisdiction benchmarks into the proposed Parts 2 and 3.
> **Supports.** Section 7.6, with the procedure summarised at 9.6.  **Material.** Technical specification.
> **What to look at.** The Level 1-4 adoption hierarchy (3.0) and the note above the Section 6.0 table.
> **Principal limitations, in this appendix's own words.** The **Part 2 human-health column cannot be sourced as attributed and the whole column is in question** - none of the cited sources publishes a generic per-substance human-health sediment table; two ecological rows do not reproduce from the source they are credited to; Director's Interim Standards are not addressed.


**Document type:** Supporting technical appendix to the Matrix Sediment Standards Options Paper (Section 7.6).  
**Status: DRAFT.** Prepared under Task 2 of the Sediment Standards Project, Phase 2 (2026-2027), an ENV Site Remediation Program project in partnership with SABCS. **Nothing in this appendix has been reviewed, endorsed, validated or adopted by anyone** - not ENV, not SABCS, not the SSTAC, not the Technical Working Group. No review cycle has taken place.  
**Draft date:** August 2026.  
**How to read this appendix:** the source hierarchy, screening criteria, workflow and tabulated values are draft proposals for discussion. No value has been adopted into CSR Schedule 3.4, and no value in this appendix is a proposed or final standard. The plan schedules the generic-adoption procedure for January to March 2027.  
**Applicability:** British Columbia's sediment standards apply to freshwater, estuarine and marine environments, and any adoption procedure has to serve all three. That is why Section 3.0 treats freshwater / estuarine / marine differentiation as a screening criterion in its own right, and why the Section 6.0 table carries separate freshwater and marine ecological columns.  
**Known gap against the project plan.** The plan's Task 7.2 asks for a procedure for adopting Generic Numerical Sediment Standards from other jurisdictions (United States, Australia, New Zealand), with protection-level, species and other factor adjustments, and it names one specific lever: to *leverage Director's Interim Standards*. This appendix covers the jurisdictions and the adjustment factors, but **it does not yet address Director's Interim Standards at all** - they appear nowhere in it, and nowhere else in the Options Paper. How Director's Interim Standards would be used, and whether they offer a faster route into Parts 2 and 3 than a full adoption workflow, is undrafted work.

## 1.0 Executive Summary & Regulatory Framework

*Drafted: 1.1, which sets out the three-part architecture of a modernized CSR Schedule 3.4 together with the adoption architecture figure. Subsections 1.2 and 1.3 are not drafted.*

### 1.1 Scope of the Generic Adoption Appendix
The British Columbia Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV), in partnership with SABCS, is examining how contaminated sediment standards could be modernized under the *Contaminated Sites Regulation* (CSR, B.C. Reg. 375/96, as amended to B.C. Reg. 102/2026). Phase 2 develops the scientific framework only; ENV will subsequently follow its internal policy, consultation, and approval processes before recommending any regulatory changes to the CSR. 

The framework examined in Phase 2 would restructure CSR Schedule 3.4 into three parts. This is a proposal for discussion; no amendment to the CSR has been drafted, approved or made:
1. **Schedule 3.4 Part 1 (Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards):** Mechanistically derived numerical standards for prioritized substances, protecting human health and ecological life across four discrete exposure pathways (Pathways 1, 2, 3, and 4) and four Sediment Use Categories (AW, AR/TH, CA, IA).
2. **Schedule 3.4 Part 2 (Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to Protect Human Health):** Adopted or adapted numerical screening standards from leading peer jurisdictions for non-prioritized substances.
3. **Schedule 3.4 Part 3 (Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to Protect Ecological Health):** Adopted or adapted numerical standards from peer jurisdictions to protect freshwater and marine benthic aquatic life.

While high-priority, bioaccumulative, and high-occurrence contaminants (such as PCBs, PCDD/Fs, methylmercury, high-MW PAHs, PFAS, and bioavailable Cadmium) undergo full matrix standard derivation under Part 1 (as detailed in Appendices B, C, D, and E), hundreds of non-prioritized chemical substances would still need protective numerical thresholds for routine site investigation and screening.

This appendix sets out a draft **Generic Standards Adoption Procedure**: a proposed method and hierarchy of peer jurisdictions for adopting, adapting and mathematically harmonizing external sediment quality benchmarks into the proposed CSR Schedule 3.4 Parts 2 and 3.

The framework derives nothing de novo for sediment (Appendix K, Atlantic RBCA row). |

## 3.0 Hierarchy of Preference & Scientific Quality Screening Criteria

*Drafted: the four-tier hierarchy of preference set out immediately below, and the screening criteria in 3.1. Protection endpoint compatibility, freshwater / estuarine / marine differentiation and geochemical applicability to British Columbia sediments appear as numbered items within 3.1; no separate subsection has been drafted for any of them.*

Candidate benchmarks must pass a **standardized four-tier hierarchy of preference**:
- **Level 1: Canadian National Standards (CCME):** Interim Sediment Quality Guidelines (ISQG) and Probable Effect Levels (PEL).
- **Level 2: Regionally Contiguous Pacific Northwest Standards (Washington State SMS / Oregon DEQ):** Derived for cold-water Pacific Northwest estuarine and freshwater ecosystems.
- **Level 3: U.S. Federal & Canadian Provincial Frameworks (U.S. EPA ESBs / Atlantic RBCA / Ontario MECP):** Mechanistic equilibrium partitioning (U.S. EPA ESBs) and risk-based or effects-based screening values. **Note that Atlantic RBCA derives nothing of its own for sediment** - its Tier I EQS and Tier II PSS are adopted values, predominantly CCME, and its references include B.C.'s own Schedule 3.4. Adopting from it at this level risks re-importing values B.C. already holds. **And the values are CCME Probable Effect Levels**: every one of six spot-checked values (arsenic, cadmium and zinc, freshwater and marine) matches the CCME PEL exactly and none matches the ISQG, so adoption here would import **probable-effect-level** protection - CCME reports 65 % incidence of adverse effects above the PEL - rather than threshold-level. **And British Columbia already derives from that same base by a different route:** Protocol 28 Chapter 7.2 derives the in-force Schedule 3.4 values as a CCME probable effect level multiplied by a probability factor - 0.62 sensitive (freshwater; none derived for marine), 1.2 typical (freshwater and marine) - whereas Atlantic RBCA applies none. Adopting from it would place unfactored PELs beside B.C. values that carry a factor. B.C. can adopt from CCME directly and apply its own (Appendix K, Atlantic RBCA row).
- **Level 4: International (ANZG 2018 / Netherlands RIVM / EU Water Framework Directive):** Species Sensitivity Distribution (SSD) hazard concentrations. **There is no "ECHA Sediment EQS" instrument.** ECHA manages REACH/CLP registration, evaluation, authorisation and classification and does not set Environmental Quality Standards; EU sediment EQS under the Water Framework Directive (Directive 2008/105/EC as amended) are set by the European Commission. No European value is carried into this paper from either body. Netherlands RIVM is named here but is not covered in Appendix K, so its derivation basis is unexamined.

### 3.1 Scientific Quality Screening Criteria
1. **Derivation Transparency:** The underlying toxicological database, statistical derivation methodology (e.g., SSD 5th percentile, EqP, AET), and test endpoints (EC20, NOEC, LC50) must be fully documented in a publicly accessible technical rationale document.
2. **Protection Endpoint Compatibility:**
   - For Schedule 3.4 Part 2 (Human Health): Benchmark must protect human direct contact (incidental ingestion and dermal adherence) based on standard human body weights (70.7 kg) and risk levels (ILCR <= $1 \times 10^{-5}$ or HQ <= 0.2).
   - For Schedule 3.4 Part 3 (Ecological Health): Benchmark must protect benthic macroinvertebrate communities from chronic toxicological impairment with a minimum 90% to 95% species protection goal.
3. **Ecosystem & Salinity Differentiation:** Adopted standards must maintain strict separation between Freshwater and Marine/Estuarine ecosystems.
4. **Geochemical Applicability to B.C. Sediments:** Normalization models must be compatible with Pacific Northwest cold-water mineralogy and organic carbon dynamics (1.0% TOC baseline).

---

## 4.0 Step-by-Step Generic Adoption & Mathematical Harmonization Workflow

*Drafted as a single workflow figure. The six steps are set out in the figure below; no per-step subsection has been drafted for any of them.*

The adoption procedure follows a **six-step protocol**. It is stated in prose at Section 7.6.2 of the body and summarised at Section 9.6.

- Node s1: STEP 1: SUBSTANCE IDENTIFICATION
- Criterion: Confirm substance is classified as Classification Category 2 and lacks Part 1 matrix derivation.
- Node s2: STEP 2: JURISDICTIONAL HIERARCHY SEARCH
- Criterion: Search peer sources in descending order (Level 1 CCME -> Level 2 WA/OR -> Level 3 EPA -> Level 4).
- Node s3: STEP 3: TOXICOLOGICAL & PROTECTION SCREENING
- Criterion: Verify derivation transparency, chronic endpoint validity, and salinity differentiation.
- Node s4: STEP 4: HARMONIZATION & RECALCULATION
- Criterion: Convert reporting units to standard mg/kg-dw; normalize non-polar organics to 1.0% TOC baseline.
- Criterion: Recalculate onto B.C. exposure assumptions where the peer value embeds them.
- Node s5: STEP 5: REGIONAL BACKGROUND & FEASIBILITY VERIFICATION
- Criterion: Compare candidate benchmark against ambient B.C. sediment background and commercial lab PQLs.
- Node s6: STEP 6: DOCUMENTATION, REVIEW & PUBLICATION
- Criterion: Record complete source metadata and derivation citation; submit rationale for review.
- Criterion: Publish in Schedule 3.4 Part 2 or 3.
