## 5.0 Mathematical Normalization & Unit Harmonization Equations

*Drafted: 5.1 and 5.2. A dry-weight to organic-carbon harmonization subsection is not drafted.*

### 5.1 Organic Carbon Normalization: Carbon-to-Dry Weight Conversion
When external jurisdictions publish standards normalized to carbon ($C_{OC}$, mg/kg-OC, such as Washington State SMS):

$$C_{sed, dry weight} = C_{OC} \times f_{oc, default} = C_{OC} \times 0.01$$

### 5.2 Equilibrium Partitioning Back-Calculation from Water Quality FCVs
When external jurisdictions publish dissolved water quality chronic standards ($FCV_{water}$):

$$C_{sed, dry weight} = K_{oc} \times f_{oc, default} \times FCV_{water} \times 10^{-3} = K_{oc} \times 0.01 \times FCV_{water} \times 10^{-3}$$

---

## 6.0 Master Generic Adopted Standards Table for Schedule 3.4 Parts 2 & 3

*Drafted as a single combined table. The proposed Part 2 (human health) and Part 3 (ecological, freshwater and marine) values are carried as separate columns of that one table; no separate subsection has been drafted for either part.*

Applying the draft six-step workflow to non-prioritized substances produces the illustrative table below (at 1.0% TOC).

*These are illustrative worked values for discussion, not proposed standards. Each entry must be verified against the cited source jurisdiction before any use; none has been verified for this draft, and no value has been adopted into CSR Schedule 3.4.*

Table: Master Generic Adopted Standards Table for Schedule 3.4 Parts 2 & 3.

> **The Part 2 human-health column cannot be sourced as attributed, and the whole column is in question.** Its values are credited, with the rest of this table, to CCME, Washington State's Sediment Management Standards, US EPA and ANZG. **None of those publishes a generic per-substance human-health sediment table.** CCME's and US EPA's sediment programs are ecological by their own titles; Washington's human-health provision, WAC 173-204-561, is a **risk-based calculation procedure, not a lookup table**; and no ANZG source document is held by this project at all. **No value in that column has been deleted or replaced** - the column is illustrative, nothing here is adopted, and substituting figures would be worse than disclosing the gap. But **the column should not be read as a set of adoptable human-health values**, and where a Part 2 value is wanted the six-step procedure at Section 7.6.2 has to be run against a source that actually derives one. The ecological columns are a different matter and largely check out - the low-molecular-weight PAH and chromium rows reproduce CCME's 2002 interim sediment quality guidelines exactly.
>
> **Two ecological rows do not check out, and both are attributed to Washington's Sediment Management Standards at Level 2.**
>
> * **The phthalate esters.** The four ecological values do not reproduce from WA SMS Table III under this paper's own stated conversion, $C_{dw} = C_{OC} \times 0.01$, and **the relative ordering across the four does not match the ordering in the source** - so no single conversion factor can produce this set from that table, whatever factor were chosen. Something other than the stated arithmetic has been applied, and what it was is not recorded.
> * **The BTEX volatile organics.** Benzene, toluene, ethylbenzene and total xylenes are credited to the same source. **WA SMS's two chemical-criteria tables - marine Table III and freshwater Table VI - contain no BTEX entries at all**, so those four values cannot have come from it by any conversion.
>
> **No value has been changed.** This table adopts nothing and is illustrative throughout; the defect is in the source attribution, and inventing a conversion to make the arithmetic work would be worse than saying it does not. Both rows need either the actual source identified or the values withdrawn.

| SUBSTANCE NAME | PART 2: HUMAN HEALTH - **see the note above; not sourceable as attributed** | PART 3: ECO FRESHWATER | PART 3: ECO MARINE | SOURCE & LEVEL |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| VOLATILE ORGANIC COMPOUNDS (VOCs) **[BTEX not in the cited source - see the note above the table]**; Benzene; Toluene; Ethylbenzene; Total Xylenes; Trichloroethylene (TCE); Tetrachloroethylene (PCE); 1,1,1-Trichloroethane (TCA); Vinyl Chloride | 0.50 mg/kg-dw 15.0 mg/kg-dw 10.0 mg/kg-dw 25.0 mg/kg-dw 1.2 mg/kg-dw 2.5 mg/kg-dw 50.0 mg/kg-dw 0.10 mg/kg-dw | 0.060 mg/kg-dw 0.35 mg/kg-dw 0.12 mg/kg-dw 0.25 mg/kg-dw 0.15 mg/kg-dw 0.20 mg/kg-dw 0.80 mg/kg-dw 0.020 mg/kg-dw | 0.060 mg/kg-dw 0.35 mg/kg-dw 0.12 mg/kg-dw 0.25 mg/kg-dw 0.15 mg/kg-dw 0.20 mg/kg-dw 0.80 mg/kg-dw 0.020 mg/kg-dw | Level 2 (WA SMS Level 2 (WA SMS Level 2 (WA SMS Level 2 (WA SMS Level 3 (EPA) Level 3 (EPA) Level 3 (EPA) Level 3 (EPA) |
| LOW-MOLECULAR-WEIGHT PAHs (LPAHs); Naphthalene; Acenaphthene; Acenaphthylene; Anthracene; Fluorene; Phenanthrene | 12.0 mg/kg-dw 20.0 mg/kg-dw 20.0 mg/kg-dw 45.0 mg/kg-dw 25.0 mg/kg-dw 30.0 mg/kg-dw | 0.035 mg/kg-dw 0.0067 mg/kg-dw 0.0059 mg/kg-dw 0.047 mg/kg-dw 0.021 mg/kg-dw 0.042 mg/kg-dw | 0.0346 mg/kg-dw 0.0067 mg/kg-dw 0.0059 mg/kg-dw 0.047 mg/kg-dw 0.021 mg/kg-dw 0.087 mg/kg-dw | Level 1 (CCME) Level 1 (CCME) Level 1 (CCME) Level 1 (CCME) Level 1 (CCME) Level 1 (CCME) |
| PHTHALATE ESTERS **[values do not reproduce - see the note above the table]**; Bis(2-ethylhexyl)phthalate (DEHP); Butyl benzyl phthalate (BBP); Di-n-butyl phthalate (DBP); Dimethyl phthalate (DMP) | 50.0 mg/kg-dw 35.0 mg/kg-dw 40.0 mg/kg-dw 80.0 mg/kg-dw | 1.30 mg/kg-dw 0.063 mg/kg-dw 0.14 mg/kg-dw 0.071 mg/kg-dw | 1.30 mg/kg-dw 0.063 mg/kg-dw 0.14 mg/kg-dw 0.071 mg/kg-dw | Level 2 (WA SMS Level 2 (WA SMS Level 2 (WA SMS Level 2 (WA SMS |
| INORGANICS & METALS (NON-PRIORITIZED); Antimony; Chromium (Total); Silver; Vanadium | 30.0 mg/kg-dw 150.0 mg/kg-dw 40.0 mg/kg-dw 200.0 mg/kg-dw | 2.0 mg/kg-dw 37.3 mg/kg-dw 0.50 mg/kg-dw 40.0 mg/kg-dw | 3.0 mg/kg-dw 52.3 mg/kg-dw 0.70 mg/kg-dw 50.0 mg/kg-dw | Level 4 (ANZG) Level 1 (CCME) Level 4 (ANZG) Level 4 (RIVM) |

**What adoption would displace, for the LPAHs already in Schedule 3.4.** Six low-molecular-weight PAHs are not new to the schedule - CSR Schedule 3.4 (Contaminated Sites Regulation, B.C. Reg. 375/96, Schedule 3.4, as amended B.C. Reg. 133/2022 App. s.12, am. B.C. Reg. 2/2023) already carries sensitive- and typical-use standards for naphthalene, acenaphthene, acenaphthylene, anthracene, fluorene and phenanthrene, in freshwater and in marine and estuarine sediment alike. Comparing those in-force values to the Part 3: Eco Freshwater figures above shows the illustrative CCME-ISQG substitution would tighten every one of them:

| Substance | Part 3: Eco Freshwater (Level 1, CCME ISQG) | In-force Sch. 3.4 FW Sensitive | In-force Sch. 3.4 FW Typical | In-force Sch. 3.4 M+E Sensitive | In-force Sch. 3.4 M+E Typical | Tightening, freshwater (Sensitive / Typical) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Naphthalene | 0.0346 mg/kg-dw | 0.24 | 0.47 | 0.24 | 0.47 | 6.9x / 13.6x |
| Acenaphthene | 0.00671 mg/kg-dw | 0.055 | 0.11 | 0.055 | 0.11 | 8.2x / 16.4x |
| Acenaphthylene | 0.00587 mg/kg-dw | 0.08 | 0.15 | 0.079 | 0.15 | 13.6x / 25.6x |
| Anthracene | 0.0469 mg/kg-dw | 0.15 | 0.29 | 0.15 | 0.29 | 3.2x / 6.2x |
| Fluorene | 0.0212 mg/kg-dw | 0.089 | 0.17 | 0.089 | 0.17 | 4.2x / 8.0x |
| Phenanthrene | 0.0419 mg/kg-dw | 0.32 | 0.62 | 0.34 | 0.65 | 7.6x / 14.8x |

**[STATUS: SOURCE GAP - PRIMARY VERIFICATION OPEN]** The former assertion that the in-force comparison column was current and fully verified is withdrawn from the active paper surface. The comparison table remains illustrative only; no value, currency statement, or amendment claim from it is accepted as a current regulatory input until the exact in-force B.C. Laws instrument and table locations are independently checked.

**Two corrections came out of the check.** The comparison column now carries the CCME freshwater interim sediment quality guidelines as published in the CCME 2002 summary tables rather than the rounded transcriptions used before - 34.6, 6.71, 5.87, 46.9, 21.2 and 41.9 ug/kg respectively. Recomputing on the published figures moves one ratio: naphthalene's typical-use tightening is **13.6x, not 13.4x**, which was an artefact of dividing by a rounded 0.035. No in-force value changed, because none was wrong.

**A note on how the schedule must be read.** Extract it with `pdftotext -table`. The default layout mode misaligns this table by four rows, so each substance is printed against another substance's CAS number and standards - benz(a)anthracene appears with acenaphthene's figures. The misreading is wrong and entirely plausible, and it is the same extraction trap recorded for *Protocol 28* Appendix 8A at Appendix H section 5.3.

---

## 7.0 Uncertainties, Maintenance Protocol, and Five-Year Re-Review

1. **Protocol governance:** If this procedure were taken forward, the workflow, source hierarchy and unit conversions would need to be codified in a director's protocol - Protocol 28, *2016 Standards Derivation Methods*, Version 3.0 (effective 1 May 2024) is the closest existing instrument. Whether to amend it is a decision for ENV; nothing in this appendix has been codified.
2. **Annual verification review:** This draft proposes an annual check of any adopted values against newly published CCME, U.S. EPA and ANZG releases. No such review has been established or committed to.
3. **Five-year re-review:** This draft proposes that any generic values in the proposed Parts 2 and 3 be re-evaluated on the *Contaminated Sites Regulation*'s five-year standards review cycle (CSR s. 68), to consider whether substances should move into a Part 1 matrix derivation. Parts 1 to 3 do not exist, and no re-review cycle has been established for them.

---

## 8.0 Comprehensive Scientific Bibliography

1. ANZG (Australian and New Zealand Governments). (2018). *Australian and New Zealand Guidelines for Fresh and Marine Water Quality: Toxicant Default Guideline Values for Sediment*. Canberra, ACT.
2. Atlantic Partners in RBCA Implementation (Atlantic PIRI). (2021, 2023). *Atlantic RBCA (Risk-Based Corrective Action) Environmental Quality Standards: Rationale and Guidance Document*. Halifax, NS.
3. Canadian Council of Ministers of the Environment (CCME). (1995; Reapproved 2002). *Protocol for the Derivation of Canadian Sediment Quality Guidelines for the Protection of Aquatic Life* (CCME EPC-98E). Winnipeg, MB.
4. European Chemicals Agency (ECHA). (2013). *Principles for Environmental Risk Assessment of the Sediment Compartment*. Helsinki, Finland. **Not held; entry unverified.** It is not in the project reference library, nothing in this paper rests on it, and it should be retrieved or withdrawn before circulation.
5. Health Canada. (2021). *Federal Contaminated Site Risk Assessment in Canada, Part I: Guidance on Human Health Preliminary Quantitative Risk Assessment (PQRA)*. Ottawa, ON.
6. Netherlands National Institute for Public Health and the Environment (RIVM). (2015). *Environmental Risk Limits for Soil, Sediment and Water*. Bilthoven, Netherlands.
7. Oregon Department of Environmental Quality (DEQ). (2007). *Guidance for Assessing Bioaccumulative Chemicals of Concern in Sediment*. Portland, OR.
8. U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (U.S. EPA). (2005). *Procedures for the Derivation of Equilibrium Partitioning Sediment Benchmarks (ESBs) for the Protection of Benthic Organisms* (EPA-600-R-02-011). Washington, DC.
9. Washington State Department of Ecology. (2013). *Sediment Management Standards (Chapter 173-204 WAC)*. Toxics Cleanup Program, Olympia, WA.

<div id="app-g" class="section-anchor"></div>

# Appendix G: BC Aquatic Database evidence architecture

**Status:** `OPTION_OPEN` / `SOURCE_GAP`. This appendix describes a future data
architecture and validation workflow. It does not contain an active equation,
numeric default, parameter value, pass/fail result, or derived standard.

## G.1 Purpose

The proposed database would hold source-linked sediment chemistry, geochemical
modifiers, toxicity observations, tissue observations, spatial metadata, and
provenance records. It does not yet exist as a validated database.

## G.2 Evidence requirements

Every future record must identify the source document and exact location, sample
identity, medium, units, analytical method, receptor, applicability, detection
status, and quality limitations. Missing or incompatible records remain
`SOURCE_GAP` or `WITHDRAW`.

## G.3 Method-testing options

[STATUS: OPTION_OPEN] Future work may test direct-contact, food-exposure,
ecological direct-contact, and ecological food-pathway method families. The
method authority and dimensional basis must be independently reviewed before a
database record can support a derivation.

## G.4 Boundary

No BSAF, BAF, BCF, TMF, FCM, SUF, M_eco, toxicity threshold, background value,
worked example, or numerical result is active in this appendix. Historical
technical text is preserved in the dated source-successor archive.

<div id="app-h" class="section-anchor"></div>

# Matrix Options evidence and method boundary

**Status:** REVIEW_READY_NOT_GREEN. This is a Phase 2 options paper. It does not
adopt a standard, score a substance, select a parameter, or release a worked
calculation. Every future method choice requires a source location, units,
applicability statement, and independent review.

Status vocabulary: `VERIFIED_PRIMARY`, `VERIFIED_SECONDARY`,
`WORKING_CONVENTION`, `OPTION_OPEN`, `SOURCE_GAP`, and `WITHDRAW`.

## Appendix H: Policy-ready input parameter compendium

### H.1 Boundary

This appendix is an evidence registry outline, not a numeric parameter table.
No default, equation, receptor value, allocation, or derived result is active.

### H.2 Human and ecological input categories

[STATUS: SOURCE_GAP] Required categories include body size, ingestion and
contact rates, exposure frequency and duration, tissue basis, organic-carbon
and lipid normalization, toxicity benchmark, receptor identity, food-web
position, and site linkage. Each future record must carry source, location,
units, medium, applicability, and uncertainty.

### H.3 Trophic transfer and site linkage

[STATUS: OPTION_OPEN] Future review may compare empirical, mechanistic, tissue
residue, or tiered treatment of trophic transfer and site use. No FCM, TMF, SUF,
trophic level, or default is selected here.

### H.4 Ecosystem modifiers

[STATUS: WITHDRAW] M_eco and BSAF_effective definitions are not active. The
competing forms were non-equivalent and are preserved in the evidence archive.
Reviewers must verify, reject, or replace any future modifier with a source and
dimensional check.

### H.5 Future registry requirements

[STATUS: SOURCE_GAP] A future active registry must distinguish verified primary
and secondary evidence from working conventions, open options, source gaps,
and withdrawn material. No calculation may consume an unverified record.

# Appendix I: Engagement Summary (Outline)
## Outline of the Planned Engagement Approach for Phase 2 (2026-2027)

> **Purpose.** A placeholder stating the *planned* engagement approach for Phase 2 - who participates, at which points, and how the eventual summary will be assembled.
> **Supports.** Task 9.1's engagement summary requirement.  **Material.** Planning outline.
> **What to look at.** Section 5.0's status of each planned engagement point, and Section 6.0 on how the summary will be compiled from records rather than reconstructed.
> **Principal limitations, in this appendix's own words.** **This appendix reports no engagement** - it records no meeting, review, consultation or endorsement; Technical Working Group and SSTAC membership for Phase 2 is not yet fixed.


**Document type:** Appendix to the Matrix Options Paper - planning outline (early DRAFT)  
**Project:** Sediment Standards Project, Phase 2 (2026-2027), Site Remediation Program (SRP), B.C. Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV), in partnership with the Science Advisory Board for Contaminated Sites (SABCS)  
**Status:** This appendix reports no engagement. It sets out the engagement that the project plan schedules. See Section 5.0 for the status of each planned engagement point at the date of this draft.

---

## 1.0 Purpose and Scope of This Appendix

### 1.1 What this appendix is
This appendix sets out the engagement approach planned for Phase 2 of the Sediment Standards Project: who takes part, at which points in the work plan they take part, and how the engagement summary required in the final Matrix Options Paper will be assembled.

It is an outline of intent. It is **not** a record of engagement. Nothing in it reports a meeting, a review, a consultation, a workshop, a coordination activity, an endorsement or a decision, because none of those has yet occurred under Phase 2.

### 1.2 Why this appendix exists
The Phase 2 project plan requires the final deliverable to carry an engagement summary:

> "The final deliverable for the Sediment Standards Project in Phase 2 is the Matrix Options Paper, which describes how the above objectives were completed including research findings, progress made, options considered, and detailed rationale supporting recommendations. The Matrix Options Paper will provide an engagement summary documenting the ongoing collaborative partnership between ENV and the SABCS, including the invaluable contributions by members of the SSTAC and the Technical Working Group (TWG)."

That summary is part of Task 9.1, "Draft final Matrix Options Paper (research findings, options considered, rationale, recommendations, engagement summary)", scheduled for months 10-11. This appendix holds its place, and states the approach, until there is engagement to report.

### 1.3 What this appendix does not do
* It does not report engagement. See Section 5.0.
* It does not offer a legal opinion, and it does not characterize the legal obligations of the Province or of any other party. Questions of consultation and approval sit with ENV's own processes: the project plan states that "ENV will subsequently follow its internal policy, consultation, and approval processes before recommending any regulatory changes to the CSR."
* It does not describe a regulatory promulgation pathway. The plan lists "Regulatory amendments", "Final numeric standards", "Supporting Technical Guidance or Protocol" and "Complete sediment database" as out of scope for Phase 2.

### 1.4 Source document for this appendix
Every statement in this appendix about participants, tasks, timelines and deliverables is taken from a single source:

* *Draft Project Plan - Sediment Standards Phase 2 (2026-2027), Site Remediation Program (SRP)*. B.C. Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV) and the Science Advisory Board for Contaminated Sites (SABCS).

**One statement in this appendix does not come from that source, and it is named here rather than left to be discovered.** The observation at section 2.1 that the ENV Project Lead is also a Co-Chair of the SSTAC is not in the project plan, which nowhere connects the two roles. It rests on the Phase 1 White Paper, *Preliminary Framework for Modernizing British Columbia's Sediment Standards* (v3.0, 2025), which lists the Phase 2 ENV Project Lead in its **Acknowledgements**, under the Science & Standards Technical Advisory Committee heading, as "SSTAC Co-Chair, ENV, SABCS", and on the project lead's own confirmation of the role as current. Everything else about participants, tasks, timelines and deliverables is from the plan alone.

The Phase 2 project plan is a planning instrument - it exists to set out vision and to coordinate internal resources and timelines, and it retains flexibility - so nothing in this appendix treats it as a binding or external instrument, and no authority is claimed from it.

Task and subtask numbers (Task 1.1, 2.2, 8.1 and so on) and all week and month numbers are that document's own numbering; months are counted from the July 2026 start of Phase 2. Quoted passages are quoted verbatim from it, with typographic quotation marks and apostrophes normalized to plain ASCII. No other source is cited in this appendix, and no engagement record is cited, because none exists.

---

## 2.0 Participants in the Phase 2 Collaboration

### 2.1 The partnership
Phase 2 is an ENV Site Remediation Program project carried out in partnership with the SABCS. The plan records that "the Site Remediation Program (SRP) within the BC Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV) partnered with the Science Advisory Board for Contaminated Sites (SABCS) in 2025 to collaboratively develop a scientific framework for modernizing the CSR standards", and that "Phase 1 of the Sediment Standards Project established an inclusive, collaborative process that successfully developed the foundational framework to modernize the sediment standards, which guides our current research."

The plan further records that "This modernization effort is led by the Science and Standards Technical Advisory Committee (SSTAC), established by the SABCS in partnership with ENV."

The ENV Project Lead for Phase 2 is also a Co-Chair of the SSTAC - one of two; the committee is co-chaired. **Source:** this is not in the project plan, which does not connect the two roles. It is recorded in the Phase 1 White Paper, *Preliminary Framework for Modernizing British Columbia's Sediment Standards* (v3.0, 2025), which lists the Phase 2 ENV Project Lead in its **Acknowledgements**, under the Science & Standards Technical Advisory Committee heading, as "SSTAC Co-Chair, ENV, SABCS", and confirmed as current by the project lead. That dual role is recorded here so that a reader can see where the ENV project team and the advisory committee overlap; it does not make any SSTAC view an ENV position, or the reverse, and no SSTAC view is reported anywhere in this paper.

### 2.2 ENV project team and advisors
The plan's governance section identifies the following roles. This appendix records governance and engagement structure only; internal staffing, capacity, and volunteer-workload modeling are not part of the Options Paper and are preserved in a separate internal companion artifact.

**Individuals are not named in this draft.** The plan names the people holding each role; this appendix records the roles only, because naming a person in a circulated document attributes work and positions to them before they have agreed to it. The names can be restored at finalization if each person confirms.

| Role in the plan | As described in the plan |
|---|---|
| Project Sponsor | Unit Head, Science, Standards & Risk Assessment |
| Project Lead | Senior Risk Assessment Officer (RAO), who is also **a Co-Chair of the SSTAC** (see the source note at section 2.1; the role is not recorded in the project plan) |
| Core Project Team | Risk Assessment Officers (RAOs) |
| Advisory | ENV staff |
| Key Advisors / Contributors | SABCS; SSTAC; TWG members; CSAP (engagement at defined points) |

### 2.3 The Technical Working Group
The plan describes the TWG as follows: "The project relies heavily on a volunteer-based Technical Working Group of over 50 members, drawing world-class expertise from industry, consulting, academia, government, and Indigenous organizations."

Its working structure is set out in the project scope as an ongoing activity from July 2026: "Coordination of the TWG with a sub-group 'core' that supports with research, drafting, and editing tasks, while the full TWG supports by sharing information and reviewing and providing feedback on draft materials".

TWG and SSTAC membership for Phase 2 is not yet fixed. Task 1.4 includes a "call for focused TWG expansion"; Task 1.6 is "Confirm SSTAC membership and resourcing" (week 2); and the plan's immediate next steps list "Confirm: TWG membership".

### 2.4 CSAP
The Contaminated Sites Approved Professionals (CSAP) Society is listed among the plan's key advisors and contributors as "CSAP (engagement at defined points)". The plan does not identify those points, and no CSAP engagement has taken place under Phase 2. **This appendix attributes no finding, position or comment to CSAP, and reports no CSAP engagement.** Any statement elsewhere in this paper that appears to attribute a position, a practice or a shortcoming to CSAP is unsupported and should be read as removed; no such attribution rests on any record.

### 3.1 Scheduled engagement points

### 3.2 Continuous engagement activities
Alongside the scheduled checkpoints, the plan provides for engagement that runs throughout Phase 2:

* **Briefings.** "Regular briefings with updates, progress and next steps for ENV, the SABCS and the SSTAC" - July 2026, ongoing.
* **TWG coordination.** The focused core sub-group and full TWG arrangement described in Section 2.3 - July 2026, ongoing.
* **Project management.** Task 10 lists Planning (10.1), Coordination (10.2), Review, non-specific (10.3) and Communications (10.4) as ongoing activities led by "ENV + SABCS/SSTAC".
* **TWG-supported workstreams.** Most subtasks under Tasks 3 through 9 are staffed "Internal + TWG" in the plan's tables, so TWG contribution is expected across much of the work and not only at the review checkpoints. The plan notes that "the two largest data tasks (3.4, 3.6, 3.8) are explicitly TWG-led with internal ENV review only."

> **The generalisation above is not uniform, and the exception matters.** **Task 7, the policy-integration task, is staffed "Internal" only on subtasks 7.1 and 7.3 through 7.6** - aligning the matrix outputs with the CSR structure, the multi-pathway standards concept, the policy-ready outline and the internal policy and legal review. **TWG staffing appears on 7.2 alone**, the procedure for adopting generic standards from other jurisdictions. Task 6.6 is likewise Internal only. Stated plainly: the TWG is heavily involved in the research, and substantially less so in the policy integration.

### 3.3 The immediate next engagement step
The plan's immediate next steps for Phase 2 begin with: "Circulate draft Options Paper for 2-week review (with focus on Sections 4 and 7)". This corresponds to Task 2.2 and is the next engagement action for the present draft.

---

## 4.0 Indigenous Engagement: Scope Statement

### 4.1 Scope
**There is no Indigenous engagement in this project, and none is within the scope of Phase 2.** Consultation with Indigenous Nations belongs to ENV's own policy, consultation and approval processes for any future regulatory change to the *Contaminated Sites Regulation*. Phase 2 develops the scientific framework only. The plan states that "ENV will subsequently follow its internal policy, consultation, and approval processes before recommending any regulatory changes to the CSR", and lists "Regulatory amendments" as out of scope.

This appendix makes no statement about the legal obligations of the Province or of any other party, and offers no legal opinion.

### 4.2 Indigenous consumption rates elsewhere in this paper are published parameters, not engagement
Elsewhere in this paper - principally in Appendices B, H and J - traditional and subsistence seafood consumption rates are used as human health exposure parameters. Those values are taken from published literature and published guidance, and they are cited there. Their use is a technical modelling choice within the framework.

Their presence in this paper does **not** represent, record, substitute for, or imply engagement, consultation, co-development or agreement with any Indigenous Nation, community or organization, and no such engagement has occurred. The corresponding item in the plan is a parameter task within framework design: Task 6.4, "Integrate Indigenous consumption modifiers and high-use scenarios" (month 7).

### 4.3 TWG membership is not consultation
The volunteer TWG draws expertise from "industry, consulting, academia, government, and Indigenous organizations". Participation by a TWG member affiliated with an Indigenous organization is participation in a volunteer technical expert group in that member's technical capacity. It is not, and is not presented in this paper as, consultation or engagement with an Indigenous Nation.

---

## 5.0 Status of Engagement at the Date of This Draft

### 5.1 Nothing is reported because nothing is yet on record
This is an early draft prepared under Task 2, at approximately month 2 of the 12-month Phase 2 schedule. No meeting, workshop, review cycle, consultation, coordination activity or endorsement is reported anywhere in this paper, and no such record is held for the purposes of this appendix. In particular:

* No TWG review cycle of this paper has been completed. Task 2.2, the first circulation, is the next scheduled review point (Section 3.3).
* The integration workshop (Task 8.1, month 9) has not taken place.
* The full TWG and SSTAC review (Task 9.2, month 11) has not taken place.
* No engagement with CSAP has taken place, and no CSAP finding is reported.
* No engagement, consultation or coordination with any other agency, laboratory, industry association or jurisdiction has taken place for the purposes of this project. Where this paper compares B.C.'s approach with the approaches of other bodies, it does so from their published documents, which are cited in the appendix concerned.

### 5.2 This draft carries no endorsement
This draft has not been reviewed, approved or endorsed by the SSTAC, the TWG, the SABCS, CSAP or ENV, and it carries no Ministry position. No statement in this paper represents the position of any of those bodies, or of any of their members. Options and recommendations in this paper are offered for discussion and refinement.

## 6.0 Preparation of the Final Engagement Summary

### 6.1 Requirement
The engagement summary is a required element of the final Matrix Options Paper (Section 1.2), and it is named among the contents of Task 9.1, scheduled for months 10-11, with the paper finalized under Task 9.3 at month 12.

### 6.2 How it will be written
The engagement summary will be written from records made as engagement occurs, rather than reconstructed afterwards. For each engagement point in Section 3.0, the intention is to record:

1. the date of the activity;
2. the body or group involved (SSTAC, focused TWG, full TWG, SABCS, CSAP, ENV internal);
3. who took part;
4. what material was circulated, and the length of the review window;
5. the comments received; and
6. how each comment was addressed in the paper, including comments not accepted and the reason.

Where a planned engagement point does not take place, or takes place in a different form, the final engagement summary will say so. Nothing will be reported in the final engagement summary that is not supported by such a record.

### 6.3 How the plan defines success for engagement
The plan's success criteria include two that this appendix will ultimately have to evidence:

> "Successful Workstream Integration: Facilitation of a comprehensive integration workshop (scheduled for month 9) that successfully aligns outputs across all parallel research streams, including bioavailability, bioaccumulation, data, and matrix framework design, resolving core technical gaps before final drafting."

> "Robust Expert Engagement: Sustained, productive collaboration with the SSTAC and the over 50 member TWG. Success is defined by the completion of all planned TWG review cycles and ensuring that the final framework reflects a defensible consensus among industry, academic, Indigenous, and government experts."

The completed engagement summary is the record against which those criteria will be assessed at the end of Phase 2.

<div id="app-k" class="section-anchor"></div>

# Appendix K: Jurisdictional Derivation Basis Compendium
## Reference Compilation of How Peer Jurisdictions Derived Their Sediment Values

> **Purpose.** How other jurisdictions actually derived their sediment values - statistical construct, dataset, receptor and effect level - as a prerequisite to any adoption decision.
> **Supports.** The generic-standards adoption procedure, Parts 2 and 3.  **Material.** Reference compendium.
> **What to look at.** The derivation tables at K.1-K.3, the B.C./CCME chain at K.1.3, and the adoption options at K.4.
> **Principal limitations, in this appendix's own words.** **It is not an adoption recommendation and ranks no jurisdiction**; several entries are marked *not retrieved*; and B.C.'s own Schedule 3.4 precedent is not fully verifiable at one link in its chain.


**Document type:** Supporting technical appendix to the Matrix Sediment Standards Options Paper.
**Status: DRAFT.** Prepared under Task 2 of the Sediment Standards Project, Phase 2 (2026-2027), an ENV Site Remediation Program project in partnership with SABCS. **Nothing in this appendix has been reviewed, endorsed, validated or adopted by anyone** - not ENV, not SABCS, not the SSTAC, not the Technical Working Group. No review cycle has taken place.
**Draft date:** August 2026.

---

## What this appendix is, and is not

This appendix compiles how other jurisdictions derived their sediment values - the statistical construct, the underlying dataset, the receptor and effect each value targets, and, where retrievable, the normalization and regulatory status attached to it. It exists as reference material supporting the Task 7.2 procedure for adopting Generic Numerical Sediment Standards (proposed CSR Schedule 3.4 Parts 2 and 3, see Appendix F), and supporting the Phase 3 adoption decisions that follow from that procedure.

**It is not an adoption recommendation.** No value in this appendix is proposed, endorsed, or recommended for adoption into Schedule 3.4, in any part, at any stage. Nothing here ranks jurisdictions or values against one another for adoption purposes.

**Why this compilation is a prerequisite, not an optional background chapter.** A value cannot be adopted or adjusted without knowing what it protects, against what effect, at what statistical confidence. Multiplying a borrowed number by a convenient factor is not a derivation if the number's own statistical meaning is unknown. BC has already done this once - Schedule 3.4 Part 1's in-force values are CCME probable effect levels (PELs) multiplied by 0.62 (sensitive sediment) or 1.2 (typical sediment) - and that precedent, including the parts of its own chain that could not be verified, is documented in Section K.1 below. Sections K.2 and K.3 extend the same treatment to the North American and Australia/New Zealand sources most likely to be consulted for Parts 2 and 3. Section K.4 lays out the adoption options this comparability picture supports. Section K.5 tabulates what each jurisdiction's values do not cover.

---

## K.1 - BC's own adoption precedent

*Source: research note A1, on B.C. adoption precedent.*

### K.1.1 What Protocol 28 Chapter 7 says

Source: *Protocol 28 for Contaminated Sites, 2016 Standards Derivation Methods, Version 3.0*, issued under section 64 of the *Environmental Management Act*, approved by the Director of Waste Management 30 April 2024, effective 1 May 2024. **[The title page names no ministry**; the attribution to the Ministry of Environment and Climate Change Strategy previously given here was not on the document, and that ministry name had in any case been superseded by the approval date.**]** Chapter 7, "Derivation of Generic Sediment Standards," pp. 94-95.
URL: https://www2.gov.bc.ca/assets/gov/environment/air-land-water/site-remediation/docs/protocols/p28__jan_2021_revisions_final_signed.pdf

Quoted in full:

> **7.1 Introduction**
> The CSR contains standards for sediment that are derived in consideration of the toxic effects from direct contact with contaminants in sediments by aquatic life species. The standards distinguish between two distinct sediment uses (sensitive sediment and typical sediment) and include standards for both freshwater and marine/estuarine aquatic life environments. The sediment standards are contained in CSR Schedule 3.4.
>
> **7.2 Standards derivation procedures**
> Generic numerical sediment standards are derived by multiplying a probable effect level from the Canadian Council for Ministers of the Environment, 1999, Environmental Quality Guidelines, for a substance by a defined probability of observing an EC20 in selected toxicity tests. Selected toxicity tests included a 28 to 42-day test for the freshwater amphipod, *Hyalella azteca*, and 10-day tests for two the marine amphipods *Ampelisca abdita,* and *Rhepoxynius abronius*. The probability is dependant on sediment use, resulting in the following use-specific criteria:
>
> 1. Generic numerical sediment standards, for sensitive sediment: a 20% probability of observing an EC20 (P20) (equal to 0.62 for freshwater, none derived for marine), and
> 2. Generic numerical sediment standards, for typical sediment: a 50% probability of observing an EC20 (P50) (equal to 1.2 for both freshwater and marine).
>
> Sediment standards are derived based on exposure scenarios for ecological organisms and are not considered protective of human health; therefore, CSR soil standards should be applied to sediment for risk assessment purposes (refer to Protocol 1 "Detailed Risk Assessment" for more information).

Establishing what this means requires three things Protocol 28 does not itself supply: (a) what the CCME PEL is and how it was derived; (b) what "a 20% [or 50%] probability of observing an EC20" means statistically and where 0.62 and 1.2 numerically come from; (c) what dataset underlies that probability calculation. K.1.2 addresses (a). K.1.3 states plainly what could and could not be established for (b)-(c).

### K.1.2 What the CCME PEL is, from what data, by what method

Source: *Protocol for the Derivation of Canadian Sediment Quality Guidelines for the Protection of Aquatic Life*, Canadian Council of Ministers of the Environment, CCME EPC-98E (1995; reprinted in *Canadian Environmental Quality Guidelines*, Chapter 6, CCME, 1999, Winnipeg).
URL: https://ccme.ca/en/res/protocol-for-the-derivation-of-canadian-sediment-quality-guidelines-for-the-protection-of-aquatic-life-en.pdf

CCME compiles matching sediment-chemistry and biological-effect data from field studies (mostly), spiked-sediment toxicity tests, and equilibrium-partitioning models into a chemical-by-chemical **Biological Effects Database for Sediments (BEDS)**:

> "Briefly, acceptable toxicological data that are incorporated into guideline derivation tables (as illustrated in Appendix A) are compiled on a chemical-by-chemical basis and are sorted according to ascending chemical concentrations... Entries identified by an asterisk (*) in the 'Effect/No Effect' column comprise the 'effect' data set... Entries for which no effects were observed comprise the no-effect data set..."

A study enters BEDS only if sediment chemistry and biological effect were measured at the same location at the same time, and if the chemical's concentration varied by at least a factor of 10 across sampling sites in that dataset. Minimum data requirements: at least 20 entries in the effect data set and 20 in the no-effect data set.

**The calculation.** TEL and PEL are geometric means of percentile cut-points:

> "**The TEL was calculated as the square root of the product (i.e., the geometric mean) of the lower 15th percentile concentration of the effect data set and the 50th percentile concentration of the no-effect data set. The PEL was calculated as the square root of the product (i.e., the geometric mean) of the 50th percentile concentration of the effect data set and the 85th percentile concentration of the no-effect data set.**"

So: $TEL = \sqrt{E_{15} \times NE_{50}}$; $PEL = \sqrt{E_{50} \times NE_{85}}$, where $E$ and $NE$ are the effect and no-effect concentration distributions pooled from BEDS.

**What ISQG and PEL represent probabilistically.** They are not fixed percentiles of a single dose-response curve; they bracket three empirically observed frequency-of-effect zones:

> "The national SQG and the PEL are used to define three ranges of chemical concentrations for a particular chemical, those that are rarely (<SQG), occasionally (between the SQG and the PEL), and frequently (>PEL) associated with adverse biological effects."

Worked example, cadmium (marine): TEL = 0.676 mg/kg, PEL = 4.21 mg/kg - "only 5.6% of the cadmium concentrations within the no-effect range... were associated with adverse biological effects... In the possible- and probable-effect range for cadmium, the incidence of adverse biological effects was 20.1% and 70.8%, respectively." Mercury: "7.8%, 23.6%, and 36.7% adverse effects were observed in the no-effect, possible-effect, and probable-effect ranges, respectively (the TEL = 0.13 mg/kg-dw and the PEL = 0.696 mg/kg-dw)."

The PEL is therefore **not** a fixed-percentile toxicological threshold in the way a spiked-sediment dose-response EC/LC value is - it is a geometric-mean boundary derived from the shape of the pooled co-occurrence dataset. The species and endpoints behind BEDS entries are heterogeneous field co-occurrence studies, not a single controlled species/endpoint: "cause-and-effect relationships cannot be inferred from this data" (CCME protocol, Appendix A) - BEDS establishes association, not causation.

### K.1.3 BC's probability factor (P20/P50): CONFIRMED versus NOT RETRIEVED

Protocol 28 names three specific species/tests (*Hyalella azteca*, *Ampelisca abdita*, *Rhepoxynius abronius*) distinct from the heterogeneous field co-occurrence data underlying the CCME PEL. BC's 0.62 and 1.2 factors are therefore **not** derived from the same dataset as the CCME PEL - they come from a separate, BC-specific statistical treatment of species-specific EC20 toxicity-test data, applied as a multiplier onto the CCME PEL.

The source document for B.C.'s probability factors - MacDonald Environmental Sciences Ltd.,
*Development and Applications of Sediment Quality Criteria for Managing Contaminated Sediment in
British Columbia*, prepared for the B.C. Ministry of Water, Land and Air Protection, 2003, 112 pp.
- **is held in the project reference library and has been read directly.** The four questions set
out below are answered in it.

**The derivation, from the report itself.** Toxicity incidence was modelled against the geometric
mean of mean PEL-quotients by logistic regression, in the form $y = y_0 + a/[1+(x/x_0)^b]$:

* **Freshwater**, 28- to 42-day tests with *Hyalella azteca* (its Figure 3; $r^2 = 0.99$,
  $p = 0.0001$): **P50 = 1.31, P20 = 0.6**.
* **Marine and estuarine**, 10-day tests with *Ampelisca abdita* and *Rhepoxynius abronius*
  (its Figure 4; $r^2 = 0.58$, $p < 0.0001$): **P50 = 1.15**. Verbatim: *"Although it was not
  possible to generate a P20 value from the resultant concentration-response relationship, a P50
  value of 1.15 was calculated using the resultant logistic model."* **That is the answer to why
  no marine sensitive-sediment factor was derived** - the marine concentration-response
  relationship would not yield one.

**How the two published factors were then formed** - and this is where the report is more precise
than Protocol 28's one-page summary:

* **0.62, sensitive sites.** Verbatim: *"the freshwater SedQC-SCS were derived by multiplying the
  freshwater PEL for each COPC by the mean PEL-Q that corresponded to a 20% probability of
  observing toxicity to freshwater amphipods (i.e., 0.62)."* For marine and estuarine sensitive
  sites the **same freshwater factor was applied**: *"the SedQC were derived by multiplying the
  marine and estuarine PEL by the P20 for freshwater amphipods (i.e., 0.62) ... because it was
  not possible to calculate a marine and estuarine P20 value and because the freshwater,
  estuarine, and marine P50 values were nearly the same."* **Protocol 28's "none derived for
  marine" is correct about derivation and silent about application:** no marine P20 was derived,
  and the freshwater one was used in its place.
* **1.2, typical sites.** Verbatim: *"derived by multiplying the PEL for each COPC by the average
  of the P50 values for freshwater and for marine and estuarine amphipods (i.e., 1.2). The P50
  values for freshwater and for marine or estuarine were averaged because they were not
  considered to be statistically different from one another (i.e., 1.15 vs. 1.30)."* **1.2 is
  therefore not a P50 at all. It is the average of two.** Protocol 28's phrasing - "a 50%
  probability of observing an EC20 (P50) (equal to 1.2 for both freshwater and marine)" - reads
  as though a single P50 of 1.2 was measured in both media. Neither medium produced 1.2.
* **Substance-independent.** Verbatim: *"the SedQC is calculated by multiplying the PEL by the
  appropriate factor (i.e., 0.62 for sensitive sites and 1.2 for typical sites)"*, and its Table 4
  note: *"established by multiplying the PEL (CCME 1999) by 0.62."* The factors are fixed
  multipliers, not per-substance values - which was the second of the four open questions.

**What still is not established.** The report gives the model form, the fit statistics and the
resulting probabilities, but this pass has not extracted the underlying matched
sediment-chemistry-and-toxicity records the regressions were fitted to, nor confirmed how many
data pairs sit behind each curve. The marine curve's $r^2 = 0.58$ against the freshwater
$r^2 = 0.99$ is a material difference in fit quality and is stated here without further comment,
because what follows from it is a judgement for the technical working group.

**Protocol 28 remains the authority for the values.** Nothing above changes 0.62 or 1.2, which
are what the in-force protocol publishes and what the enacted Schedule 3.4 reproduces. What has
changed is that the paper can now describe *how they were arrived at* instead of recording that
it cannot.

**What is confirmed independently:** the arithmetic chain CCME PEL x 0.62 (sensitive) and CCME
PEL x 1.2 (typical) reproducing the enacted Schedule 3.4 values cell-for-cell for total PCBs,
benzo[a]pyrene, mercury, and cadmium has been verified per the project lead, and is consistent
with the mechanism above. This does not re-perform that arithmetic verification; it documents the
*mechanism* the verification confirmed operates.

**Finding for the project.** This gap is closed. The basis of BC's own current Schedule 3.4 standards is now documented from the derivation report itself, not inferred - which matters because that basis is the precedent every question of adopting an external value is measured against. What the report also shows is that the in-force factors rest on a marine concentration-response relationship that could not yield a sensitive-site probability at all, and on an average of two media's P50 values presented in the protocol as a single figure. Both are relevant to how much weight the existing standards can carry as a precedent, and neither was visible from Protocol 28 alone.

### K.1.4 The adoption chain, and what it does not do

1. CCME's underlying effects data (field co-occurrence, some spiked-sediment/EqP data) -> CCME ISQG/PEL (geometric-mean percentile cut-points, E15xNE50 / E50xNE85) -> BC's probability factor (0.62 sensitive / 1.2 typical, from species-specific EC20 test probabilities - **the derivation is documented at section K.1.3 from the MacDonald (2003) report**) -> **enacted Schedule 3.4 value** (CCME PEL x factor, rounded per Protocol 28 Chapter 10). No further adjustment for BC-specific background, bioavailability, or sediment characteristics is applied, unlike the soil chapters (Chapter 9).

**Coverage of the held copy.** All 211 pages were searched. *Sediment* appears 28 times on five pages: the contents page; two pages of Chapter 1 framing generic standards for "soil, water, vapour and sediment" and noting that sediment carries differential protection by management goal; the vapour chapter, which assigns human-health-specific vapour standards to freshwater or marine sediment at sensitive and typical sediment use sites; and Chapter 7 itself, which carries 15 of the 28. **Chapter 7 is accordingly the whole of the protocol's sediment derivation content, though not the whole of its sediment content.**

2. **Limits of the precedent, per Protocol 28's own text:**
   - **Not protective of human health** - stated in the same paragraph as the derivation procedure: "Sediment standards are derived based on exposure scenarios for ecological organisms and **are not considered protective of human health**; therefore, CSR soil standards should be applied to sediment for risk assessment purposes." No human-exposure pathway (ingestion, dermal contact, fish consumption) exists anywhere in this chain.
   - **Not linked to specific BC sediment characteristics** - CCME: "Currently, the available information indicates that only ISQGs can be developed" (i.e., not the field-verified full SQGs that would require TOC, particle size, pH, or AVS linkage). No TOC-normalization or bioavailability adjustment appears anywhere in the BC chain.
   - **Not based on cause-and-effect toxicology for most of the underlying data** - "'associative' relationships only"; "cause-and-effect relationships cannot be inferred from this data."
   - **Not addressing bioaccumulation / food-web transfer** - "the SQGs and ISQGs derived using the NSTP approach and the SSTT approach do not specifically address the potential for adverse effects on higher trophic levels of the food chain as a result of the bioaccumulation of persistent toxic chemicals."
   - **Receptor scope is benthic/epibenthic aquatic life only** - not birds, wildlife consumers of aquatic life, or humans.

---

## K.2 - North American derivation bases

*Source: research note A2, on North American methods. Purpose stated in that note: to record how each value was derived, not what BC should adopt. No values are recommended here.*

| Source | Derivation basis | What it protects | Effect level / statistical basis | Normalization | Regulatory status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **CCME ISQG/TEL/PEL** | Co-occurrence / effects-database approach, a modification of NOAA's NSTP screening-level approach; >=20 effect and >=20 no-effect entries per substance required. | Benthic and other sediment-associated aquatic life generally (database-wide, not a single species). | TEL/PEL geometric-mean percentile cut-points (see K.1.2). Reported incidence of adverse effects: below TEL 22%, TEL-PEL 24%, above PEL 65%, "prior to the application of a safety factor." | Dry weight; organic-carbon normalization applied for organics where noted per substance (not independently re-verified here for every chemical). | National guideline, not itself legally binding; BC has adopted derived values into the in-force Schedule 3.4. |
| **US EPA Equilibrium Partitioning Sediment Benchmarks (ESBs)** | Equilibrium partitioning (EqP) theory, not an effects/co-occurrence database. Metals: excess SEM (simultaneously extracted metals) minus AVS (acid-volatile sulfide). Tier 1 (endrin, dieldrin, PAH mixtures, metal mixtures) and Tier 2 (other nonionic organics, greater uncertainty). | Benthic organisms, via protection from porewater toxicity. | Partitioning to a bioavailable (porewater) concentration compared to water-quality-criteria-type toxicity thresholds - not a percentile of a field effects database. NOT RETRIEVED: the specific safety-factor/percentile structure within EqP toxicity-threshold selection per Tier. | Organic-carbon normalization (Koc-based, nonionic organics); AVS-SEM normalization (metal mixtures) - fundamentally different from CCME's dry-weight approach. | EPA benchmarks/guidance, not promulgated numeric standards; used in Superfund/sediment risk assessment. |
| **NOAA ERL/ERM (Long & Morgan / Long et al. 1995)** | Co-occurrence/effects-database, percentile method (same family as CCME's NSTP antecedent, not an identical procedure). "The 10th percentile and the 50th percentile (median) of the effects database were identified for each substance." | Marine/estuarine benthic and aquatic life generally (1995 update used saltwater data only). | ERL = 10th percentile ("adverse effects rarely occur" below); ERM = 50th percentile/median ("effects frequently occur" above) - pure percentile cut points, not a no-effect/probable-effect pairing in CCME's sense. | Dry weight as compiled; NOT RETRIEVED whether organic-carbon normalization was applied uniformly for organics in the 1995 compilation. | Advisory screening guideline, not promulgated; widely used as a screening tool (e.g., EPA Superfund ecological risk screening). |
| **Washington Ecology SMS, Ch. 173-204 WAC (SQS, CSL)** | Apparent Effects Threshold (AET) - a paired chemistry/biology approach, distinct from CCME/NOAA percentile-of-co-occurrence and from EPA's EqP method. AET = concentration above which a specific biological effect is *always* observed in the station-paired dataset, taken as the highest (most conservative) AET across endpoints considered. | SQS: "no adverse effects, including acute or chronic adverse effects on biological resources and no significant health risk to humans" (as promulgated for marine sediment). | SQS = concentrations below which adverse effects are "considered unlikely"; CSL = concentrations above which they are "considered significant" (cleanup trigger ceiling). Threshold-per-bioassay-endpoint from a regional (Puget Sound) paired dataset, not a national-database percentile. | Dry weight; organic-carbon normalization applied to organics in some Ecology tables (not independently re-verified per chemical). | Promulgated state standard - SQS is legally binding; CSL is the cleanup-level ceiling under the same chapter. WAC 173-204 as promulgated governs marine and marine-influenced (estuarine) sediments; a confirmed, separately promulgated freshwater numeric SQS/CSL set under this chapter is **NOT CONFIRMED**, not established. |
| **Oregon DEQ sediment screening level values** | **Two different sets.** For its **direct-contact** screening values Oregon compiles from external sources, including NOAA's discontinued SQuiRTs, rather than deriving from a Pacific-Northwest-specific database or an EqP model. **But for bioaccumulative chemicals Oregon does derive in-house**: the held *Guidance for Assessing Bioaccumulative Chemicals of Concern in Sediment* (2007) sets out named equations, exposure and toxicity parameter tables, and a chemical-specific screening-level-value table, back-calculating protective sediment concentrations from tissue residue thresholds. | NOT RETRIEVED with sufficient specificity - function as risk-based screening levels in DEQ's site-specific program, but derivation traces to whichever secondary source DEQ's table cites per chemical. | NOT RETRIEVED (see above). | NOT RETRIEVED. | Risk-based screening levels, not promulgated standards. (Oregon/Washington/Idaho also jointly use the *Sediment Evaluation Framework for the Pacific Northwest* (RSET 2016) for dredged-material suitability - a disposal-suitability framework, not a general sediment quality standard; not to be conflated with WAC 173-204.) |
| **Atlantic RBCA sediment EQS (Tier I) and PSS (Tier II)** - Atlantic PIRI, *Environmental Quality Standards: Rationale and Guidance Document*, July 2021, updated June 2023 (NS, NB, PE, NL). | **Adopted, not derived.** Section 4.2 states that "de novo derivation or development of specific provincial standards was not warranted" and that "all EQS and PSS values are adopted values that have been derived by CCME and other regulatory authorities." The stated adoption hierarchy for sediment is: Atlantic PIRI (2012/2021) ecological screening levels for BTEX and petroleum hydrocarbons -> **CCME** Canadian Sediment Quality Guidelines -> Federal Environmental Quality Guidelines -> **BCMOECCS** -> Ontario MOE (2008) -> U.S. jurisdictions. The Tier II table's per-row Reference column bears this out: mostly CCME PEL/ISQG, then BC MOE, US EPA (2008), Simpson et al. (2013), NOAA (Buchman 2008) and Washington Ecology (2013). | **Benthic invertebrates and organisms in direct sediment contact only** - a single value per substance, with no receptor-specific tiers. **There are no human-health sediment EQS in the framework at all**: the rationale states there are "no human health-based guidelines that are specifically derived for the assessment of human sediment exposure", and instead applies Health Canada (2017) guidance to screen sediment against **residential soil** values for direct contact, with explicit caveats that this may be over- or under-protective. | **CCME's Probable Effect Level (PEL), not the ISQG.** Checked against the held CCME 2002 summary tables for three substances across both waterbody classes, and **all six values match the PEL exactly while none matches the ISQG**: arsenic, CCME freshwater ISQG 5.9 / PEL **17.0** against an Atlantic RBCA freshwater value of **17.0**, and marine 7.24 / **41.6** against **41.6**; cadmium 0.6 / **3.5** against **3.5**, and 0.7 / **4.2** against **4.2**; zinc 123 / **315** against **315**, and 124 / **271** against **271**. That is a three-substance sample, not all ~131 parameters. **The consequence is a protection level, not a technicality:** the PEL is the concentration above which adverse effects are *frequently* expected - CCME reports the incidence of adverse effects above the PEL at **65 %**, against 22 % below the TEL - so these are probable-effect values, not threshold values. **The rationale document reproduces no equation of its own**; equilibrium partitioning and co-occurrence are named conceptually and deferred to USEPA (2008) and CCME/MOECC documentation respectively. | **Freshwater and marine values are given separately**, in mg/kg dry weight, across roughly 131 parameters. **No AVS-SEM normalization is mentioned anywhere**, and grain-size normalization in the document applies to soil, not sediment. The PFAS rows are present but **blank**. | Regional multi-province framework (NS, NB, PE, NL). **Two cautions for this paper.** (1) **Tier I EQS and Tier II PSS carry the same numeric values** wherever spot-checked - arsenic 17 / 41.6, cadmium 3.5 / 4.2, zinc 315 / 271, total PCBs 0.277 / 0.189 (freshwater / marine) - the Tier II table adding Comments and Reference columns rather than pathway-differentiated numbers. **It is not a worked example of multi-pathway sediment standards.** (2) Because its Tier II references include **BCMOECCS and CSR Schedule 3.4**, adopting values from Atlantic RBCA into B.C. would in part be **adopting B.C.'s own values back from Atlantic Canada**. Any use of this framework as precedent must state which of the two it means. (3) The values are **CCME Probable Effect Levels** (see the effect-level column), and that places them in a precise relationship to British Columbia's own standards. **Protocol 28 derives the in-force Schedule 3.4 values as a CCME probable effect level multiplied by a probability factor** - 0.62 for sensitive sediment, 1.2 for typical (Chapter 7.2; see section K.1.3). **Atlantic RBCA applies no factor.** An Atlantic RBCA value therefore sits *between* B.C.'s two use classes for the same substance, on the same CCME base: adopting it would import an unfactored PEL alongside B.C. values that are factored. B.C. can adopt from CCME directly and apply its own factor, which is a decision this framework would otherwise make for it. *(Edition note: Protocol 28 cites CCME's 1999 guidelines; the values above were checked against CCME's 2002 summary tables. Same series, edition not asserted identical.)* |
| **Ontario MECP sediment quality Guidelines (LEL/SEL)** - label correction: **these are Guidelines, not Standards.** | Three-tier: No-Effect Level (NEL), Lowest Effect Level (LEL), Severe Effect Level (SEL). "The NEL are based on the related Provincial Water Quality Objectives (PWQO) and the LEL and SEL are based on co-occurrence of chemical and biological information" - NEL is borrowed from a water-column PWQO, not independently sediment-derived; LEL/SEL are a co-occurrence / screening-level-concentration method, related to but distinct from both NOAA ERL/ERM and CCME TEL/PEL. **The percentile cut points are documented in a held source**: the Ontario Ministry of the Environment document carrying the unchanged 1993 Persaud, Jaagumagi and Hayton process gives **LEL as the 5th percentile and SEL as the 95th percentile of the Screening Level Concentration distribution**. Retrieved 2026-09-02 - the same defect as the MacDonald (2003) entry at section K.1.3. | Benthic biological resources generally. | LEL: "clean to marginally polluted," below which "no significant effects on benthic biological resources are anticipated." SEL: "expected to be detrimental to the majority of sediment-dwelling organisms." NOT RETRIEVED: the exact percentile cut points Persaud et al. (1993) used within the co-occurrence dataset. | NOT RETRIEVED. | **Guideline, not a Standard** - assessment/screening tool used by MECP, not a legally binding numeric limit. |
| **ECCC Federal Environmental Quality Guidelines (FEQGs)** | NOT RETRIEVED whether ECCC has published FEQGs specifically for sediment at all (examples retrieved - iron, quinoline - are general substance guidelines, not confirmed sediment-medium). Do not treat as a distinct sediment-derivation method from CCME without further retrieval. | NOT RETRIEVED, contingent on the above. | NOT RETRIEVED. | NOT RETRIEVED. | "The use of FEQGs is voluntary unless prescribed in permits or other regulatory tools" - advisory, per ECCC's own description. |

**Label correction: there is no "ECHA Sediment EQS."** ECHA (European Chemicals Agency) manages REACH/CLP substance registration, evaluation, authorisation and classification; it does not set Environmental Quality Standards. EU surface-water and sediment EQS under the Water Framework Directive (Directive 2008/105/EC as amended) are set by the European Commission, informed by technical work including the Commission's Common Implementation Strategy and JRC. Any reference to "ECHA Sediment EQS" in the options paper should be corrected to identify the EU Water Framework Directive EQS Directive (European Commission) as the actual source, or removed if the underlying figure cannot be re-traced to that correct source. NOT RETRIEVED: the specific EU sediment EQS values/derivation possibly being referred to - that would need a dedicated EU-methods inventory, out of scope for A2/A3.

---

## K.3 - Australia and New Zealand

*Source: research note A3, on Australian and New Zealand methods and adjustment, Part 1. Status per that note: inventory only, no recommendation. Direct retrieval of the primary waterquality.gov.au pages timed out repeatedly during that research session; quotes below are drawn from search-result extracts of those pages plus the CSIRO 2013 revision report and the NZ Auckland Council/MfE technical publication. Points that could not be corroborated at the sentence level are marked NOT RETRIEVED.*

**Governing document.** The *Australian and New Zealand Guidelines for Fresh and Marine Water Quality* (ANZG 2018) superseded ANZECC & ARMCANZ (2000) but, per the search extracts, **carried the 2000 sediment toxicant values forward unchanged** pending a full revision.
- Default values: https://www.waterquality.gov.au/anz-guidelines/guideline-values/default/sediment-quality-toxicants
- Derivation method: https://www.waterquality.gov.au/anz-guidelines/guideline-values/derive/field-effects/method-toxicants-sediments

**What DGV and GV-high are, and how derived:**

> "ANZECC & ARMCANZ (2000) derived guideline values using a ranking of both field ecological and laboratory ecotoxicity-effects data from North America (Long et al. 1995), with the guideline value representing the 10th percentile value of the data distribution and using the median value as an additional upper guideline value (GV-high)."

This is the same lineage as NOAA ERL/ERM and MacDonald et al. (2000) TEL/PEL: $DGV = $ 10th percentile of the pooled effects-concentration dataset; $GV\text{-}high = $ 50th percentile (median) of that same dataset.

> "The 'upper' guideline values (GV-high) provide an indication of concentrations at which you might already expect to observe toxicity-related adverse effects, and the GV-high value should only be used as an indicator of potential high-level toxicity problems, not as a guideline value to ensure protection of ecosystems."

**Species/effects data.** A mixed field-and-laboratory compilation, not a single-species or standardized toxicity-test battery:

> "ANZECC & ARMCANZ (2000) introduced toxicant guideline values for sediments but only a limited number of sediment toxicity tests were available at that time, making it not possible to derive reliable guideline values using species sensitivity distributions of chronic toxicity data, as completed for toxicants in water."

Sediment DGVs are percentile-of-effects-concentrations values, not species-sensitivity-distribution outputs, and were never built to a stated statistical protection level (e.g., "protects 95% of species"). NOT RETRIEVED: the exact count and taxonomic composition of the underlying Long et al. (1995)/MacDonald et al. (2000) effects dataset as re-presented in ANZECC 2000 Vol. 2 - needs direct retrieval of ANZECC 1999-2000 Volume 2 Chapter 8.

**Protection level represented.** DGV/GV-high are **not** expressed as a percentage-of-species-protected figure the way ANZG water-quality trigger values are (PC99/95/90/80). No stated numeric protection percentage is assigned to either.

**Reliability classification.** ANZG's reliability categories ("very high, high, moderate, low, very low or unknown," based on data volume/type and SSD model fit) were found in the search extract in the context of water toxicant DGVs. It is **NOT RETRIEVED** whether the identical scheme applies to **sediment** DGVs - and since sediment DGVs explicitly do not use an SSD model, applying the same scheme as written would be inconsistent. This contradiction is flagged, not resolved. NOT RETRIEVED: the sediment-specific reliability rating actually assigned to each of the ~15 ANZECC/ANZG sediment toxicants.

**ANZG's own instruction on adjustment (tiered, site-specific refinement).** DGV/GV-high are framed as a first-tier screen:

> "If a guideline value is exceeded, the next level of screening considers the fraction of the contaminant that is likely to be bioavailable or can be transformed and mobilised in a bioavailable form."
> "Bioavailability and toxicity of contaminants is influenced by sediment grain size. The binding of contaminants by fine sediment fraction (<63 um) should be considered..."
> "For organic contaminants specifically, hydrophobic organic contaminants (HOCs) are normalized to 1.0% organic carbon (OC)..."

The CSIRO 2013 revision report recommends moving beyond single-value screening: "recommend the use of multiple lines of evidence (from chemistry, toxicity, bioaccumulation and ecology) in a weight of evidence assessment process where guideline values are exceeded." Whether ANZG 2018 formally adopted this methodology change is **NOT RETRIEVED** - direct retrieval of the ANZG 2018 sediment guideline text is needed to confirm whether today's values are the unrevised 2000 ERL/ERM-based figures or a revised set.
(CSIRO 2013: https://people.csiro.au/-/media/People-Finder/S/S/Stuart-Simpson/Revision-of-SQGs-Final-Report-2013--Final.pdf)

**New Zealand.** No separate NZ statutory sediment numeric standard was found. NZ guidance located: the joint ANZECC/NIWA sedimentation-rate document (physical sedimentation/burial, a different endpoint from chemical toxicant concentration - https://environment.govt.nz/assets/publications/niwa-anzecc-estuary-sedimentation-final.pdf), and a regional (non-national) application of the ANZECC/ARMCANZ chemical values in Auckland Council TR2009/050:

> "The Interim Sediment Quality Guidelines-Low (ISQG-L) and -High (ISQG-H) guidelines are based on ERL and ERM values from ANZECC/ARMCANZ (2000). The ISQG-low values assume 1% organic carbon in the sediment."

NOT RETRIEVED: any national NZ Ministry for the Environment numeric sediment standard independent of the joint ANZECC/ANZG values.

---

## K.4 - Comparability and the adoption options

*Source: research note A3 Part 2, and A2's comparability note. Options only - nothing here is a recommendation.*

**These derivation bases are not interchangeable, and adopting a value across bases without adjustment is a category error** (A2 comparability note):

- CCME TEL/PEL and NOAA ERL/ERM are both co-occurrence/effects-database percentile-type methods and are the closest to each other in structure, but are not identical procedures and are not numerically interchangeable without checking each substance's underlying database.
- Washington's AET-based SQS/CSL is a fundamentally different logic - a per-endpoint, always-observed-effect threshold from a regional paired dataset, not a percentile of a compiled multi-region database. Not equivalent to a TEL/PEL or ERL/ERM percentile without separately validating that bioassay endpoints and effect definitions align.
- US EPA's ESBs sit on a wholly different, mechanistic (equilibrium-partitioning) basis, normalised to organic carbon or AVS-SEM rather than dry weight/co-occurrence - not comparable to CCME/NOAA/Washington values without converting through the partitioning relationship.
- Ontario's LEL/SEL sit in the same general co-occurrence family as CCME/NOAA but with a distinct procedure (and, for NEL, an entirely different water-column-PWQO origin); they are also explicitly Guidelines, not enforceable Standards.
- Oregon's screening level values cannot yet be placed on this comparability map - their basis is whatever secondary source (e.g., NOAA SQuiRT) DEQ's table cites per chemical, not a proprietary Oregon derivation.
- ECCC/FEQG status as a distinct sediment-derivation method is unconfirmed - treat as not-yet-established, not as a parallel method to CCME.

**The ANZG basis compounds the problem for Parts 2-3.** BC's Schedule 3.4 Part 1 was built by taking CCME PELs and multiplying by a protection-level factor (0.62 / 1.2) - verified to reproduce the in-force values exactly for total PCBs, benzo[a]pyrene, mercury, and cadmium. Task 7.2 calls for the same kind of adoption from US/Australia/NZ sources for Parts 2-3. This will not be a mechanical repeat of the CCME exercise: ANZG's DGV/GV-high pair is an empirical percentile pair with **no declared statistical protection level**, whereas CCME's PEL at least shares a lineage with a stated effects-range logic that BC's factor could operate on.

**Five options for bringing an external value into Schedule 3.4** (not a recommendation; decision inputs listed at the end):

- **(a) Direct adoption, unadjusted.** Needs the source value's protection goal, statistical basis, and underlying species/effect set to already match BC's stated protection goal for the corresponding category. For ANZG DGV/GV-high specifically, this requires first establishing what numeric protection level DGV/GV-high correspond to - not stated by ANZG itself - so "already matches" cannot currently be verified, only asserted.
- **(b) Adoption with a protection-level adjustment factor (the CCME 0.62/1.2 precedent).** Needs (i) a known, population-level statistical meaning for the source value (e.g., a percentile of an SSD or of an ERL/ERM-type distribution) so a probability/percentile transform to BC's target is mathematically defined, and (ii) the underlying distribution or enough summary statistics to compute it, not just the published number. For ANZG, because no equivalent numeric protection target is stated, a defensible factor cannot yet be built by analogy to 0.62/1.2 - the prerequisite data would need to come from the underlying ANZECC 2000 dataset or the Long et al. (1995)/MacDonald et al. (2000) sources.
- **(c) Re-derivation from the source jurisdiction's underlying effects data using BC's own protection goal.** Most defensible, most expensive. Needs the raw effects-concentration dataset underlying the source value, plus BC's own decision on target percentile/probability, then a fresh calculation. For ANZG this means obtaining the Long et al./MacDonald et al. compiled dataset ANZECC 2000 drew on.
- **(d) Adoption as a Director's Interim Standard under EMA s.63.1.** Bridge mechanism only; needs confirmation the route is available for a substance not currently in Schedule 3.4, plus internal ENV sign-off. One-year life, renewable at Director's discretion; stated to be the only currently in-force legal route for regulating a sediment substance absent a Schedule 3.4 entry. Buys time for (b) or (c) without pre-committing to a permanent value.
- **(e) Adoption as a screening level or trigger, not a standard.** Needs a decision that the value's role is to flag sites for further site-specific investigation - matching how ANZG itself frames GV-high - rather than set an enforceable cleanup number. Lowest evidentiary bar, but does not by itself fulfill Task 7.2's aim of a numeric standard for Parts 2-3; would need pairing with (d) or a future (b)/(c).

**Decision information a TWG would need per substance, to choose among (a)-(e):**
1. The source value's exact statistical construct (percentile of what distribution; SSD vs. ERL/ERM/PEL vs. other) and whether it is documented well enough to support a probability-style transform (rules in/out b and c).
2. Access to the underlying raw effects dataset, not just the published number (required for c; helpful for b).
3. BC's own stated protection goal for the relevant category, stated in the same statistical language as the source construct (required to compute any factor at all).
4. Species/taxa overlap and relevance between the source dataset and BC receiving-environment species.
5. Time pressure and whether an interim bridge (d) is needed while (b) or (c) is completed.
6. Whether the substance's screening/management need can be met by a trigger (e) alone, deferring the cost of (b)/(c) indefinitely.

---

## K.5 - What each jurisdiction's values do NOT cover

Built strictly from what A2 and A3 already state. Most values below protect benthic organisms from direct-contact toxicity only; deviations and gaps are noted per row. Any cell the sources do not settle is marked "not stated in the retrieved source."

| Jurisdiction / value | Receptors addressed | Exposure route addressed | Receptors / routes NOT covered |
|---|---|---|---|
| **CCME ISQG/TEL/PEL** | Benthic and other sediment-associated aquatic life generally, database-wide. | Direct contact with sediment-associated contaminants (co-occurrence/field exposure, implicit in the effects database). | No stated human-health pathway; not stated in the retrieved source whether bioaccumulation/food-web transfer to higher trophic levels is addressed (A1 elsewhere confirms the CCME protocol states this is *not* addressed - "do not specifically address the potential for adverse effects on higher trophic levels... as a result of... bioaccumulation" - carried here for consistency with K.1). Not birds or wildlife consumers of aquatic life. |
| **US EPA ESBs** | Benthic organisms specifically. | Porewater toxicity (bioavailable fraction via equilibrium partitioning). | Not stated in the retrieved source whether higher-trophic-level, food-web, or human-health routes are addressed; framed throughout as a benthic-community porewater-toxicity tool only. |
| **NOAA ERL/ERM** | Marine/estuarine benthic and aquatic life generally. | Direct contact / co-occurrence with effects-database concentrations. | Not stated in the retrieved source whether bioaccumulation, food-web, or human-health routes are addressed; 1995 version excludes freshwater/marginal-quality data entirely (removed relative to the 1990 version). |
| **Washington SQS/CSL (WAC 173-204)** | Biological resources generally, and explicitly humans for SQS: "no adverse effects, including acute or chronic adverse effects on biological resources **and no significant health risk to humans**." | Not stated in the retrieved source which specific human exposure route(s) (e.g., seafood consumption, direct contact) the "no significant health risk to humans" finding is based on. | Freshwater coverage: WAC 173-204 as promulgated governs marine and marine-influenced (estuarine) sediments only; a separately promulgated **freshwater** numeric SQS/CSL set is NOT CONFIRMED in the retrieved source. |
| **Oregon DEQ screening level values** | Not stated in the retrieved source with sufficient specificity - contingent on whichever secondary source (e.g., NOAA SQuiRT) DEQ cites per chemical. | Not stated in the retrieved source. | Not stated in the retrieved source; flagged in A2 as requiring chemical-by-chemical tracing to the cited secondary source before any receptor/route claim can be made. |
| **Ontario LEL/SEL (Guidelines)** | Benthic biological resources generally. | Direct contact / co-occurrence with chemical concentration (LEL/SEL); NEL is a water-column PWQO borrowed value, not a sediment-contact pathway. | Not stated in the retrieved source whether bioaccumulation, food-web, or human-health routes are addressed. Not a Standard - Guideline status only. |
| **ECCC FEQGs** | Not stated in the retrieved source - not confirmed that a sediment-medium FEQG exists at all. | Not stated in the retrieved source. | Not stated in the retrieved source. |
| **ANZG 2018 DGV / GV-high (ANZ)** | Benthic and aquatic ecological receptors generally (field-and-laboratory effects compilation); no stated numeric protection percentage (e.g., no "protects X% of species" claim, unlike ANZG's own water-quality SSD trigger values). | Direct contact / co-occurrence with sediment concentration. | No stated human-health pathway. Not stated in the retrieved source whether bioaccumulation/food-web transfer is addressed. GV-high is explicitly *not* an ecosystem-protection value - ANZG states it is "an indicator of potential high-level toxicity problems... not a guideline value to ensure protection of ecosystems." |
| **NZ regional application (Auckland Council TR2009/050, ISQG-L/H)** | Same receptor basis as the ANZECC/ARMCANZ (2000) ERL/ERM-derived values it applies directly. | Direct contact, assuming 1% organic carbon in sediment (stated normalization assumption for ISQG-low). | No independent NZ national standard exists (NOT RETRIEVED in source); not a distinct derivation from the ANZ values above, so carries the same gaps. |

---

## Closing note

This compendium is reference material for the Task 7.2 generic adoption procedure and for the Phase 3 adoption decisions that follow it. It resolves no adoption question and recommends no value. What it establishes is the precondition for either: each jurisdiction's value rests on a different statistical construct, a different underlying dataset, and a different, often only partially stated, receptor and exposure scope - and BC's own Schedule 3.4 Part 1 precedent (K.1) is itself not fully verifiable at one link in its chain. Adopting a value across these incompatible derivation bases without an adjustment step tied to what the source value actually represents would be a category error, not a shortcut.
