## Full approved project plan transcription

# SOURCE: Draft Project Plan - Phase 2 Sediment Standards (SABCS), user-confirmed document title.

Draft Project Plan
Sediment Standards Phase 2 (2026-2027)
Site Remediation Program (SRP)
1. Project Overview
1.1 Background
British Columbia's (BC's) Contaminated Sites Regulation (CSR) Schedule 3.4 Generic Numerical Sediment Standards are over two decades behind the science. Currently, they are pathway-limited and established primarily to protect bottom-dwelling invertebrates from direct contact. While invertebrates are an important food source in the aquatic ecosystem, these standards do not account for substances that bioaccumulate up the food web into higher organisms and people, nor for human health through direct contact or diet. Furthermore, the standards lack standard organic carbon normalization used by other jurisdictions that decrease the unnecessary conservatism associated with bioavailability and site variability. These significant gaps mean that some of our highest-risk and highest profile sites sit exactly where the standards are weakest, forcing costly, inconsistent site-specific assessments and some of making our priority sites harder to manage and protect.
To address this, the Site Remediation Program (SRP) within the BC Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV) partnered with the Science Advisory Board for Contaminated Sites (SABCS) in 2025 to collaboratively develop a scientific framework for modernizing the CSR standards. Phase 1 of the Sediment Standards Project established an inclusive, collaborative process that successfully developed the foundational framework to modernize the sediment standards, which guides our current research.
This modernization effort is led by the Science and Standards Technical Advisory Committee (SSTAC), established by the SABCS in partnership with ENV. The project relies heavily on a volunteer-based Technical Working Group of over 50 members, drawing world-class expertise from industry, consulting, academia, government, and Indigenous organizations. SABCS solely funded Phase 1, and Phase 2 was scoped to run within SABCS's confirmed baseline budget; however, funding support is needed to support the increased scope this plan proposes. Phase 1 demonstrated the remarkable efficiency of this collaborative volunteer-driven model in supporting ENV's standards development. By acting as a massive force multiplier, the partnership leveraged a minimal seed investment into an exponential expansion of our research capabilities, delivering robust, world-class scientific research and tools that would otherwise be cost-prohibitive.
Building directly on this foundation, Phase 2 develops a modern, multi-part Schedule 3.4 consisting of matrix numerical standards for priority substances that pose the greatest threat (evaluated by considering biomagnification, toxicity, persistence, and local occurrence), and generic numerical standards adopted from other jurisdictions for all other substances. This will result is a scalable, defensible foundation that protects ecological and human health across all routes, including Indigenous food-based exposure pathways.
The final deliverable for Phase 2 is a Matrix Options Paper, a scientifically defensible recommendation package presented to ENV for consideration. Because ENV is responsible for both regulating the implementation of the contaminated sites legal framework and developing and maintaining these standards. ENV will subsequently follow its internal policy, consultation, and approval processes before recommending any regulatory changes to the CSR.
Moving forward, it is anticipated that Phase 3 will utilize this framework to derive and calculate the new numerical standards for a subset of prioritized substances.
1.2 Project Purpose
The purpose of Phase 2 of the Sediment Standards Project is to develop a modern scientific framework for updating BC's CSR Schedule 3.4 Generic Numerical Sediment Standards, integrating best-available science to protect aquatic ecosystems and the communities that depend on them.
1.3 Vision Statement
Establish a fully integrated sediment standards framework that protects ecological and human health across all exposure routes. Modernize Schedule 3.4 to include three parts:
- Part 1: Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards to protect human and ecological health, including four receptor-pathways:
- Protect Human Health - Direct Exposure to Sediment Contaminants;
- Protect Human Health - Exposure to Sediment Contaminants in Food;
- Protect Ecological Health - Direct Exposure to Sediment Contaminants; and
- Protect Ecological Health - Exposure to Sediment Contaminants in Food.
- Part 2: Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to protect human health; and
- Part 3: Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to protect ecological health.
The Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards represent a refined approach for priority substances that pose a threat to human and ecological health, and thus require a high duty of care, determined by considering key factors such as biomagnification, toxicity, persistence, and local occurrence. The Generic Numerical Sediment Standards are values adopted from other jurisdictions, since it is not feasible to develop Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards for all substances due to limitations in available information. This modern multi-part approach with matrix and generic standards creates a defensible, scalable, and adaptable foundation for the contaminated sites regulatory framework.
1.4 Project Objectives
Develop a modern sediment standard framework to protect aquatic ecosystems and the human communities that depend on them, including:
CSR Schedule 3.4 Part 1 Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards to protect human and ecological health from direct exposure and food-based exposure to contaminants. These require a comprehensive set of equations for each receptor-pathway group that are needed to derive these standards;
CSR Schedule 3.4 Part 2 Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to protect human health. These require a procedure for adopting values from other jurisdictions (e.g., United States, Australia, New Zealand) which have different protection levels, species and other factors from Canadian jurisdictions requiring careful consideration, which can leverage work being done on Director's Interim Standards; and
CSR Schedule 3.4 Part 3 Generic Numerical Sediment Standards to protect ecological health. These require the same procedure as Part 2 above.
Given that Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards require specific information (e.g., biomagnification, toxicity, persistence, and local occurrence) and considerable effort, we are developing a substance prioritization framework to identify priority substances requiring standards based on their potential to cause unacceptable risk in BC's aquatic receiving environments;
BC Aquatic Database to be developed by the Ministry of Environment and Parks (ENV) based on documents submitted to the Site Remediation Program and potentially shared data sets. This is a large undertaking requiring significant time and resources in which the partnership with the SABCS will play a key role. This database is critical for multiple internal uses, including:
Establish concentration ranges for substances in sediments across BC, which could be used to develop provincial and regional background concentrations. Understanding these concentration ranges are needed to ensure standards are not below natural background concentrations;
Identify occurrence of substances in BC sediments to understand prevalence of non-scheduled substances, and substances with concentrations above current CSR generic numerical standards and screening levels from other jurisdictions; and
Determine which substances in BC sediments require site-specific risk-based assessment and whether and how bioavailability adjustment played a role in meeting CSR risk-based standards).
Compile a comprehensive set of Policy-ready input parameters required to calculate the Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards in Phase 3 of the Sediment Standards Project. These input parameters will include Toxicity Reference Values for protection of human and ecological health, which is a considerable undertaking. Other examples of types of input parameters include but are not limited to sediment grain size thresholds, receptor characteristics, exposure assumptions, water lot use classifications. Protocol 28 provides input parameters for other media with CSR standards.
The final deliverable for the Sediment Standards Project in Phase 2 is the Matrix Options Paper, which describes how the above objectives were completed including research findings, progress made, options considered, and detailed rationale supporting recommendations. The Matrix Options Paper will provide an engagement summary documenting the ongoing collaborative partnership between ENV and the SABCS, including the invaluable contributions by members of the SSTAC and the Technical Working Group (TWG).
2. Project Scope
2.1 In Scope
July 2026 (ongoing): Initiate BC Aquatic Database development early since this is a large, complex task and requires fully utilizing the TWG support. Data compilation includes:
1) Substance occurrence data in BC to support prioritization;
2) Sediment concentration data to identify natural background conditions and support development of provincial and regional background concentrations. This is important for adjusting preliminary toxicity-based standards, so the approved standards are not below natural background concentrations; and
3) Sediment concentration data to identify the prevalence of concentrations above the current CSR Schedule 3.4 Generic Numerical Sediment Standards. This will also help identify where CSR risk-based standards are used on a site-specific basis and the importance of bioavailability adjustments to account for environmental conditions).
July - September 2026: Identify options for equations to derive Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards for each of the four receptor-pathway combinations.
July 2026 (ongoing): Initiate data compilation of policy-ready input parameters required for calculating preliminary toxicity-based standards (in Phase 3). It is a large undertaking to compile this comprehensive set of information, so we need to start early and optimally leverage TWG support to assist with compilation and review of values, information and references.
October 2026 - March 2027: Initiate phased research on key topics to support the options analysis process, including bioavailability (months 4-6), bioaccumulation (months 5-7), substance classification (months 6-8), and matrix framework design (months 7-9).
January - March 2027: Develop a substance prioritization framework for identifying which substances should be considered for Matrix Numerical Sediment Standards.
January - March 2027: Develop a procedure for adopting values for Generic Numerical Sediment Standards.
April - June 2027: Draft the Matrix Options Paper to summarize the collaborative work completed in 2026 associated with this project.
July 2026 (ongoing): Regular briefings with updates, progress and next steps for ENV, the SABCS and the SSTAC.
July 2026 (ongoing): Coordination of the TWG with a sub-group "core" that supports with research, drafting, and editing tasks, while the full TWG supports by sharing information and reviewing and providing feedback on draft materials).
2.2 Out of Scope
Final numeric standards
Regulatory amendments
Supporting Technical Guidance or Protocol
Complete sediment database

### Resource Reconciliation

**Table 12 (4x4).**

| Capacity Pool | Est. Hours | Available Hours | Headroom |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Internal (ENV, 1 FTE) | ~1500 | ~1650 | ~10% slack |
| TWG + SABCS (volunteer) | ~1835 | ~3200-3600 | Comfortable (~50%) |
| Total Phase 2 | ~3200 | - | - |
The plan fits within a single internal ENV FTE plus volunteer TWG support, but the internal slack is thin. The constraint is the month 7-9 convergence, where the research outputs, matrix design, integration workshop, and start of final drafting all land together. This is why task delivery is staggered in "smart waves" (Risk section) and why the two largest data tasks (3.4, 3.6, 3.8) are explicitly TWG-led with internal ENV review only. All hour figures are planning estimates and will be adaptively managed.

6. Project Phase 2 Risks & Mitigation
Internal and TWG capacity constraints
Mitigation: staggered "smart waves"
Data gaps
Mitigation: prioritize high-value datasets first
Scope creep
Mitigation: reinforce "no standards derivation" boundary
Mitigation: extended project and workshop due to approvals and FTE in April and May have cost implications requiring consideration
Perception of new regulatory burden
Mitigation: emphasize existing authority and consistency objectives in communications

8. Success Criteria
Success for Phase 2 of the Sediment Standards Project will be evaluated against the delivery of its foundational scientific frameworks and the effectiveness of its collaborative governance model. The key criteria for success include:
- Delivery of the Matrix Options Paper: Finalization and submission of the Matrix Options Paper to ENV executive leadership. This deliverable must provide scientifically defensible recommendations, articulate the multi-part Schedule 3.4 expansion concept, and provide a clear, policy-ready outline for future regulatory integration.
- Establishment of the BC Aquatic Database: Successful development of a functional, QA/QC-screened sediment database. Success requires compiling sufficient site data across varying BC regions to identify natural background conditions, explicitly aligned with the statistical and spatial approaches of Protocols 4 and 9 which establish background concentrations for soil and groundwater in BC.
- Successful Workstream Integration: Facilitation of a comprehensive integration workshop (scheduled for month 9) that successfully aligns outputs across all parallel research streams, including bioavailability, bioaccumulation, data, and matrix framework design, resolving core technical gaps before final drafting.
- Robust Expert Engagement: Sustained, productive collaboration with the SSTAC and the over 50 member TWG. Success is defined by the completion of all planned TWG review cycles and ensuring that the final framework reflects a defensible consensus among industry, academic, Indigenous, and government experts.
- Schedule and Resource Adherence: Completion of the Phase 2 scope within the 2026-2027 timeline and the defined 1.0 internal ENV FTE capacity limit, successfully demonstrating the cost effectiveness of the volunteer-driven SABCS partnership.
